\[ \text{retrospective legibility} \neq \text{retrospective effective capacity}. \]

At the present stage, the narrower point is sufficient. A claim must carry its temporal position because the option field, consequence field, and available reversibility may be different at another time.

The index also helps avoid a second error: treating a later revised claim as if it were merely a clearer articulation of what was always true in the analysis. Sometimes that will be the case. Sometimes the world changed. Sometimes the evidence changed. Sometimes the frame improved. Sometimes the actor changed the field. Temporal indexing allows these possibilities to be separated rather than compressed into one timeless statement.

Where a concrete analysis requires it, scene time and analysis time should be distinguished explicitly:

\[ t_s=\text{scene time}, \qquad t_a=\text{analysis time}. \]

An analyst at \(t_a\) may evaluate what was structurally available to an actor at \(t_s\). The notation remains optional and does not enlarge the core VECTOR tuple. Its purpose is anti-retrojective:

\[ \boxed{ Legibility_{analyst}(t_a) \not\Rightarrow Legibility_{actor}(t_s). } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ knowledge_{t_a} \not\Rightarrow effective\ capacity_{t_s}. } \]

Later analysis may establish what was true at scene time. It cannot, without an additional evidence bridge, establish what the actor could see, access, know, authorize, or effectively change then. This temporal distinction is especially important for retrospective option claims and later attribution.

Together, \(O,F,Q,\pi,t\) now provide the minimum claim-bearing ground from which the VECTOR architecture itself can be stated.

3.6 Minimal VECTOR Form

With the claim-bearing coordinates declared, VECTOR can now be stated in its minimal form.

The core architecture is:

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR:=\langle DIR,ROT\rangle. } \]

This expression names two function families rather than two same-typed state coordinates:

  • DIR is a directional claim family.
  • ROT is an admissible perspective-testing and discovery operation family.

A concrete typed VECTOR state is therefore:

[
\boxed{
V_t\(O\mid F,Q,\pi\)

\langle DIR_t,RP_t\rangle,
}
]

where:

\[ \boxed{ RP_t:=ROTProfile_t. } \]

The distinction is constitutive. A concrete \(V_t\) records current claim-relevant states: \(DIR_t\) is the current directional claim/status and \(RP_t\) is the current rotation-test profile. The ROT (Rotation) operation itself remains separate:

\[ ROT_\rho(C_0)\rightarrow C_1. \]

Thus:

\[ \boxed{ROT\neq RP.} \]

A ROT (Rotation) profile may record which materially relevant rotations have been applied, their outcomes, whether they were robustness-bearing or exploratory, which losses or revisions they produced, and which claim-critical rotations remain open. It is an audit state, not a second name for the operation.

DIST as a Derived Supporting Relation

VECTOR retains DIST (Distance) as useful shorthand for effective orientation–enactment separation, but not as a core component. Chapter 19 applies the paper's own redundancy test and finds that the practical discrimination carried by DIST can be reconstructed without loss through existing PMS relations—especially (Χ) (distance), (Θ) (temporality), (Ψ) (self-binding), (Ω) (asymmetry), and (Α) (attractor)—together with explicit publicness, option, capacity, and reversibility conditions.

Accordingly:

\[ \boxed{ DIST_t =\text{derived orientation--enactment separation descriptor}, } \]

but:

\[ \boxed{ DIST\notin VECTOR_{core}. } \]

This reduction does not make DIST (Distance) useless. It fixes its claim type. DIST can summarize whether meaningful low-cost correction remains available before further consolidation, but a strong claim must remain traceable to the source relations that produce that separation. The shorthand cannot add warrant merely by receiving a name.

The type boundaries established earlier can now be collected once:

\[ \boxed{DIR\neq\nabla} \]
\[ \boxed{ROT\neq\Phi} \]
\[ \boxed{ROT\neq PROJECT_AS} \]
\[ \boxed{DIST\neq Χ} \]
\[ \boxed{\pi\neq P_{MIP}} \]
\[ \boxed{t\neq\Theta.} \]

These inequalities are type boundaries, not claims of irrelevance. DIR (Direction) may be informed by structures generated through the PMS Base grammar; it is not (\nabla) (directed activation). ROT (Rotation) may involve (\Phi) (recontextualization); it is not that Base operator. DIST (Distance), where used as derived shorthand, is not identical to (Χ) (distance), even though its discrimination must remain reducible to the actual source relations rather than protected as a new primitive. Perspective is not MIP practical power, and temporal indexing is not (Θ) (temporality) itself.

Geometry Firewall

The language of vector, direction, rotation, distance, and resultant is intentionally non-Euclidean unless a domain separately earns stronger mathematics. The canonical boundary is:

\[ \boxed{ \begin{aligned} VECTOR &\neq \text{vector space by default},\ DIR &\neq \text{signed scalar},\ ROT &\neq \text{geometric rotation},\ DIST &\neq \text{metric distance},\ DIR_R &\neq \text{vector sum}. \end{aligned} } \]

ROT (Rotation) need not be invertible, angle-bearing, norm-preserving, or group-composable. DIST (Distance) has no default unit. No magnitude, norm, dot product, average, vector addition, or Euclidean projection is implied. A later resultant orientation is a new bounded claim reconstructed from convergent directional relations, not an algebraic composition of vectors.

The architecture also excludes a common form of expansion drift. As the paper proceeds, VECTOR will use evidence, warrant, calibration basis, residue, option fields, cost topology, effective capacity, uptake, result status, and supporting relations such as DIST (Distance). These are not additional VECTOR components.

\[ \boxed{ \text{claim-support fields and derived relations} \neq \text{VECTOR components}. } \]

This distinction preserves a compact core. Warrant can increase or decrease while DIR (Direction) remains nominally the same. A new option can become visible without becoming a third component. Calibration, uptake, provenance, or DIST (Distance) can condition or summarize a claim without being promoted into VECTOR itself. The architecture remains DIR–ROT (Rotation) while a concrete state uses DIR–RP (ROT Profile) to avoid operation/state type collapse.

The minimal architecture can therefore be read as a discipline of two core questions:

\[ DIR:\quad \text{What bounded direction is being claimed?} \]
\[ ROT:\quad \text{What happens to that orientation under admissible change of view?} \]

A separate derived question remains practically important:

\[ DIST:\quad \text{How much effective separation remains between orientation and enactment?} \]

Everything that follows should make these questions more discriminating without turning any of them into a universal score or allowing the supporting relation to recreate a hidden third primitive.

3.7 Relational and Ordinal before Metric

VECTOR is relational and ordinal before it is metric.

The word before is essential. VECTOR does not prohibit quantification. It refuses to presume quantification where the comparison has not yet earned it.

A directional relation can be meaningful without a scalar magnitude. One reconstruction can preserve a relevant asymmetry better than another for a declared question without the analyst being able to state that it is 37 percent better. A role may have less available reversibility than another without a universal Reversibility Index. A rotation can place a claim under stronger pressure without that strength being reducible to a count of rotations. A DIST (Distance) relation can be comparatively tighter or looser without being a physical distance.

The default commitment is therefore:

\[ \boxed{ \text{relational / ordinal before metric}. } \]

This default blocks several premature mathematical inferences.

A local relation such as

\[ X\prec_VY \]

need not imply a numeric value (v(X)) and (v(Y)). It does not imply that all relevant objects can be embedded in one common scalar field. It does not imply transitivity. It does not imply that the relation between (X) and (Y) can be compared in magnitude with the relation between (A) and (B). It does not imply compensability across different questions.

Likewise, the core architecture

\[ \langle DIR,ROT\rangle \]

does not imply a vector magnitude

[
|V|,
]

an angle between orientations, or arithmetic combination of components.

This restraint is not anti-formal. It is a demand for additional warrant before formal strength is increased.

\[ \boxed{ \text{non-metric default} \neq \text{anti-quantitative doctrine}. } \]

A domain may provide legitimate measurement. Empirical work may establish calibrated thresholds. A constrained technical application may support a metric for one component under a narrow question. If so, that metric is an additional domain claim. It must declare what is measured, how the scale is calibrated, which transformations preserve meaning, what uncertainty remains, and which generalizations are not licensed.

The naming discipline matters because formal notation can create a false sense of precision. A symbol can make a relation easier to inspect while leaving its calibration unchanged. A schema can improve record consistency while leaving the underlying judgment contestable. A numerical output can be precise in syntax and weak in warrant.

VECTOR therefore distinguishes formal explicitness from measurement:

\[ \boxed{ \text{formal representation} \neq \text{metric validation}. } \]

The theory's first obligation is discriminative: can it tell relevant structural relations apart in a bounded and corrigible way? Metric extension, where useful, comes later.

This ordering also protects the human boundary. Once a broad orientation theory acquires scores, there is strong pressure to apply them to persons, groups, institutions, or cultures as if a single scalar summarized their worth or maturity. VECTOR has no such function. Its relational default makes explicit that a comparison is always typed to a reference and question. Even where numerical calibration becomes legitimate for a local variable, nothing converts that variable into intrinsic human value.

The mathematical modesty of VECTOR is therefore not aesthetic. It preserves the difference between direction that can be argued and measurement that must be separately earned.

3.8 Typed, Bounded, Revisable

The minimal VECTOR object is not merely contextual. It is claim-disciplined.

A VECTOR claim must be typed, bounded, and revisable.

Typed

Typing answers what kind of claim is being made and what kind of object it concerns. Is the reference a reconstruction, a trajectory, a coordination relation, an option field, or a praxis scene? Is the claim descriptive, comparative, orientational, or later responsibility-relevant? Which perspective is being represented? Which temporal position is relevant?

Typing prevents semantic borrowing. A claim about a structure cannot silently become a claim about a person. A claim about a hypothetical probe cannot silently become a claim about an actual option. A claim about system-level carriage cannot silently become a personal duty. A claim about practical necessity cannot silently become moral goodness. Many of the most dangerous later drifts in VECTOR can be understood as type jumps.

Bounded

Boundedness answers where the claim applies.

A directional relation is valid under some declared \(O,F,Q,\pi,t\), not in an unspecified universe. A result may hold for one frame and collapse at another granularity. It may be relevant to one role while another role occupies a different temporal or asymmetrical position. It may hold while a constraint (G) remains operative and change when (G) is revised.

Boundedness is therefore not mere cautionary language added after a strong claim. It determines the claim's content.

An expansion of scope is a new burden. If the frame widens, the question changes, the temporal interval expands, or a local relation is generalized ecosystem-wide, the analyst must establish whether the original warrant survives the change. Scope cannot be enlarged by rhetorical momentum.

Revisable

Revisability answers what could make the claim smaller or different.

A serious VECTOR claim should be able to state, at least in principle, which kinds of change could:

  • weaken its DIR,
  • reveal a trade-off,
  • reverse the relation,
  • expose reference discontinuity,
  • require a narrower frame,
  • suspend the result,
  • or produce non-capture.

This does not require exhaustive prediction of every future criticism. It requires the claim not to be structured so that every counterpressure automatically becomes further confirmation.

The coordinate form makes this discipline visible. If

\[ (O,F,Q,\pi,t)_0 \rightarrow (O,F,Q,\pi,t)_1, \]

and the change is material to the claim, VECTOR must ask whether the same claim still exists.

\[ \boxed{ \text{There is no automatic claim continuity across material coordinate change.} } \]

This rule anticipates several later distinctions. A frame change can create a valuable probe without preserving same-claim robustness. A temporal change can alter option status without making the earlier claim inconsistent. A revised proposition can become better without the original proposition having survived. A new perspective can expose a real relation that the first perspective missed without proving that every perspective is equally adequate.

The five coordinates are therefore not optional metadata attached to an otherwise context-free VECTOR value.

They are claim-bearing conditions.

The full operational record appears later in Appendix B. The theoretical point belongs here: what a VECTOR claim means depends on the conditions under which it is made, and those conditions must remain visible enough that later transformation does not rewrite the claim's lineage.

Typedness, boundedness, and revisability together also establish a burden on positive results. A robust claim is not simply one that appears repeatedly. It is one whose type has remained stable enough to be tested, whose boundaries have remained visible enough to know what survived, and whose revisability has remained real enough that failure was possible.

Calibration Boundary

Typing a threshold does not generate the threshold. VECTOR repeatedly depends on judgments such as relevant, materially distinct, sufficient, effective, actionable, viable, claim-critical, and robust. The architecture can require those thresholds to be declared, supported, and revisable; it cannot manufacture all of their substantive calibration from its own vocabulary.

\[ \boxed{ \text{VECTOR identifies and structures the thresholds on which orientation claims depend.} } \]
\[ \boxed{ \text{VECTOR does not generate all such thresholds endogenously.} } \]

A threshold may depend on source material, domain convention, empirical measurement, legal or technical standards, role-specific conditions, explicit comparison criteria, or another externally accountable basis. Formal operator discipline and record completeness do not eliminate this dependency.

Accordingly:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Two VECTOR-conform analyses may remain calibration-divergent} } \]

while agreeing on reference, claim type, rotation discipline, and evidence status. Where the divergence is traceable to a genuine threshold disagreement, the appropriate result may be narrower scope, explicit calibration divergence, bounded orientation, suspension, rival-sensitive disagreement, or non-capture. The mere existence of calibration divergence does not show that one analysis is malformed.

This boundary reconstructs a calibration pressure already explicit in PMS Base, PMS-DISCIPLINE (application discipline), PMS-UNDER LOAD (success-pressure critique), and PMS-STRATA (controlled transformation). It is not warrant for VECTOR; it is a limit on what VECTOR may claim when its own analysis depends on thresholds it did not independently establish.

3.9 VECTOR Is Not a Universal Scale

The final consequence of the VECTOR object is positive rather than merely restrictive.

VECTOR does not need a universal scale in order to support orientation.

\[ \boxed{ \text{No universal scale} \neq \text{no orientation}. } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ \text{No total order} \neq \text{no comparative discrimination}. } \]

This distinction is central to the entire project. If orientation required a single global metric, the theory would either have to produce one or retreat to flat pluralism. VECTOR rejects that forced choice. Bounded relational comparison is enough to make some directional claims meaningful while leaving other relations as trade-offs, incomparable, suspended, or non-capturable.

A local comparison may therefore establish that one reconstruction is better for a declared question without claiming that it is better in every respect. A set of local directional relations may later support a resultant orientation without ranking every object in the field. A constraint may narrow the available response set without establishing that the constraint is legitimate. A perspective may expose a previously hidden consequence without becoming the privileged standpoint for all analysis.

STRATA provides an important methodological precedent here. Its transformation discipline explicitly refuses to turn additional granularity, composition, or projection into a universal scale or higher authority. A finer reconstruction may gain discrimination while losing something else; analytical mobility does not itself rank the resulting structures globally. VECTOR extends the same anti-totalizing discipline into orientation.

The later resultant orientation of Chapter 13 must therefore not be misunderstood as a scalar aggregate. Even where several locally warranted directions converge, the result remains a bounded new claim. It does not automatically assign every relevant praxis configuration a place on a common line.

\[ \boxed{ DIR_R\neq\text{global scalar}. } \]

This is also where the human boundary becomes unavoidable. A theory capable of directional comparison can easily be misread as a theory of ranked human worth. VECTOR rejects the type jump.

\[ \boxed{ \text{structural orientation} \neq \text{human worth hierarchy}. } \]

A person may participate in a more or less corrigible practice. A role may possess more or less effective capacity. An enactment may preserve asymmetry more truthfully than another. An orientation may be more or less robust under declared pressure. None of these claims establishes greater or lesser intrinsic human value.

VECTOR therefore begins neither with a metric nor with a verdict. It begins with a bounded reference, a declared frame, a question, a perspective, and a temporal position—and asks whether any directional relation can survive the transformations that follow.

That question leads directly into the theoretical core. Part II defines DIR (Direction) and ROT (Rotation) as the core orientation architecture, develops DIST (Distance) as a derived orientation–enactment relation, and then asks how direction, perspective, enactment proximity, and reversibility change through time without turning the architecture into a closed dynamical system.

Part II — DIR, ROT, and Derived Enactment Separation

Part I established the conditions under which a VECTOR claim can be meaningfully stated. No VECTOR state is context-free. A reference object, declared frame, question, perspectival position, and temporal position must be visible before orientation can be attributed. It also established a stricter architectural boundary: VECTOR is not a universal scale, and the notation

\[ V_t(O\mid F,Q,\pi)=\langle DIR_t,RP_t\rangle \]

does not turn orientation into a metric space.

Part II now develops the theoretical core and the supporting praxis relation that initially appeared as its third component.

The movement is deliberately sequential without being hierarchical. DIR (Direction) asks whether a relevant difference can carry bounded direction. ROT (Rotation) asks whether that direction depends on an unexamined viewing position, and whether admissible changes of view reveal additional relations. Chapter 6 then reconstructs effective orientation–enactment separation as DIST (Distance), while keeping open the redundancy question later decided in Chapter 19. The Reversibility Gradient distinguishes current separation from the path-dependent correction and reversal budget left by prior praxis. VECTOR Dynamics finally shows how these relations can modify one another through time without forming a closed deterministic system.

The analytical sequence is:

\[ DIR \rightarrow ROT \rightarrow DIST_{derived} \rightarrow REV \rightarrow \text{recursive VECTOR dynamics}. \]

The ordering is not an authority order. DIR (Direction) is introduced before ROT (Rotation) because a directional candidate must exist before its robustness can be tested. ROT may nevertheless weaken or destroy that candidate. DIST (Distance) remains useful because the practical availability of further examination depends partly on how close orientation already is to enactment; its later reduction means only that this relation does not require an independent core component. Reversibility follows because current separation does not by itself tell us what prior praxis has made difficult or impossible to undo.

Together, these relations provide a bounded account of orientation and its praxis conditions without constituting a complete metric dynamics of praxis.

4. DIR — Direction

PMS can make structural difference legible without converting every difference into a ranking. VECTOR begins exactly at this boundary.

A difference may be real and important while remaining non-directional. Two arrangements may distribute asymmetry differently without one dominating the other for the question being asked. Two models may preserve different forms of discrimination. Two trajectories may be mutually non-integrable. Conversely, the refusal of universal ranking does not require the refusal of all directional judgment. Some differences can be better supported, less distortive, more corrigible, or more adequate relative to a declared structural question.

DIR names this bounded possibility.

The basic problem is therefore not whether two objects differ. It is whether a relevant difference can support a directional relation without being inflated into a total order.

\[ \boxed{ \text{difference} \neq \text{direction} \neq \text{total order}. } \]

DIR (Direction) is the first VECTOR component because orientation requires more than classification. A framework that can only say that two configurations are different may be highly descriptive but remain unable to distinguish a meaningful improvement from a merely different arrangement. A framework that automatically converts every difference into rank has the opposite problem: it manufactures order where the relevant structure may only support trade-off, incomparability, or non-capture.

VECTOR therefore treats direction as a claim that must be earned locally.

4.1 Local Directional Relations

Let the relevant VECTOR conditions be collected as

\[ C=(O,F,Q,\pi,t). \]

A minimal directional claim can then be written:

\[ \boxed{ X\prec_{V,C}Y. } \]

The expression states that, under the declared conditions, the relevant comparison carries direction from (X) toward (Y). It does not state that (Y) is universally better than (X), morally superior to (X), or greater than (X) on an underlying scalar quantity. It states only that the comparison burden defined by (C) supports a directional relation.

This is why the notation must be read cautiously:

\[ \boxed{ \prec_V \text{ is directional notation before it is an order-theoretic commitment.} } \]

The distinction is not cosmetic. Symbols such as (\prec) invite familiar mathematical inferences. VECTOR refuses those inferences unless they are separately warranted.

Most importantly, transitivity is not assumed by default. Suppose:

\[ X\prec_{V,C_1}Y \]

and

\[ Y\prec_{V,C_2}Z. \]

Even if the verbal questions appear similar, it does not follow that

\[ X\prec_VZ. \]

The two claims may rely on different frames, temporal cuts, non-compensatory constraints, role positions, or comparison bases. Even where \(C_1=C_2\) appears to hold, transitivity would be an additional structural property of the relevant comparison domain. It is not built into DIR (Direction).

Accordingly:

\[ \boxed{ X\prec_VY\land Y\prec_VZ \not\Rightarrow X\prec_VZ. } \]

A domain may later support a transitive subrelation. If so, that is a stronger domain-specific claim. VECTOR does not forbid it. It merely refuses to obtain it for free from the notation.

The same restraint applies to magnitude. From

\[ X\prec_{V,C}Y \]

nothing follows about how much better (Y) satisfies the relevant comparison. There is no default quantity (v(X)), no implied interval, and no guarantee that the directional difference between (X) and (Y) can be meaningfully compared with a directional difference elsewhere.

This keeps DIR aligned with the relational and ordinal priority established in Part I. A directional relation can be substantively real even when no legitimate metric exists.

The local character of DIR (Direction) is therefore not a weakness that later analysis should remove. It is part of the claim type. Direction begins bounded because the conditions under which a structural difference becomes relevant are themselves bounded.

4.2 Conditions of a Valid DIR Claim

A DIR statement is not licensed merely by the presence of preference, coherence, familiarity, or theoretical elegance. It requires a comparison that can survive explicit reconstruction.

At minimum, a serious DIR claim must make visible:

  • the reference object or objects being compared;
  • the frame within which the comparison is meaningful;
  • the question that defines the relevant directional burden;
  • the perspectival and temporal position of the claim;
  • the structural difference alleged to carry direction;
  • the warrant for treating that difference as directional rather than merely distinct;
  • the claim-critical losses that would reduce or defeat the comparison.

These requirements can be compressed into a simple distinction between the VECTOR component and the support structure around it.

DIR is part of:

\[ \langle DIR,ROT\rangle. \]

Its warrant is not.

One may therefore write schematically:

\[ W(DIR\mid C), \]

where (W) records the support for the directional claim. But:

\[ \boxed{ W \notin \langle DIR,ROT\rangle. } \]

This separation matters because warrant can change while the nominal directional relation remains the same. A claim may begin weakly supported, survive a demanding rotation, and become more credible without acquiring a new VECTOR component. Conversely, a DIR (Direction) may remain verbally unchanged while its warrant deteriorates because a previously hidden dependency or countercase has appeared.

Warrant Source Boundary

Warranted cannot mean merely plausible after enough VECTOR processing. A material DIR (Direction) claim should make visible what carries its warrant. Depending on the claim, that may include supplied evidence, a source-carried structural relation, a domain-specific factual premise, an explicit comparison criterion, reference continuity, rival pressure, a counter-scene result, or an external empirical, legal, technical, historical, causal, or other declared premise.

The inventory is not a universal warrant algorithm. Different domains can require different support. Its purpose is to prevent VECTOR's own formal activity from becoming an epistemic source by default.

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR\text{-internal robustness} \neq external\ premise\ warrant. } \]
\[ \boxed{ \text{formal inspectability} \neq \text{warrant source}. } \]

A warrant record should therefore distinguish source types, material external premises, unresolved dependencies, and—where material—the calibration basis of a threshold. These support fields condition DIR (Direction) without becoming additional VECTOR components.

The practical burden is therefore not satisfied by saying that one configuration “looks better.” The comparison must explain why the relevant difference matters for (Q).

Suppose two reconstructions of the same scene exist. Reconstruction (Y) preserves a real asymmetry that reconstruction (X) collapses. If the declared question concerns adequate representation of that asymmetry, this may support:

\[ X\prec_{V,Q}Y. \]

But if (Y) achieves that preservation only by abandoning reference continuity, importing unavailable information, or introducing an equally claim-critical distortion, the directional relation may fail. Direction is therefore not the addition of positive features. It is a bounded judgment about a comparison under constraints.

This is where the claim ceiling becomes operative. A local finding such as

Y preserves the relevant asymmetry more adequately than X for this question

cannot be restated without further warrant as

Y is the better theory.

The second sentence has silently enlarged the object and question. It is a new claim.

DIR (Direction) accordingly demands more precision as its scope expands. This rule will later become explicit in convergence testing, but it already governs every local comparison: the stronger the directional statement, the larger the burden of preserving the conditions that made it meaningful.

4.3 Reference-, Frame-, and Question-Bounded Comparison

Directional claims are coordinate-relative without being arbitrary.

This phrase marks a difficult middle position. If every directional relation were context-free, VECTOR would quickly become a universal ranking system. If every relation were merely a function of whichever frame the analyst happened to prefer, VECTOR would collapse into perspectival arbitrariness. Bounded comparison avoids both outcomes by requiring the conditions of the claim to remain visible and contestable.

Consider two questions:

\[ Q_1=\text{Which reconstruction preserves the relevant asymmetry more faithfully?} \]

and

\[ Q_2=\text{Which reconstruction minimizes complexity for the current bounded task?} \]

It may be the case that

\[ X\prec_{V,Q_1}Y \]

while

\[ Y\prec_{V,Q_2}X. \]

No contradiction follows. The comparisons ask different questions. The relevant mistake would be to merge them into an unspecified overall judgment.

VECTOR therefore rejects floating superiority claims:

\[ \boxed{ \text{No floating superiority claim.} } \]

Every directional statement implicitly answers “better for what?” The question is not an afterthought. It identifies which differences count, which losses are claim-critical, and which alternative advantages may remain outside the current comparison.

The frame performs a parallel function. A comparison may be valid inside \(F_0\) and fail in \(F_1\). This does not automatically make the first comparison arbitrary. It may instead show that the first claim was correctly bounded. What is prohibited is the silent expansion from one frame to another while preserving the original confidence.

Reference matters for the same reason. If the object being compared changes materially, a previous DIR (Direction) cannot simply follow it. A perspective change may expose a relation between a policy and the affected role, but that does not make the policy and the role interchangeable objects of comparison. The new relation may strengthen understanding of the original object without being the same claim.

The resulting discipline can be expressed as a question repeated throughout VECTOR:

Better for what, under what conditions, relative to what reference?

This is not relativism. A bad answer can still be wrong. A frame can hide relevant structure. A question can be badly typed. A purported reference continuity can fail. Evidence can contradict the claim. The fact that a relation is bounded does not make it immune to correction; it makes the conditions of correction clearer.

4.4 Non-Compensatory Comparison

A recurrent temptation in comparative theory is to convert heterogeneous strengths and losses into one aggregate score. VECTOR refuses that move by default.

Suppose (Y) improves three aspects of a reconstruction while losing one. An additive comparison would invite the question whether three gains compensate for one loss. But this assumes that all four features belong to a common scale and can trade against one another. Sometimes they can. Often they cannot.

A comparison becomes especially unstable when the loss affects a condition that the claim itself requires. VECTOR calls such a loss a critical regression.

A critical regression is not simply a large negative effect. It is a loss that undermines the comparison burden itself or violates a condition that cannot be compensated within the declared question.

Examples include:

  • a finer reconstruction that gains detail by losing reference continuity;
  • a more coherent account that achieves coherence by excluding the strongest rival;
  • a coordination pattern that appears more stable only because the exposure of one affected role has disappeared from the frame;
  • a highly reversible arrangement that preserves optionality by preventing the commitment required for the very function being evaluated.

In each case, unrelated strengths cannot repair the specific failure.

\[ \boxed{ \text{A critical regression cannot be compensated by unrelated strength.} } \]

This principle does not mean that trade-offs are forbidden. It means that trade-offs must remain visible as trade-offs rather than being hidden by an unexplained aggregate.

STRATA supplies an important methodological precedent. Its transformation discipline requires explicit loss, type integrity, reference continuity, and bounded claims; additional detail or structural mobility does not automatically outweigh a claim-critical loss elsewhere. VECTOR borrows that discipline for directional comparison without inheriting STRATA's operation type or authority.

Non-compensation also blocks a subtler form of ranking. An analyst may be tempted to count the number of dimensions on which (Y) appears favorable and infer dominance from the majority. But directional validity is not a vote. Ten minor strengths do not automatically override one condition on which the current claim depends.

The same principle will later constrain convergence. A large number of recurrent local directions cannot compensate for one countercase that destroys the scope of the claimed resultant. For now, the local rule is sufficient: comparison must preserve the differences that matter to the claim rather than dissolve them into arithmetic convenience.

4.5 Local Superiority without Universal Superiority

A local DIR can be strong without becoming universal.

This is not merely a disclaimer attached to comparison. It is the positive structure of VECTOR's directional claim.

At least three levels should be distinguished.

First, local directional superiority:

\[ X\prec_{V,Q}Y. \]

The comparison supports a direction for a declared question under bounded conditions.

Second, wider directional robustness. A similar relation may survive several materially distinct tests or appear across differently constrained local cases. This can justify a broader claim, but only after the additional burden is met.

Third, universal superiority:

\[ Y>X\quad\forall O,F,Q,\pi,t. \]

Nothing in the first two relations yields the third.

The point is easy to state but difficult to preserve in practice. Strong local results create rhetorical pressure to generalize. If one reconstruction repeatedly performs better, the analyst may begin speaking as though its superiority were intrinsic. If one structural tendency recurs across several domains, the tendency may be redescribed as a universal axis. VECTOR treats each such expansion as a new claim rather than a free consequence of success.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Local superiority does not imply universal superiority.} } \]

This rule applies even to later ecosystem-wide convergence. A resultant orientation remains a bounded claim about a field of local directions. It does not become a scalar ordering of all praxis.

The absence of universal superiority therefore does not weaken DIR into mere description. It simply prevents a warranted local relation from being rhetorically upgraded beyond its support.

4.6 Dominance, Trade-Off, Incomparability, and Non-Capture

DIR requires more than a yes/no output. The absence of a simple dominance relation can arise for importantly different reasons. VECTOR therefore distinguishes four basic result types.

Dominance / Directed Result

A directed result exists where the declared comparison supports:

\[ X\prec_{V,C}Y. \]

The term dominance is local and claim-bound. It does not imply total-order dominance or universal priority. It means that the relevant non-compensatory comparison supports direction.

Trade-Off / Multidirectional Result

A trade-off exists where relevant directional relations can be reconstructed but do not support one non-compensatory resultant.

For example:

\[ X\prec_{Q_1}Y \]

and

\[ Y\prec_{Q_2}X. \]

The case is not directionless. On the contrary, its directional structure may be unusually clear. What is unavailable is a legitimate collapse of those relations into one overall rank.

A trade-off therefore contains more information than a generic statement that the objects are incomparable. It says that distinct directional burdens have been identified and remain jointly relevant.

One may write schematically:

\[ TradeOff_V(X,Y\mid C). \]

The notation is descriptive, not a new core operator.

Incomparability

Incomparability occurs where a meaningful common comparison context exists but no warranted dominance relation can be established.

\[ X\parallel_{V,C}Y. \]

The reasons may include non-substitutable functions, incompatible but legitimate objective relations, insufficient priority rules, or mature non-integrability. The point is that comparison itself remains meaningful even though rank does not.

Non-Capture

Non-capture is stronger:

\[ \varnothing_{V,C}. \]

Here VECTOR should not generate the directional comparison as currently framed.

This may occur because reference continuity has broken, the question is wrongly typed, the available warrant is too weak, relevant residue cannot responsibly be compressed, or the comparison would require a category jump that VECTOR explicitly excludes—for example, from structural enactment to human worth.

The distinctions are therefore:

\[ \boxed{ TradeOff \neq Incomparability \neq NonCapture. } \]

A trade-off says: several directions are visible, but no single result is warranted.

Incomparability says: the comparison is meaningful, but no warranted direction is available.

Non-capture says: the directional operation itself should not be asserted in this form.

These outputs are not fallback states to be eliminated by more theoretical ingenuity. They are part of VECTOR's discriminative capacity.

4.7 Incomparability Does Not Mean Equality

The refusal of rank is often misread as a statement of equivalence.

VECTOR rejects that inference.

\[ X\parallel_{V,C}Y \not\Rightarrow X=Y. \]

Nor does it imply equal quality, equal consequence, equal adequacy, or equal practical significance. It means only that the current VECTOR comparison does not warrant a dominance relation.

Two structures can be profoundly different while remaining incomparable for the declared question. Two trajectories can impose different kinds of irreversible loss without a legitimate common scale. Two theories can discriminate different aspects of a phenomenon so well that no bounded reason exists to collapse them into one rank.

The distinction matters because a theory that refuses universal ranking can otherwise drift into flat pluralism. If every non-ranked relation were treated as equality, the original orientation problem would simply reappear in another form. Difference would become legible while direction remained impossible by definition.

VECTOR instead preserves the stronger proposition:

\[ \boxed{ \text{The absence of warranted rank is not evidence of equal structure.} } \]

This is one of the main ways in which the project holds together non-finality and discrimination. A claim may fail to establish a final or universal order while still distinguishing non-equivalent structures sharply.

4.8 Positive Local Precedents

VECTOR does not invent the possibility of local directional claims within PMS. Several existing papers already make bounded structural comparisons. They do not prove VECTOR, but they show that the orientation problem arises from material already present in the ecosystem.

4.8.1 EDEN — Truthful and False Coordination

PMS–EDEN distinguishes real asymmetry from its coordination. Its central contrast is not asymmetry versus symmetry but truthful coordination of real (\Omega) (asymmetry) versus false coordination of that same real asymmetry. A scene may contain unequal capacity, exposure, dependency, protection, or burden without reciprocity having failed. The failure occurs when the operative asymmetry is falsely coordinated in ways that displace responsibility, block integration or self-binding, and erode correction.

That distinction is already directional in a bounded sense. For a question concerning truthful coordination of real asymmetry, a reconstruction that preserves the actual structuring effect can be preferable to one that makes the asymmetry disappear through false framing. The claim need not rank persons, sexes, roles, or whole ways of life. It is local to the coordination relation.

EDEN therefore supplies a useful precedent for the proposition that structural comparison can be directional without becoming a worth hierarchy.

4.8.2 CRITIQUE — Reaction, Correction, and Follow-Up

PMS–CRITIQUE likewise contains directional transition structure. Irritation or reaction is not identical with correction. Correction requires additional conditions: sufficient distance, a frame that can still be revised, and follow-up capable of changing the relevant praxis relation rather than merely repeating the reaction.

The directional relation concerns the structure of correction, not the moral maturity of the person involved. A transition can become more correction-capable without turning the actor into a higher-ranked person.

This local structure later becomes especially important for DIST (Distance) and timing, but at the level of DIR (Direction) it already demonstrates a bounded directional claim: some transitions preserve more actual corrective capacity than others.

4.8.3 DISCIPLINE — Bounded Adequacy

PMS–DISCIPLINE provides an even clearer comparison precedent. It distinguishes coherence from permission, legibility from entry, and broad explanatory reach from bounded adequacy for a specific use. A narrower corrected claim may be more adequate for a declared application than a broader elegant claim precisely because it respects a lower claim ceiling and remains more corrigible.

This yields a useful directional form:

\[ \text{more adequate for a bounded use} \not\Rightarrow \text{more expansive or universally superior}. \]

VECTOR generalizes that comparison discipline without making DISCIPLINE a validator of VECTOR itself.

The three precedents share only what is needed here: local structural direction can already be stated without a universal scale. Their differences remain important and will later be treated as lineage rather than collapsed into proof.

4.9 Negative Cases

The stronger test of DIR is whether it allows mature cases in which no single direction should be produced.

4.9.1 ANTICIPATION — Incompatible Optimizations

PMS–ANTICIPATION repeatedly confronts temporally structured situations in which different relevant objectives cannot all be maximized together. Preserving optionality can conflict with timely commitment. More preparation can reduce one risk while increasing another. Waiting for stronger confirmation can improve epistemic confidence while allowing a meaningful window to close.

Such configurations do not always support one monotonic rule. “More anticipation” is not universally better, just as “more reversibility” will later prove not to be universally better. A structurally honest result may therefore be a trade-off in which several directional relations remain visible without one dominating the whole field.

This is a negative case for any attempt to define DIR as a single axis of improvement.

4.9.2 CONFLICT — Mature Non-Integrability

PMS–CONFLICT provides the harder case. It allows conflicts in which the relevant positions are reflective, structurally intelligible, and not reducible to simple error, yet remain non-integrable. Temporal exposure, infrastructure, non-transferable burdens, and asymmetric risk can prevent a joint consolidation even after considerable correction.

Such a case may contain strong local direction on several dimensions while resisting a single shared ordering. The correct result may be trade-off, incomparability, or a later tragic constraint—not forced convergence.

The consequence for DIR is fundamental:

Direction is a possible property of a bounded comparison, not a compulsory output of structural sophistication.

\[ \boxed{ DIR\text{ permits warranted structural direction without presuming that every relevant relation has one.} } \]

That conclusion creates the need for ROT (Rotation). A local DIR (Direction) may be carefully bounded and well warranted under its initial conditions while still depending on a perspective that has not yet been tested. Direction therefore remains a candidate until it has been exposed to admissible change of view.

5. ROT — Perspectival Freedom

DIR establishes a bounded directional candidate. That is not yet enough.

A claim can be carefully framed, locally warranted, and internally coherent while still depending on an unexamined viewing position. The analyst may have preserved the declared reference and question yet treated the initial perspective as though it were neutral. A framework can thereby reproduce its own standpoint while mistaking the reproduction for robustness.

ROT is VECTOR's answer to that risk.

Its core question is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{What happens to orientation when relevant viewing conditions are deliberately changed?} } \]

ROT (Rotation) has two functions. It can test an existing directional claim by changing a relevant perspective while preserving enough of the original claim for comparison. It can also discover relations that the original position did not make legible.

\[ \boxed{ ROT=\text{test}+\text{discovery}. } \]

The distinction is productive only if the two functions remain separate. A change that generates insight need not increase the robustness of the original claim. A special case may reveal what blocks an option without making the option real in the original scene. A rival model may expose a blind spot while changing enough of the framing that the result becomes a new bounded claim rather than simple same-claim confirmation.

ROT therefore expands freedom of examination, not freedom from reference.

5.1 Perspective as a Variable

Part I introduced

\[ \pi=\text{perspectival position} \]

as a claim-bearing coordinate. Chapter 5 turns that coordinate into an explicit testing variable.

The initial position \(\pi_0\) has no epistemic privilege merely because it is the position from which the analysis began. It may be institutionally dominant, culturally familiar, internally coherent, or simply easiest for the analyst to access. None of those conditions makes it neutral.

At the same time, perspectival variation does not imply perspectival equality.

\[ \pi_A\neq\pi_B \not\Rightarrow W_A=W_B. \]

One position may have access to effects another cannot observe. Another may possess information that the first lacks. One may be heavily exposed to a consequence but poorly placed to reconstruct system-level dependencies. Another may see infrastructure clearly while externalizing local cost. ROT (Rotation) does not decide in advance which position is superior. It makes their difference available for testing.

This is why perspectival freedom should not be confused with relativism. VECTOR does not claim that every orientation is true from some point of view. It claims that the position from which a claim is made is itself a variable capable of affecting what becomes legible.

The move is especially important in a framework such as PMS because the analyst is also situated. The use of a common operator grammar does not place the analyst outside the field of framing, learned legibility, asymmetry, or recontextualization. A sophisticated meta-perspective can still become a default perspective. ROT (Rotation) therefore applies in principle to the analyst's position as well as to the positions represented inside the scene.

No view becomes privileged merely by remaining unrotated.

5.2 Definition of ROT

Let

\[ C_0=(O,F,Q,\pi_0,t) \]

be the conditions of a current VECTOR claim.

A rotation can be written schematically as:

\[ \boxed{ ROT_\rho(C_0)\rightarrow C_1, } \]

where (\rho) identifies the declared rotation family.

A rotation is not defined merely by the fact that something changes. It requires a rotation contract: a statement of what is changed, what is intended to remain constant, and why the change is relevant to the current claim.

A minimal contract can therefore be written:

\[ \rho=(\Delta C,C_{held},purpose). \]

In a role rotation, for example, the perspectival coordinate may change:

\[ \pi_A\rightarrow\pi_B, \]

while the analyst attempts to preserve:

[
O,F,Q,t.
]

This need not imply perfect invariance. Real role changes can alter access to information or exposure. The point is not to pretend that only one formal variable has changed. The point is to make the relevant changes visible enough to judge what kind of claim the result can support.

The contract therefore performs two jobs.

First, it prevents accidental claim substitution. If the object or frame has materially changed, the analyst must acknowledge that the result may belong to a new bounded claim.

Second, it makes the rotation reproducible enough for criticism. Another analyst can ask whether the claimed constants were in fact preserved, whether the changed condition was relevant, and whether the rotation had real capacity to alter the result.

ROT is consequently not arbitrary reframing. It is declared change under a continuity burden.

5.3 Robustness-Bearing Rotation and Exploratory Probe

The most important distinction in the ROT architecture is between a robustness-bearing rotation and an exploratory probe.

A robustness-bearing rotation changes a materially relevant viewing condition while preserving enough of the original reference, frame, question, and temporal claim to continue testing the same directional proposition.

A role rotation is the cleanest example. Suppose the initial claim is made from \(\pi_A\). The analysis then moves to \(\pi_B\) while keeping the relevant scene and question stable enough for the same DIR (Direction) to be challenged. If the directional relation remains, the result can increase warrant for the original bounded claim.

Schematically:

\[ DIR(C_0) \xrightarrow{ROT_\rho} DIR(C_1). \]

If the claim-relevant relation remains substantially intact under a demanding and admissible change of view, one may have reason to strengthen (W(DIR)).

An exploratory probe is different. It deliberately changes a condition in order to discover what the original configuration depends on. A counterfactual rotation may alter a resource, a temporal window, a role relation, or a frame. A granularity rotation may change resolution. A rival-model rotation may change the conceptual apparatus through which the reference is rendered legible.

Such moves can generate substantial orientation. They can show which condition carries the result, expose a hidden option, reveal a false symmetry, or generate a new relational hypothesis. But they do not automatically increase the robustness of the original claim.

\[ \boxed{ \text{orientation gain} \neq \text{same-claim robustness gain}. } \]

The distinction is essential because exploratory insight is often rhetorically powerful. Once an alternative frame makes something visible, the analyst may be tempted to speak as if the original claim had survived the test. It may not have survived at all. It may have been replaced by a better claim.

VECTOR treats that replacement as progress, but not as survival.

This distinction later becomes structurally parallel to the self-test rule:

\[ Revision\neq Robustness. \]

At the level of ROT, the corresponding rule is that a productive change of question or frame can improve orientation without proving the original statement.

5.4 Perspectival Freedom without Arbitrary Reframing

Perspectival freedom becomes epistemically empty if the analyst may change any condition until a preferred result appears.

VECTOR therefore places a simple restriction on ROT:

\[ \boxed{ \text{perspectival freedom} \neq \text{claim laundering}. } \]

Suppose the original frame is \(F_0\). An analyst constructs a special case \(F^*\) in which an option becomes feasible or a conflict disappears. The special case may be analytically excellent. It may reveal exactly which condition blocks the option in the original configuration. What it cannot do is silently establish that the same option was available under \(F_0\).

\[ o\in\mathcal O(F^*) \not\Rightarrow o\in\mathcal O(F_0). \]

This yields a crucial double boundary:

\[ \boxed{ \text{invalid as an option} \not\Rightarrow \text{invalid as a probe}. } \]

The distinction preserves imagination without confusing imagination with practical availability. It also allows ROT (Rotation) to be genuinely generative. A counter-scene may be valuable precisely because it differs from the original scene. The analytical gain lies in the difference, not in pretending that the difference was absent.

Arbitrary reframing also includes more subtle moves. An analyst may remove a difficult affected role from the frame, redefine the question after encountering a counterexample, or change granularity until the original problem becomes invisible. These moves may produce new claims, but they cannot count as evidence that the initial claim survived.

ROT therefore grants freedom to change perspective under explicit conditions. It does not grant freedom to change the object of evaluation without consequence.

5.5 Reference Continuity under Rotation

Reference continuity is the hinge between exploration and robustness.

A useful rotation need not preserve every descriptive detail of the initial analysis. If that were required, most significant perspective changes would be impossible. But a rotation that materially changes the reference object cannot silently serve as evidence about the original reference.

\[ \boxed{ \text{A perspective change that materially changes the reference} \text{ cannot silently count as evidence about the original reference.} } \]

The requirement is familiar from STRATA, where transformation must preserve source traceability and declared reference conditions rather than allow higher-level labels to replace their source. VECTOR uses the discipline for a different purpose. ROT (Rotation) is not COMPOSE, DECOMPOSE, or PROJECT_AS. The shared issue is simply that analytical mobility can otherwise conceal a change of claim.

Reference continuity can be strong, weak, or broken depending on the case. A role rotation within the same bounded scene may preserve it strongly. A temporal rotation across a major institutional change may preserve only a historical relation. A rival model may retain the same empirical reference while changing conceptual segmentation enough to make exact same-claim comparison difficult.

The appropriate response is not to ban the weaker cases. It is to type their evidential consequence correctly.

Where continuity is strong, the rotation may bear directly on robustness.

Where continuity is partial, the rotation may reveal conditionality or generate a revised claim.

Where continuity breaks, the result is exploratory or belongs to a new object.

Reference continuity thus prevents ROT from gaining strength merely by moving its target.

5.6 Learned Legibility and the Non-Privilege of the Unrotated View

A stable perspective can become invisible as perspective.

PMS–EDEN provides a useful local precedent through its treatment of learned legibility and false coordination. A field can become highly readable inside a stabilized framing while systematically obscuring a real asymmetry. The problem is not that the participants see nothing. It is that what has become easy to see may itself be structured by a learned coordination pattern.

VECTOR generalizes the epistemic pressure without importing EDEN's domain claim wholesale.

\[ \boxed{ \text{No viewpoint gains privilege merely from being unexamined.} } \]

This rule has two consequences.

First, the default view is not neutral by default. Familiarity, institutionalization, or analytical convenience cannot substitute for testing.

Second, ROT (Rotation) does not produce a final external standpoint. The analyst who rotates among positions remains situated. A rival model can expose limits in the analyst's vocabulary. A later temporal position can reveal dependencies that were genuinely unavailable earlier. A granularity change can show that the previous level of description was either too coarse or unnecessarily fine.

The goal is not a view from nowhere. It is a claim that becomes more explicit about the conditions under which it survives.

This is also why perspectival freedom does not dissolve objectivity. The relevant structure may be real even when access to it is uneven. If an affected role sees a cost that the initiating role did not see, the cost does not become “true for one perspective and false for another.” The perspectives differ in legibility. ROT (Rotation) makes that difference itself analytically available.

5.7 Rotation Families

VECTOR uses rotation families as recurring types of admissible pressure. They are not exhaustive, and their names do not guarantee validity. Each concrete rotation still requires a declared contract and a relevant loss possibility.

5.7.1 Role Rotation

Role rotation changes the relational position from which the same bounded scene is examined.

\[ \pi_A\rightarrow\pi_B. \]

Where reference, frame, question, and temporal position remain sufficiently stable, role rotation is among the strongest robustness-bearing ROT forms.

Its purpose is not to ask whose subjective account should win. It asks whether the directional relation depends on one role's structural position. A claim about coordination, cost, access, authority, or correction that disappears immediately when the role changes may have been narrower than initially stated.

5.7.2 Actor / Affected-Role Rotation

This family makes a particularly important asymmetry visible: the role that chooses or initiates an action may not be the role that bears its effects.

The rotation asks what changes when analysis moves from actor-centered legibility to the position of those materially affected by the praxis relation.

It does not automatically privilege the affected role. Exposure can reveal costs while obscuring system-level dependencies; initiating roles can see constraints that affected roles do not. The value of the rotation lies in preventing one position from silently representing the whole field.

5.7.3 Inside / Outside Rotation

Inside/outside rotation changes whether the analysis emphasizes the internal functional logic of a configuration or its external relations and effects.

An institution may be internally coherent while exporting cost. A local practice may be intelligible from the inside while becoming structurally unstable when dependencies outside the immediate frame are included. Conversely, an external reading may miss coordination requirements that are only visible from inside the role system.

The rotation therefore tests a recurring boundary:

\[ \text{internal coherence} \neq \text{external consequence adequacy}. \]

5.7.4 Intention / Effect Rotation

Intention/effect rotation changes the focal relation from declared or supportable intention to structurally produced effect.

It is not permission to infer hidden mental states. Intention must be available through declaration or sufficiently bounded evidence. Nor does effect automatically determine blame. The rotation asks a narrower question: does the current DIR (Direction) depend on treating intended function as though it exhausted actual consequence?

This family is especially useful where good intention stabilizes a frame that obscures externalized effect, or where negative effect is used to infer malicious intention without warrant.

5.7.5 Temporal Rotation

Temporal rotation changes the temporal cut from which a claim is reconstructed.

\[ t_{short}\rightarrow t_{long} \]

or more generally:

\[ t_0\rightarrow t_1. \]

This rotation is often exploratory or conditionally robustness-bearing rather than a simple same-claim test. Later states may contain information and consequences that were genuinely unavailable earlier. They must not be retroactively imported as though the earlier actor possessed them.

Temporal rotation can nevertheless reveal periodization dependence. A claim that appears strongly directional over a short interval may reverse once downstream costs become visible. Another claim may appear weak in a snapshot but become strongly supported over a trajectory.

The correct result is often not “the earlier view was irrational” but “the directional claim is temporally bounded.”

5.7.6 Ω-Position Rotation

Ω-position rotation changes the position relative to asymmetry, exposure, dependency, capacity, or cost.

It asks whether the current direction remains when the analysis is reconstructed from another location in the asymmetry field.

This can reveal that what appears reversible to one role is already costly or irreversible to another; that a low-cost option for the decision-maker transfers substantial burden elsewhere; or that a supposedly constrained actor actually possesses structural power unavailable to those affected.

Again, Ω-position is not moral rank. It is relational position inside an asymmetry structure.

5.7.7 Counterfactual Rotation

Counterfactual rotation changes one or more declared conditions to test dependency.

A useful counterfactual does not ask only “what if things were different?” It asks which specific change would alter the current relation and what this reveals about the original conditions.

The analytical value frequently lies in:

\[ \Delta(F_0,F^*). \]

If removing one constraint makes a previously impossible option feasible, the result identifies the constraint as structurally load-bearing. It does not make the option retroactively available.

Counterfactual rotation is therefore the paradigmatic exploratory probe.

5.7.8 Rival-Model Rotation

Rival-model rotation exposes the reference to a conceptually different model that is genuinely allowed to discriminate better.

This condition is crucial. A rival that is immediately translated into PMS vocabulary and evaluated only by how well it reproduces PMS distinctions is not a serious rival. The comparison must preserve enough of the rival's own organizing logic for local superiority to remain possible.

A rival can expose over-complexity, under-discrimination, framework habituation, or a false directional relation. It can also fail. In either case, the result is informative only if victory was not predetermined.

Rival-model ROT later becomes central to convergence and bounded test-burden closure.

5.7.9 Granularity Rotation

Granularity rotation changes resolution.

\[ coarse\leftrightarrow fine. \]

Finer description can reveal hidden relations, but it can also generate noise, lose functional structure, or exceed the relevance floor of the question. Coarser description can reveal stable pattern while erasing decisive local differences.

Accordingly:

\[ \boxed{ \text{finer} \neq \text{better}. } \]

The directional claim should survive the level of resolution relevant to (Q), not every possible decomposition.

If a granularity change materially retypes the reference object rather than merely pressuring the same claim, it no longer counts as same-claim robustness evidence. It becomes an exploratory probe or, where the operation is more accurately typed there, a PMS-STRATA (controlled transformation) transformation with its own bounded claim.

5.8 Rotation Outcomes

A rotation can produce several qualitatively different results. VECTOR uses four primary outcome types.

5.8.1 Invariant

The claim-relevant directional relation remains substantially intact under the rotation.

This is bounded invariance, not metaphysical invariance. The result supports the claim only relative to the pressure actually applied.

5.8.2 Variant

The directional relation changes in strength, scope, form, or direction.

Variation is not automatically failure. A genuinely role-dependent relation should vary under role rotation. A temporally bounded direction should change when the temporal window changes. The question is whether the variation was already contained in the claim or forces revision.

5.8.3 Emergent

The rotation reveals a relation not present in the original formulation.

Emergence creates a new claim candidate. It does not validate the relation merely by making it visible.

\[ \boxed{ \text{emergent relation} \neq \text{validated relation}. } \]

5.8.4 Collapse

The original DIR no longer carries under the relevant rotation.

Collapse is a legitimate and often valuable result.

\[ \boxed{ Collapse\text{ can be VECTOR working correctly.} } \]

If a claim disappears when a materially relevant affected-role perspective is introduced, the loss is not an embarrassment to be repaired by redefining ROT (Rotation). It is evidence that the original orientation was narrower or weaker than asserted.

A split or trade-off need not be a fifth primary outcome. It can arise as a particular form of variation or emergence and then be typed at the DIR level as multidirectional.

5.9 Rotation Must Be Able to Hurt

ROT becomes decorative if its admissibility criteria guarantee survival.

VECTOR therefore requires genuine claim-loss capacity:

\[ \boxed{ ROT\text{ must possess claim-loss capacity.} } \]

A relevant rotation must be capable, in principle, of making the claim weaker, narrower, reversed, multidirectional, incomparable, or non-capturable.

This requirement is stronger than a generic call for “multiple perspectives.” A framework can collect many perspectives while allowing none of them to alter its central judgment. It can celebrate pluralism at the descriptive level while preserving an invariant conclusion by definition. VECTOR treats that as pseudo-rotation.

At the same time, loss capacity does not mean maximal hostility. A deliberately absurd perspective or irrelevant frame change can destroy almost any claim. The burden is relevant adverse capacity. The rotation must preserve enough type integrity to count as pressure on the current claim while remaining capable of changing the result.

This is where ROT (Rotation) connects to the self-critical discipline already present in PMS. EDEN's counter-scene requirement and UNDER LOAD's insistence that framework success must not become immunity both point toward the same methodological condition: a test only counts where a negative result remains possible.

The positive selection rule is therefore:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Rotation selection follows claim vulnerability, not desired outcome.} } \]

A robustness-bearing ROT (Rotation) should satisfy five burdens: claim relevance, sufficient reference continuity, material distinctness from pressure already applied, a credible route to claim loss, and a declared reason why this rotation matters to this claim. Material distinctness is not a count. The analyst must ask whether the ROT pressures a genuinely different dependency—role, asymmetry, temporal cut, model assumption, frame vulnerability, or another claim-bearing condition—rather than restating pressure already applied.

The practical implication is simple. When selecting rotations, the analyst should ask not only:

What other perspective can I include?

but:

Which admissible perspective could make this claim materially smaller if I am wrong?

That is a much stronger test.

Testing also needs a bounded stop rule. ROT (Rotation) pressure may stop for the current claim when known claim-critical vulnerabilities have been materially pressured; remaining rotations are redundant or irrelevant to the declared question; further testing depends on unavailable external evidence; a stronger result would require a new claim scope; or the current result is already Suspend, Non-Capture, or Revision Required. Stopping closes the declared current test burden. It does not close the object or prohibit later reopening under new evidence, a new rival, or a materially different claim.

5.10 Rotational Robustness

A DIR (Direction) that survives one relevant rotation may gain warrant. A DIR that survives several materially distinct and differently constraining rotations may gain more. But rotational robustness is not the number of rotations survived.

\[ \boxed{ \text{rotation count} \neq \text{rotational robustness}. } \]

Twenty near-identical role substitutions can provide less pressure than one serious rival-model rotation. Several rotations may share the same hidden premise. A long list of counterfactuals may all depend on one framing choice. Count therefore cannot substitute for the quality and distinctness of the burden.

Nor should VECTOR casually describe rotations as epistemically independent. Within one framework, shared concepts, source assumptions, and analytical habits create dependencies. The safer standard is:

materially distinct and differently constraining pressure.

\[ \boxed{ \text{distinct pressure} \neq \text{independent evidence}. } \]

Rotational robustness therefore increases only where the claim remains meaningfully the same, the pressure is relevant, the rotation could have damaged the claim, and the surviving relation is not merely reproduced by shared assumptions.

A cautious schematic implication is:

\[ \text{survival under materially relevant admissible ROT} \Rightarrow \text{possible warrant increase}. \]

Not:

\[ \Rightarrow \text{truth}. \]

This distinction also preserves the separation between DIR (Direction) and warrant. The directional relation can remain nominally unchanged while the reasons for trusting it improve. Conversely, a claim may survive multiple rotations only because the rotations were weak, correlated, or badly contracted. Apparent stability is not enough.

5.11 Rotation as Difference and Relation Generator

ROT is not only a defensive procedure. It is also a generator of relational information.

Suppose a cost relation is invisible from \(\pi_A\) but central from \(\pi_B\). The important result is not merely that two perspectives disagree. The difference itself reveals a structural relation: cost visibility is distributed by role position.

Or suppose a counterfactual in which one institutional constraint is removed creates a new feasible option. The special case does not prove that the option existed originally. It identifies the constraint as part of the original option topology.

This generative function can be expressed schematically as:

\[ \Delta\big(ROT_{\rho_1}(C),ROT_{\rho_2}(C)\big) \rightarrow \text{new relation candidate}. \]

The resulting questions may concern asymmetry, visibility, framing, cost, temporality, or option structure. ROT can therefore expand the field of orientation even when it weakens the original DIR.

This yields the final boundary of the chapter:

\[ \boxed{ \text{discovery} \neq \text{automatic warrant}. } \]

A newly visible relation must itself be typed, bounded, and tested. ROT creates no exemption from the requirements it imposes on the claim it tests.

The chapter therefore leaves VECTOR in a richer but more difficult position. Direction can now be subjected to perspectival pressure, and perspective can itself reveal new structure. Yet the practical significance of that increased orientation remains unknown. An analyst may recognize that a claim should be revised while an institution is already committed to it. A role may become legible only after an action window has nearly closed. A new relation may be visible while almost no practical room remains to act on the insight.

That is the problem of DIST.

6. DIST — Derived Orientation–Enactment Separation

DIR (Direction) answers whether a bounded structural difference carries direction. ROT (Rotation) asks what happens when that direction is exposed to admissible perspectival change. Neither core component by itself states how much practical room remains between the current orientation and its consolidation into enactment, public commitment, institutional procedure, or self-binding.

That relation is usefully summarized by DIST.

DIST (Distance) is a derived supporting construct, not a third quality axis or third core component. It does not answer whether an orientation is good, true, mature, or responsible. It asks how effectively separated orientation currently remains from enactment while requiring the analysis to retain the actual source conditions that generate that separation. Chapter 19 tests whether this relation earns independent core status and reduces it after finding that the same discrimination can be made without informational loss through existing PMS and praxis relations.

[
\boxed{
DIST_t

\text{effective orientation–enactment separation}.
}
]

The word effective is essential. DIST (Distance) is not elapsed clock time. It is not reflective sophistication. It is not simply the presence of the PMS operator (Χ) (distance). It is a derived relational description of multiple conditions that determine how much real room remains for additional rotation, revision, delay, commitment, or redirection before praxis becomes more costly or binding.

Chapter 6 therefore describes a current derived orientation–enactment relation. Chapter 7 will ask the different question of how much reversal or correction remains possible after the path already taken.

\[ \boxed{ DIST \neq REV_{avail}. } \]

6.1 DIST Is Not Χ

PMS already contains (Χ), distance. VECTOR does not rename it.

\[ \boxed{DIST\neq Χ.} \]

The distinction can be seen in cases where reflective distance is available but practical separation from enactment is small. An actor may still be able to step back cognitively, reconsider a claim, or imagine alternatives while facing an institutional deadline that will irreversibly commit resources within hours. In such a case, (Χ) (distance) may remain substantial while effective DIST (Distance) is low.

The reverse is also possible. A process may have considerable practical delay before enactment while possessing very poor reflective distance. A committee may be months away from implementation yet already cognitively and socially bound to one framing. Calendar time is high; effective orientation–enactment separation may be much lower.

The relation summarized by DIST is not identical to any one PMS operator. Its reconstruction can depend on:

\[ Χ,\Theta,\Psi,\Omega,Α, \]

as well as publicness, role position, institutional procedure, material constraints, and other scene-specific conditions.

The relation is not expressed as a universal function

\[ DIST=f(Χ,\Theta,\Psi,\Omega,Α,\ldots) \]

because VECTOR does not claim a complete law connecting those conditions. The point is structural: effective separation from enactment emerges from their interaction and must be reconstructed locally.

This gives DIST a compact descriptive role. (Χ) concerns distance as a PMS function. DIST summarizes how much that and other conditions jointly leave orientation practically separable from enactment in the current scene; the discrimination remains carried by the source relations.

6.2 Orientation–Enactment Separation

The most intuitive mistake about DIST is to treat it as time remaining.

Chronological time matters, but it does not determine effective orientation–enactment separation by itself.

\[ \boxed{ \text{chronological duration} \neq \text{effective DIST}. } \]

A long period can contain very little effective separation if the relevant decision has already been publicly committed, technically locked in, or bound through expectations that make revision disproportionately costly. A short interval can contain substantial DIST (Distance) if the decision remains private, reversible, and institutionally easy to alter.

The operative relation is therefore between orientation and enactment, not between two points on a clock.

Orientation includes the current structured understanding from which a directional claim may be made. Enactment includes more than overt physical action. It can include:

  • public commitment;
  • institutional adoption;
  • procedural lock-in;
  • self-binding that materially changes later freedom;
  • implementation that begins to distribute consequence;
  • role-specific action that closes meaningful alternatives.

DIST is high where significant correction remains practically available before such consolidation. It is low where orientation is already close to becoming difficult to revise in consequence-bearing praxis.

The relation can differ across actors inside the same scene. A decision-maker may still experience a proposal as exploratory while an affected role already faces irreversible preparation costs. An analyst may remain at high epistemic distance while the organization being analyzed has nearly completed implementation.

DIST is therefore not a property of the abstract claim alone. It is a relation among orientation, role, timing, binding, and enactment conditions.

6.3 Transition Reachability

The importance of the relation summarized by DIST lies partly in the practical reachability of transitions.

Where the source conditions leave a wider effective orientation–enactment separation, transitions such as the following may remain comparatively accessible:

\[ ROT, \quad revision, \quad reframing, \quad additional\ evidence, \quad partial\ withdrawal. \]

Where the source conditions leave a narrower separation, the same transitions may become more difficult, more costly, purely analytical, or practically irrelevant to the current enactment.

A wider separation does not cause correction. An actor can have ample room and fail to use it. Nor does a narrow separation make correction impossible by definition. It may still occur at high cost. DIST describes the resulting transition room; it does not produce it.

A useful formulation is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{source conditions}\rightarrow\text{effective transition room}\rightarrow\text{derived DIST description}. } \]

CRITIQUE provides a local precedent for this structure. Correction requires more than irritation or reaction. It depends partly on whether sufficient distance and revision space remain for the initial framing to be interrupted before the response stabilizes into a more difficult trajectory. VECTOR generalizes that local structure across orientation claims.
