Transition reachability also connects DIST (Distance) to ROT (Rotation). A rotation can be analytically conceivable while no longer being practically available to the actor whose praxis is at issue. This distinction becomes explicit in Chapter 8:

\[ ROT_{conceivable} \neq ROT_{practically\ available}. \]

The underlying timing, binding, publicness, reversibility, capacity, and dependency relations can produce the gap; DIST may summarize the resulting separation.

6.4 Enactment Proximity

DIST also makes enactment proximity structurally visible.

The term proximity should not be interpreted geometrically. There is no default formula such as

\[ speed=\frac{1}{DIST}. \]

A low-DIST orientation is not physically near an action in a measurable space. It is close in the practical sense that fewer low-cost transitions remain between the current orientation and consequential consolidation.

This may occur because:

  • a decision deadline is imminent;
  • a public statement has already been prepared;
  • resources have been committed;
  • expectations have been created;
  • an institutional process has moved beyond an easy correction point;
  • an actor has bound themselves in ways that raise later reversal cost.

The relevant claim is therefore modest:

\[ \boxed{ \text{timing / binding / publicness / capacity / reversibility}\rightarrow\text{enactment proximity}, } \]

with DIST available only as a derived description of that relation.

It does not uniquely determine enactment. An actor can refuse to proceed even at very low DIST (Distance). An external event can reopen the situation. A procedural stop can create new room. But the current relation to enactment has changed, and treating it as identical to an earlier high-DIST stage would lose relevant praxis structure.

6.5 Revision Interval

The term revision interval names the practically available region in which an orientation can still be tested, narrowed, reframed, or redirected before additional consolidation materially changes the conditions of revision.

It is not a fixed duration. A revision interval can be brief in clock time and large in practical freedom, or long in clock time and almost empty in meaningful corrective possibility.

The interval ends not when revision becomes metaphysically impossible, but when the relevant claim moves into a materially different cost and binding regime.

This distinction prevents Chapter 6 from collapsing into Chapter 7. The revision interval concerns what room is currently available before further consolidation. Reversibility concerns what can still be corrected or reversed given the trajectory already accumulated.

An orientation can therefore have meaningful current DIST (Distance) while already carrying substantial historical irreversibility. A late-stage institution may temporarily pause implementation and regain some revision interval, yet prior commitments, lost options, and downstream effects may remain unrecoverable.

\[ \boxed{ \text{new revision space} \neq \text{restored history}. } \]

The distinction becomes central in the Reversibility Gradient.

6.6 Source Relations of Derived DIST

Effective orientation–enactment separation is constituted by interacting conditions rather than one universal variable. DIST is the optional shorthand for the resulting relation. The following source relations are especially recurrent within PMS and VECTOR.

6.6.1 Χ — Distance Availability

(Χ) can increase effective separation by making interruption, reconsideration, or reframing possible. Without distance availability, orientation may collapse quickly into reaction or habitual enactment.

But (Χ) alone is insufficient. Reflective capacity can coexist with a closing window, sunk costs, or public commitments that make actual revision increasingly difficult.

Accordingly:

\[ Χ\uparrow \not\Rightarrow DIST\uparrow \]

as a universal law.

The relation is conditional on the rest of the scene.

6.6.2 Θ — Temporal Compression

(Θ) can compress effective orientation–enactment separation by changing the cost of delay and the availability of future transitions; the result may be described as lower derived DIST.

A closing window can make an orientation–enactment separation shrink even when nothing has yet been enacted. The relevant possibility may simply be disappearing with time.

Temporal compression can also work unevenly. One role may experience an approaching deadline while another still imagines the scene as open. That divergence is not necessarily a matter of subjective urgency; the roles may face genuinely different temporal structures.

6.6.3 Ψ — Binding

(Ψ) changes the relation between current orientation and future freedom.

A self-binding, commitment, contract, institutional decision, or durable promise can reduce the ease with which an orientation remains practically separate from enactment. This does not make binding a pathology. Many forms of coordination require it.

The point is structural:

\[ \boxed{ \text{binding changes future freedom.} } \]

Chapter 7 will examine the resulting reversal budget. Binding can reduce the practical space for low-cost revision; lower derived DIST may summarize that changed relation.

6.6.4 Ω — Exposure and Asymmetry

Effective orientation–enactment separation can be distributed asymmetrically; derived DIST may therefore differ by role.

An actor with decision authority may possess substantial room to revise while those exposed to the decision have already incurred preparation costs. A vulnerable role may experience a rapidly shrinking window while the institution retains procedural discretion. A powerful actor may be able to reopen a decision that others cannot.

Thus:

\[ DIST_A\neq DIST_B \]

may hold in one scene.

This is not merely a perspectival disagreement about how much time remains. It may be a real asymmetry in practical revision affordance.

6.6.5 Α — Attractor Inertia

Stable attractors can narrow effective orientation–enactment separation without making alternatives logically impossible.

A practice may become so routinized that deviations require significant coordination, explanation, or institutional effort. An interpretation may become so stabilized that alternative readings are immediately translated back into the familiar frame. An organization may still possess formal discretion while its practical corridor has narrowed through repeated enactment.

Attractor inertia therefore matters as a source of transition friction. It can make orientation appear farther from revision than clock time alone would suggest.

6.6.6 Publicness

Publicness can alter exposure, commitment, reputational cost, coordination effects, and reversal conditions, thereby changing effective orientation–enactment separation.

A private working hypothesis can often be revised cheaply. The same statement, once made publicly by an institution, may create expectations and dependencies that reduce later correction room. Publicness can therefore increase (Ω) (asymmetry), harden (Ψ) (self-binding), accelerate temporal sedimentation, and raise reversal costs.

This does not produce a default directional rule:

\[ private>public \]

is not a VECTOR principle. Public action may be necessary, responsible, or the only way to create correction. Publicness is a modulator, not a quality category.

6.6.7 Role-Relative DIST

The preceding dependencies converge on one important conclusion:

\[ \boxed{ \text{There need not be one scene-wide DIST value.} } \]

Different roles can occupy different orientation–enactment relations inside the same nominal situation.

CONFLICT supplies a particularly clear precedent. Parties can be temporally asynchronous. One role may still be exploring possible correction while another has already absorbed irreversible risk, continuity load, or reputational cost. To describe the whole scene as “still open” because one actor retains options would erase the other's practical position.

Role-relative DIST (Distance) therefore protects VECTOR from treating perspective as the only source of disagreement. Sometimes roles do not merely see different revision possibilities. They actually possess different revision possibilities.

This distinction will later matter for option fields, capacity, timing, and responsibility.

6.7 Narrow Orientation–Enactment Separation

Configurations summarized as low derived DIST can carry recognizable risks.

A directional candidate may move toward enactment before relevant rotation has occurred. A public commitment may harden before affected-role consequences are visible. An actor may interpret immediate confidence as sufficient reason to bind. A reaction may become institutional procedure before correction remains easy.

These possibilities can be summarized only after the operative source relations are stated: an untested DIR combined with a closing review window, early binding, public fixation, or reduced reversibility can create a risk of premature consolidation. Low derived DIST may summarize that configuration; it does not cause the risk.

But low DIST is not intrinsically bad.

A genuinely closing temporal window may leave little room. Emergency response, infrastructure continuity, or an irreversible external event can compress the revision interval without this compression itself constituting error. In some cases, acting under low DIST (Distance) may be the least damaging available path.

The point is therefore not to maximize distance but to know when distance is low and what this changes about the burden of orientation.

6.8 Excessively Preserved Orientation–Enactment Separation

Deliberately preserving a wide orientation–enactment separation can also become a failure condition; high derived DIST may summarize such a configuration.

A framework that treats continued openness as automatically superior can use reflection to avoid consequence. Repeated reframing can substitute for decision. Additional rotations can be demanded indefinitely even after the relevant test burden has been met. A real option can disappear while the actor continues to preserve analytical distance.

CRITIQUE and ANTICIPATION both contain local forms of this pressure. Protected revision windows matter, but endless revision can become its own practice. Anticipation can improve timing, but waiting for impossible certainty can allow the field to change irreversibly.

Thus:

\[ \boxed{ DIST\uparrow \not\Rightarrow orientation\uparrow. } \]

The conditions associated with high derived DIST can preserve correction. Deliberately maintaining those conditions can also contribute to commentary paralysis, missed windows, continuing externalization, or refusal to bind where binding is necessary for the declared function.

The correct relation remains local and question-bound.

6.9 More Distance Does Not Mean More Adequacy

DIST is therefore a derived descriptor, not a quality axis.

\[ \boxed{ DIST\text{ is a derived descriptor, not a quality axis.} } \]

This boundary is necessary because the language of distance easily inherits positive connotations: more reflection, more detachment, more optionality, more freedom. VECTOR does not reject those possible benefits. It rejects their monotonic conversion into adequacy.

The appropriate orientation–enactment separation depends on the declared question, temporal window, asymmetry, capacity, existing bindings, and consequence structure. A configuration summarized by high DIST can be excellent for one phase of inquiry and destructive in another. A configuration summarized by low DIST can reveal premature closure or accurately reflect that the field has become genuinely constrained.

VECTOR therefore does not seek an optimal universal distance. It seeks to make the current orientation–enactment separation visible enough that later judgments do not confuse reflection with reversibility, delay with safety, or commitment with irreversible correctness.

This distinction creates the next theoretical problem. The source relations summarized by DIST can tell us how much effective room remains before further consolidation. They cannot by themselves tell us what prior consolidation has already made difficult, expensive, or impossible to undo.

That requires a path-sensitive concept of reversibility.

7. The Reversibility Gradient

Derived DIST (Distance) summarizes the current effective separation between orientation and enactment. That relation can be analytically important, but it remains insufficient for one reason: present room for reflection does not reveal what the path already taken has made costly or unavailable.

A system can regain analytical distance after a decision without recovering the options that disappeared because of the decision. An actor can revise a belief while remaining bound by consequences produced under the earlier belief. An institution can reopen deliberation after public commitment while facing dependencies that did not exist before the commitment.

Reversibility therefore has to be path-sensitive.

VECTOR uses the Reversibility Gradient to describe the non-binary and historically conditioned character of correction, redirection, exit, and recovery.

The term gradient does not imply a universal metric. It signals that practical reversibility can vary by path, role, object, and time.

A minimal notation is:

\[ \boxed{ REV_{avail}(a,t\mid F,Q). } \]

This records the available practical correction or reversal budget for a relevant actor or role under bounded conditions.

The governing distinction remains:

\[ \boxed{ DIST \neq REV_{avail}. } \]

DIST concerns present orientation–enactment separation. Reversibility concerns what the accumulated path still permits.

7.1 Available Reversibility

Available reversibility is the remaining range of practically meaningful correction, redirection, exit, modification, or partial recovery that the current path still allows.

The definition is deliberately broader than literal reversal. A trajectory may be impossible to return to its original state while still allowing meaningful correction. An institution may be unable to undo a public event but able to change its downstream handling. An actor may be unable to restore a lost relationship while still being able to stop further damage. A technical decision may be irreversible at one layer but replaceable at another.

This is why:

\[ \boxed{ \text{reversibility} \neq \text{restoration}. } \]

Restoration asks whether a prior state can be recreated. Reversibility asks which elements of the current trajectory can still be altered and at what structural cost.

The distinction prevents an all-or-nothing model. If exact restoration is impossible, a binary theory would label the situation irreversible and stop discriminating. VECTOR instead asks what correction remains possible, what has been lost, what can be redirected, and which consequences persist regardless of later orientation.

Available reversibility is also role-relative. An institution may be able to reverse a policy while an individual cannot reverse costs already borne. A powerful role may reopen a decision that a dependent role cannot escape. The same scene can therefore contain different \(REV_{avail}\) relations at once.

No universal numerical value follows. Statements such as

\[ REV_A<REV_B \]

are legitimate only where the bounded comparison supports an ordinal relation.

7.2 Reversibility as Path-Dependent

Reversibility cannot be inferred from a snapshot alone.

Two present configurations can appear similar while carrying very different histories:

\[ state_A(t)\approx state_B(t) \]

but

\[ history_A\neq history_B. \]

If those histories produced different commitments, lost options, dependencies, or consequences, then:

\[ REV_A(t)\neq REV_B(t) \]

may hold even where the present states look alike.

STRATA provides a strong methodological precedent for this claim. Its PATH structure treats path dependence as a property in which present meaning, cost, or continuation cannot be adequately reconstructed without historical development. It also explicitly rejects the idea that path dependence implies determinism. New frames and events may alter trajectories, while earlier structures remain part of the path.

VECTOR adopts the same historical discipline for reversibility.

Path dependence can arise through:

  • prior self-binding;
  • public commitment;
  • institutional reliance;
  • resource expenditure;
  • lost alternatives;
  • accumulated exposure;
  • repeated enactment that stabilizes an attractor;
  • downstream effects already borne by other roles.

These conditions make current possibility historically structured. They do not necessarily eliminate alternatives. They change their cost, availability, or meaning.

This is why “we can still choose differently” is not yet an adequate reversibility claim. The relevant question is what choosing differently now would actually change and what it cannot change anymore.

7.3 Binding Changes Future Freedom

Binding is one of the clearest ways in which praxis changes later orientation conditions.

A binding can be explicit—a contract, promise, public commitment, institutional adoption—or implicit in repeated coordination and dependency. In each case, the important structural effect is that future options no longer have the same practical relation as before.

\[ \boxed{ \text{binding changes future freedom.} } \]

This proposition should not be read as an argument against binding.

Reliable coordination often requires exactly the opposite of maximal reversibility. Commitments allow others to plan. Institutional continuity requires decisions to stabilize. Responsibility can require self-binding rather than perpetual optionality. Without durable expectations, many forms of collective praxis would become impossible.

VECTOR therefore treats binding neither as virtue nor pathology. It treats binding as trajectory formation.

Once (\Psi) (self-binding) enters a path, later redirection can require new acts of explanation, compensation, renegotiation, repair, or withdrawal. Even where a binding is legitimate and desirable, its presence changes the structure of future correction.

The central question is not whether binding should be avoided. It is whether the current orientation recognizes what binding has made possible and what it has made harder to undo.

7.4 Rotation Cost and Reversal Cost

The distinction between changing orientation and changing enacted praxis is formalized through two different cost relations:

\[ \boxed{ C_{ROT} \neq C_{REV}. } \]

Rotation Cost

\(C_{ROT}\) concerns the practical friction involved in reorienting a relevant actor, role, institution, or interpretive field.

It can include:

  • reputational cost of revising a public position;
  • organizational relearning;
  • role-identity friction;
  • loss of coherence in a stabilized narrative;
  • institutional embarrassment;
  • coordination effort required to make another framing usable.

The term does not refer to the analyst's computational effort in performing a ROT (Rotation) operation. An analyst can rotate a model cheaply while the organization represented by that model would face enormous practical friction in reorienting.

Reversal Cost

\(C_{REV}\) concerns the cost of materially changing a praxis that has already been enacted or bound.

It can include:

  • sunk resources;
  • technical rollback;
  • broken dependencies;
  • irreversible third-party consequences;
  • lost trust;
  • lost windows;
  • transition costs created by undoing a prior implementation.

The two costs can move independently.

A particularly important VECTOR configuration is:

\[ C_{ROT}\downarrow \quad\text{while}\quad C_{REV}\uparrow. \]

The actor or institution may become increasingly able to recognize that the current orientation is wrong while becoming increasingly unable to reverse what has been done under it.

This is one reason orientation cannot be treated as a purely epistemic state. The practical value of recognizing error depends partly on when the recognition occurs and what the path has already sedimented.

7.5 Temporal Sedimentation, Public Fixation, and Asymmetric Reversibility

Reversibility changes through time even where no dramatic single event occurs.

Repeated enactment can sediment expectations. Public statements can create dependencies. Institutions can allocate resources around a decision. A role can absorb costs that are not recoverable even if the initiating actor later revises the claim.

This accumulation produces asymmetric reversibility.

\[ REV_A(t)\neq REV_B(t) \]

can hold not merely because A and B interpret the situation differently, but because their paths through the situation have actually diverged.

Public fixation is especially important. A private claim can often be revised without forcing others to adapt. Once the claim becomes public praxis, other actors may rely on it, oppose it, incur costs, or organize around it. The same later reorientation therefore operates on a different field.

This also blocks a common misuse of perspective. If an affected role has lost an option, rotating back to the initiating role does not restore the option. The difference is no longer purely epistemic. Praxis has changed the field.

The distinction anticipates Part III:

\[ \boxed{ \text{visibility} \neq \text{availability}. } \]

A role may be perfectly capable of seeing an alternative that is no longer available to it.

7.6 Exit Changes Trajectory; It Does Not Reset It

Exit is one of the clearest cases in which reversibility and restoration diverge.

To exit a role, relationship, institution, or practice can change the future trajectory substantially. It may stop continued exposure, remove a binding, or open new alternatives. But exit does not erase what occurred before exit.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Exit changes trajectory; it does not reset history.} } \]

PMS–SEX repeatedly uses this structural realism in a different domain: recurring praxis does not reset after isolated episodes, and later reframing does not dissolve accumulated cost or outer legibility. VECTOR uses the narrower point here. A later exit can be highly meaningful without functioning as a temporal reset.

The same principle applies beyond literal exit. A policy can be withdrawn while costs remain. A public claim can be retracted while expectations and reputational effects persist. A relationship can be ended while dependencies created during it continue to structure future options.

Exit is therefore one form of redirection inside a path, not a deletion of the path.

7.7 Renewed Orientation–Enactment Separation Does Not Undo Θ

A pause, reopened review window, reduced binding, or other real change can reopen reflection without undoing temporal accumulation; higher derived DIST may summarize the renewed separation.

\[ DIST_t\uparrow \not\Rightarrow \Theta_{<t}=0. \]

A pause may create a new revision interval. It may allow additional ROT, evidence gathering, or reframing. But the passage of time may already have closed options, changed expectations, or distributed costs.

This distinction becomes especially important when an actor responds to a late realization by treating the renewed ability to reflect as though the original situation had returned. It has not. A renewed wider orientation–enactment separation is a new state on the trajectory; high derived DIST (Distance) may summarize it.

The practical question becomes:

What can this new distance still change?

not:

How do we return to the state before the consequences accumulated?

7.8 ROT Does Not Erase Ψ

The same rule applies to reorientation.

\[ ROT(\Psi) \neq undo(\Psi). \]

A perspective change can show that a binding was poorly grounded, too broad, or based on a false orientation. That discovery can justify reconsideration. It does not by itself dissolve the practical effects of the binding.

The distinction is essential to the later Ought discussion. An actor may eventually decide to revise or abandon a maintained goal (G). VECTOR must allow that. But changing the orientation toward (G) is not identical with erasing the commitments, dependencies, or costs already formed around (G).

This is another instance of the general rule:

\[ \text{orientation change} \neq \text{history reset}. \]

The value of ROT lies in making correction possible where possible—not in creating a fiction of untouched reversibility.

7.9 Reversibility Is Not an Absolute Good

A theory that recognizes irreversible cost can easily drift toward the rule that more reversibility is always better.

VECTOR rejects that rule.

\[ \boxed{ more\ REV \neq better\ praxis. } \]

Reversibility matters because it affects correction, exit, and the cost of error. But durable praxis also requires conditions that reduce reversibility. A promise that can be abandoned at no cost may not function as a promise. An institution that reopens every decision indefinitely may fail to coordinate. A relationship that treats all commitments as permanently provisional may preserve optionality by destroying reliability.

The point is not to find a universal middle value. VECTOR does not replace maximal reversibility with a Goldilocks principle of “moderate reversibility.” The appropriate relation remains dependent on function, question, asymmetry, timing, consequence, and legitimate binding.

The theoretical conclusion is narrower and stronger:

Reversibility is a budget to be understood, not a value to be maximized.

A praxis field carries a certain historically conditioned capacity for correction. Good orientation requires knowing something about that capacity, especially where commitment may transform it. But the existence of the budget does not determine how much should be preserved in every case.

With the DIR (Direction)–ROT (Rotation) core, derived DIST (Distance), and reversibility now separated by claim type, VECTOR can ask how orientation and its praxis conditions interact through time. That interaction is recursive, but it is not closed.

8. VECTOR Dynamics

Dynamics here means open recursive dependence and temporal feedback, not predictive transition dynamics.

The minimal VECTOR form is a state description:

[
V_t\(O\mid F,Q,\pi\)

\langle DIR_t,RP_t\rangle.
]

Praxis, however, does not remain still while orientation is being analyzed. Directional claims can change under new perspectives. Effective orientation–enactment separation can shrink as commitments accumulate; derived DIST may summarize that narrowing. Bindings can make later rotations harder. External events can alter the reference field entirely. A previously invisible relation can become obvious after consequences appear.

VECTOR therefore requires a dynamic account.

The account must remain open. It is not a deterministic update law in which the next VECTOR state can be calculated from the previous one. The dynamics are better understood as recursive dependencies among orientation, praxis, temporal accumulation, binding, other actors, and external change.

[
\boxed{
VECTOR\ dynamics

\text{open recursive dependency, not deterministic control}.
}
]

8.1 VECTOR through Time

A VECTOR state is temporally indexed because the conditions of orientation can change.

Between (t) and (t+1), there may be changes in:

  • evidence;
  • reference or frame;
  • perspectival position;
  • available ROT;
  • effective DIST;
  • binding;
  • reversibility;
  • external constraints;
  • the praxis field itself.

Thus:

\[ V_t\neq V_{t+1} \]

is not automatically evidence of contradiction or analytic failure.

A later orientation can legitimately differ because the object has changed, because new evidence has appeared, because a previously hidden effect has become visible, or because the actor's own praxis has altered the field.

This is also why temporal indexing must remain distinct from PMS temporality:

\[ t\neq\Theta. \]

The index marks the evaluation position. (\Theta) is part of the temporally structuring praxis that can make one indexed state differ from another.

The dynamic question is therefore not whether VECTOR should produce one stable answer forever. It is whether changes in the answer remain traceable to changes in relevant conditions.

8.2 ROT Can Change DIR

The most direct interaction is:

\[ DIR_t \xrightarrow{ROT} DIR'_t. \]

A rotation can strengthen, preserve, weaken, reverse, split, or collapse a directional claim.

This relation clarifies the analytical priority established earlier:

\[ \boxed{ DIR\text{ is prior to ROT in analytic sequence, not in authority.} } \]

A DIR candidate is needed before its robustness can be tested, but the candidate possesses no privilege merely because it came first. A serious ROT can eliminate it entirely.

The same rotation may also generate a new relation. If so, the result should not be forced back into the original DIR. The new relation enters as a new claim candidate with its own burden.

The dynamic architecture therefore allows both correction and expansion without treating either as automatic confirmation.

8.3 Source Conditions Change the Practical Availability of ROT

ROT is conceptually available whenever the analyst can formulate an admissible change of view. But a real actor or institution may not retain the practical capacity to instantiate the corresponding reorientation.

This produces an important distinction:

\[ \boxed{ ROT_{conceivable} \neq ROT_{practically\ available}. } \]

Where the review window remains open, binding is low, public reliance is limited, and reversibility remains comparatively high, additional examination may still occur before enactment. A role rotation can lead to procedural change. A rival model can still influence the decision. A new frame can be tested cheaply. Such a configuration may be summarized as high derived DIST.

Where binding, public reliance, path loading, already-borne cost, or reduced reversibility narrow the field, the same intellectual moves may remain conceivable while becoming practically late. A policy is already implemented. The public commitment has created dependencies. The affected role has already borne the cost. The actor can understand another perspective but no longer use that understanding to preserve the option that the perspective would have recommended. Such a configuration may be summarized as low derived DIST.

The source conditions therefore determine not only enactment proximity but the practical relevance of future ROT; DIST can summarize the resulting separation but does not supply the causal explanation.

This is one reason exhaustive perspective taking is impossible as a general demand. Some rotations become visible only after the field has changed. Others are too costly or too late to instantiate. VECTOR seeks relevant and materially different pressure, not an impossible totality of viewpoints.

8.4 Stabilized DIR Can Alter the Praxis Conditions Summarized by DIST

The influence also runs in the opposite direction.

A directional claim that becomes more stable can increase readiness to act, commit, publish, or institutionalize. Those praxis moves can alter self-binding, publicness, temporal loading, option relations, dependencies, and available reversibility; the resulting narrower orientation–enactment separation may then be summarized as lower derived DIST.

One possible dependency path is:

\[ DIR_{stabilized} \rightarrow \text{commitment / publicness / binding changes} \rightarrow \text{narrower orientation--enactment separation}. \]

But the relation is not normatively monotonic. Stronger confidence is not always a reason to reduce distance.

ANTICIPATION supplies the crucial counterpressure. Apparent confirmation can produce closure drive. It can make further checking feel unnecessary exactly when confidence increases the cost of error or the likelihood that the orientation will be externalized onto others.

Thus a strengthened DIR can itself generate a new need to preserve or reopen correction conditions before further binding.

\[ \boxed{ \text{DIR stabilization can itself become a load condition.} } \]

This is a central recursive insight. A successful orientation is not exempt from the pressures that apply to a weak one. Success changes the praxis situation. It may increase the probability of binding, raise publicness, or narrow the perceived need for rival pressure. The stronger the claim becomes, the more consequential its transition into enactment may be.

VECTOR therefore treats confirmation as a possible reason for action and a possible reason for renewed stress, depending on the scene.

8.5 Binding Alters Later Orientation

Binding changes not only action possibilities but the conditions under which later orientation occurs.

Once a role is committed, alternative perspectives may become more costly to entertain. Institutional narratives may be built around the commitment. Evidence may be interpreted through the need to preserve continuity. A public position may create reputational pressure against revision. Other actors may organize around the commitment and thereby make the original frame more real in practice.

Thus:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Orientation does not merely precede binding; binding feeds back into orientation.} } \]

This feedback can be epistemically benign or problematic. A stable commitment can create information unavailable in abstraction. Enactment can reveal real consequences and improve later orientation. But binding can also produce selective legibility, sunk-cost effects, or institutional resistance to ROT (Rotation).

VECTOR does not psychologize these dynamics. The feedback can be structural even where no actor is consciously defending a prior belief. Procedures, dependencies, and coordination needs can make some orientations easier to sustain than others.

The important consequence is that orientation should not be modeled as an external observer repeatedly reading an unchanged field. The field partly changes through what the orientation helps enact.

8.6 Recursive and Self-Modifying Orientation

The minimal recursive movement can be written:

\[ \boxed{ V_t \rightarrow praxis_t \rightarrow conditions_{t+1} \rightarrow V_{t+1}. } \]

The expression is deliberately schematic. It says that orientation can influence praxis, praxis can change later conditions, and later orientation occurs under those changed conditions.

In this limited sense, orientation is self-modifying: not because VECTOR autonomously rewrites itself, but because oriented praxis helps produce the field from which future orientation becomes possible.

A successful role rotation may lead an institution to include an affected role in later decisions. That changed procedure alters the future perspective field. A strong DIR (Direction) may produce binding that reduces later reversibility. A failed orientation may create costs that make previously abstract consequences obvious. A newly visible relation may change what the actor now treats as relevant evidence.

The recursion is therefore not purely cognitive. It runs through praxis.

At this stage, VECTOR should still resist the stronger claim that orientation itself is always a praxis event. That proposition requires additional conditions and will be developed in Part V. Chapter 8 establishes only the necessary precursor: orientation can enter feedback relations in which its enactment modifies later orientation conditions.

8.7 VECTOR Dynamics Is Open, Not Closed

The recursive picture must not be mistaken for a closed system.

There is no general law:

\[ V_{t+1}=f(V_t). \]

Between two VECTOR states, the world can change for reasons not produced by the current actor or orientation. Other actors act. Material conditions shift. Institutions intervene. Evidence arrives. Non-events accumulate. New differences emerge. Chance matters. Previously unknown constraints become active.

A more honest schematic relation is:

\[ V_t \xrightarrow{\Theta+praxis+external\ change} V_{t+1}. \]

Even this is a dependency schema, not an equation of determination.

The open character of the system has a further implication:

\[ \boxed{ Actor \neq \text{author of the whole field}. } \]

This boundary will become essential in Part III. VECTOR will argue that actors can sometimes alter the option space itself rather than merely choose from a fixed menu. But the ability to transform part of a field must not become sovereignty over all future possibility.

The openness of VECTOR dynamics therefore provides the correct bridge to agency. Praxis matters because it can change later conditions. It does not matter because actors control all conditions.

Part II can now be condensed without reducing its distinctions.

DIR (Direction) makes a relation directional. ROT (Rotation) tests whether that direction depends on an unexamined viewing position and can also generate new relational candidates. The source conditions summarized by DIST (Distance) determine how much effective room remains between orientation and enactment. The Reversibility Gradient shows that present room is not the same as the correction budget left by the path already taken. VECTOR Dynamics shows that direction and perspective interact recursively with timing, binding, publicness, reversibility, option structure, and other praxis conditions while remaining open to other actors and external change.

Together, these components still do not tell an actor what to do.

They reveal something prior and more consequential: the field in which action becomes possible is temporally structured, unevenly reversible, perspective-sensitive, and partly altered by praxis itself.

The next problem is therefore not ranking but possibility.

\[ \boxed{ \text{What exactly becomes available, unavailable, or transformable as orientation changes?} } \]

That question opens Part III.

Part III — Possibility, Agency, and Consequence

Part II developed VECTOR as a bounded relational architecture of orientation. DIR (Direction) makes a structural relation directional without converting it into a total order. ROT (Rotation) exposes that direction to admissible perspective change and can both test and discover. Effective orientation–enactment separation remains visible through its source relations and may be summarized by derived DIST (Distance), while the Reversibility Gradient distinguishes current separation from the path-dependent correction budget left by prior praxis. VECTOR Dynamics then places these relations inside an open recursive field rather than a closed update system.

Part III asks what this changes in the field of possible praxis.

The question is not simply which option should be chosen. Before any such question can be stated responsibly, VECTOR must distinguish what is merely conceivable from what is visible, structurally available, effectively actionable, and viable under the conditions that define the current claim. It must also distinguish an option that is absent from the actor's current orientation from an option that does not actually exist for that actor under the relevant conditions. Once that distinction is in place, a second question appears: does agency consist only in selecting among available options, or can praxis sometimes alter the option field itself?

VECTOR answers the second question affirmatively, but only under a strict capacity boundary. Actors can sometimes create options, open blocked options, preserve windows, close alternatives, change sequences, or alter relations among options. Such second-order agency is real, but it is neither unlimited nor guaranteed. An imagined alternative is not yet a transformable one, and a collectively transformable field does not imply equal individual capacity.

The third problem follows immediately. Changing a possibility field also changes consequence relations. Options carry costs, non-action carries consequences, and the distribution of effect can diverge sharply from the distribution of choice. Some consequences are direct; others are delayed, externalized, relational, or only indirectly supported. Orientation can improve their legibility, but it can also be false while remaining practically powerful. A false orientation can alter the field in which later action occurs without becoming true.

Part III therefore develops a sequence:

\[ \boxed{ \text{possibility} \rightarrow \text{option status} \rightarrow \text{second-order agency} \rightarrow \text{consequence topology} \rightarrow \text{timing}. } \]

The sequence is not an optimization model. More options do not automatically mean more freedom, more agency, or better orientation. More visible consequences do not imply consequence omniscience. Greater imagination does not create capacity. The objective is narrower: to make the field in which action becomes possible sufficiently typed that later claims about responsibility, necessity, and non-neutrality do not rest on fictional options or fictional powers.

The governing boundary of the part is therefore:

\[ \boxed{ \text{constraint-bound agency} \neq \text{fatalism} \neq \text{possibilism}. } \]

9. Option Space, Branch Status, and Action Corridors

A theory of orientation becomes practically consequential only when it can say something disciplined about possibility. This is harder than listing alternatives.

In ordinary reasoning, statements such as there was another option, they could have done otherwise, we can still change course, or a third way must exist often carry substantial practical and normative weight. Yet such statements routinely collapse several different statuses into one. An alternative can be imaginable without being available. It can be available in the abstract while inaccessible to the relevant role. It can be visible but no longer actionable. It can be actionable but incompatible with a maintained constraint. It can have existed earlier but be lost now. It can also be a construction that becomes possible only because the analyst has removed the very condition that defines the original scene.

VECTOR therefore treats option status as a typed relation rather than a binary property.

The primary distinction is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{conceivable} \neq \text{visible} \neq \text{available} \neq \text{actionable} \neq \text{viable}. } \]

These categories are not steps in an automatic ladder. An option can be available but invisible. It can be visible but unavailable. It can be structurally available to one role but not actionable by another. It can be actionable and still fail a declared viability condition. The point is not to maximize the number of categories but to prevent later claims from borrowing strength across them.

9.1 The Praxeological Option Space

Let the relevant option field for actor or role (a) at time (t) be written schematically as:

\[ \boxed{ \mathcal O_{a,t}(O\mid F,Q). } \]

The notation does not denote the set of all metaphysically possible worlds. It denotes the bounded field of alternatives whose possibility, accessibility, or transformation is relevantly supportable for the declared reference, frame, question, actor or role, and temporal position.

This definition matters because there may be no single scene-global option set.

Suppose two roles participate in the same institutional situation. One role possesses formal authority to revise a decision, access to the relevant information, and a still-open procedural window. Another role bears the effects of the decision but lacks both revision authority and access to the decision process. The two roles inhabit the same nominal scene while possessing different effective option fields.

\[ \boxed{ \mathcal O_{A,t} \neq \mathcal O_{B,t} \quad\text{may hold within the same scene.} } \]

The difference is not necessarily psychological. It may arise from real distributions of access, means, authority, resources, exposure, information, or timing. MIP provides an adjacent precedent for this distinction by separating formal possibility from practical room for action and by refusing to evaluate enactment as though every actor occupied the same power position. LOGIC later makes the boundary sharper: potential is effective, frame-bound capacity, not abstract possibility.

The same temporal discipline applies to retrospective analysis. An analyst at analysis time \(t_a\) may reconstruct an option that was not legible to the actor at scene time \(t_s\), or may possess evidence that did not exist then. This can improve explanation of the past. It does not automatically establish that the actor possessed the same option field at the earlier time.

\[ \boxed{ \text{retrospective option legibility} \neq \text{retrospective effective capacity}. } \]

Option status and evidence status must therefore remain separate. A label such as

\[ Actionable_a(o,t) \]

is an option-status claim. The support for that claim is a second relation. VECTOR must permit, especially in retrospective analysis:

\[ \boxed{ Unknown(Actionable_a(o,t)). } \]

The same applies to availability and viability. An analyst's inability to establish a status does not convert it into the opposite status:

\[ \boxed{Unknown\neq Unavailable} \]
\[ \boxed{Unknown\neq Available.} \]

This separation is not agnosticism by default. It prevents syntax from making an option look more certain than the evidence allows.

The option field is therefore praxeological rather than merely logical. Its elements are conditioned by real relations of frame, role, time, access, capacity, and consequence.

This also means that an option field is not static. Praxis can change it. Other actors can change it. Time can change it. Material conditions can change it. A decision can close a corridor, an intervention can open one, and an event outside the actor's control can remove or create possibilities. The field is temporally indexed because possibility itself is temporally structured.

9.2 Conceivable

The weakest status is conceivable.

An option is conceivable when it can be meaningfully formulated, imagined, modeled, or constructed as an alternative. Conceivability is epistemically useful because it allows the analyst or actor to ask counterfactual questions that the current field does not answer directly.

A conceivable option may reveal a hidden constraint. It may show that a particular institutional rule, resource absence, role distribution, or temporal condition is doing more work than the original orientation recognized. It may also provide a rival configuration against which the current field can be tested.

But conceivable possibility is not effective possibility.

\[ \boxed{ Conceivable(o) \not\Rightarrow Available(o). } \]

This distinction prevents two opposite errors.

The first is possibilist inflation: because an alternative can be stated, the analyst treats it as though the actor could actually enact it. The second is anti-counterfactual rigidity: because an alternative was not real in the original scene, the analyst treats the construction as analytically useless.

VECTOR rejects both.

A hypothetical alternative may be invalid as an option and still valid as a probe.

\[ \boxed{ \text{invalid as option} \not\Rightarrow \text{invalid as probe}. } \]

This is one of the most important boundaries in Part III. Counterfactual construction can improve orientation without altering the real option field. The difference between a hypothetical case and the actual case can itself reveal which condition blocks, enables, or transforms the relevant possibility.

Conceivability therefore belongs to the architecture of orientation, but it does not carry practical availability by itself.

9.3 Visible

An option is visible when it is legible as a relevant possibility from the declared orientation or perspectival position.

Write:

\[ Visible_a(o,t). \]

Visibility is relational. An option can be visible to one actor, role, or analyst and invisible to another. It can also become visible after ROT (Rotation) changes the viewing position, after new information arrives, or after the frame is reconstructed at a more useful granularity.

Two boundaries follow immediately:

\[ Visible(o) \not\Rightarrow Real(o), \]

and:

\[ \neg Visible(o) \not\Rightarrow \neg Real(o). \]

The first blocks one route into Option Illusion: visibility cannot by itself license inclusion in the real or effective option field. The second blocks one route into Option Blindness: invisibility cannot by itself license exclusion from that field.

The two boundaries are related but not type-identical. An option can be invisible without any status error having yet occurred. Blindness begins only when invisibility, uncertainty, blockage, or another weaker status is treated as sufficient warrant for a stronger negative option-status claim than the evidence supports. Chapter 9.7 will therefore distinguish Option Invisibility as a legibility condition from Option Blindness as an exclusion error.

Visibility therefore concerns legibility, not existence. A person can vividly see a route that does not actually exist for them. Conversely, a viable path can remain structurally available while absent from the current orientation because the actor lacks information, occupies a narrow role position, or has learned a frame in which the route does not register as a possibility.

ROT (Rotation) becomes practically important at this point. A role rotation can expose an option visible from an affected position but absent from the decision-maker's orientation. A granularity rotation can reveal a procedural step hidden by an overly coarse framing. A rival-model rotation can make a transformation path legible that the original framework did not represent well.

But the epistemic gain must remain typed:

\[ \boxed{ ROT\text{ can expand option legibility without creating the option.} } \]

The option may have been there all along. Or it may still not be real. Visibility is only one status.

9.4 Structurally Available

An option is structurally available when, under the relevant frame and temporal conditions, a real path exists by which the relevant actor or role could enter that alternative.

Write schematically:

\[ Available_a(o,t\mid F). \]

Structural availability requires more than logical consistency. It depends on actual relations such as access, institutional permission, resources, other actors, physical conditions, existing bindings, and temporal windows.

An option can be costly and still available. It can be risky and still available. It can involve conflict, sacrifice, or substantial reversal cost and remain part of the real field. Availability is therefore not a quality judgment.

\[ \boxed{ Available(o) \neq Good(o). } \]

Nor is availability the same as visibility. An institution may contain a review mechanism that the current decision-maker has forgotten. A technical workaround may exist but be unknown to the project team. A role may legally possess an exit right that is not salient inside the current coordination frame. In each case, the option is structurally available while remaining poorly legible.

The opposite case is equally important. An actor may believe an option is available because they can describe it, while a closed procedural window, absent resource, unavailable authority, or dependency on another actor blocks the route in practice.

That case will become Option Illusion.

9.5 Effectively Actionable

Structural availability is still not enough.

An option is effectively actionable for actor or role (a) when the actor possesses the situated capacity required to initiate or realize the relevant path under the current conditions.

Write:

\[ Actionable_a(o,t). \]

Effective actionability may require:

  • access to the relevant means;
  • sufficient information;
  • actual decision authority or legitimate procedural access;
  • material resources;
  • cooperation from other actors where cooperation is constitutive;
  • an open temporal window;
  • sufficient control over the relevant part of the field.

These conditions are evidence targets, not a checklist that proves actionability by completion. A material actionability claim should state, where relevant, what supports the actor's authority, access, means or resources, information, temporal window, dependencies on other actors, practical control, relevant cost, and procedural permission. If those relations cannot be established, the status may remain unknown rather than being inferred from analyst imagination or institutional possibility.

LOGIC provides the strongest existing PMS boundary here. Potential is not abstract possibility. It is effective capacity to produce change within a frame. Where means, access, authority, or the relevant temporal window are absent, potential is absent in the responsibility-relevant sense.

VECTOR adopts the same capacity discipline without yet making the responsibility move.

\[ \boxed{ Available(o) \neq Actionable_a(o). } \]

A policy option may be structurally available to an institution while not actionable by a particular employee. A technical intervention may exist while the actor lacks access to the system required to implement it. A collective could change a process while no individual member can do so alone.

This distinction is critical because later normative claims can otherwise rest on a fiction of capacity. Statements such as they could have done otherwise often smuggle institutional or collective possibility into an individual attribution.

VECTOR requires the relation to be typed:

Who could do what, when, through which actual capacity?

9.6 Structurally Viable

An option can be real and actionable while still failing the conditions that define the relevant continuing praxis problem.

VECTOR therefore distinguishes structural viability.

Write:

\[ Viable_a(o,t\mid G,Q), \]

where (G) denotes a declared operative condition whose continuation matters for the current question.

An option is viable when it can be carried as part of a continuing trajectory under the relevant conditions of the claim. Viability does not mean morally right, desirable, or optimal. It is conditional.

For example, a role may possess the actionable option to leave an institution immediately. If the current question is whether a critical infrastructure function can continue without downstream collapse, immediate exit may be actionable but not viable for the maintained condition (G). The actor can still reject (G); that possibility becomes a different branch with its own consequences. Viability therefore does not convert the operative condition into moral legitimacy.

\[ \boxed{ Viable(o\mid G) \neq MorallyGood(o). } \]

The concept becomes important later in the Ought typology, but Chapter 9 needs only the option-status distinction: an option can be conceivable, visible, available, and actionable while failing the specific continuity or structural condition that defines the current problem.

This allows VECTOR to describe constrained fields without pretending that every actionable branch is equivalent relative to the question being asked.

9.7 Option Invisibility

Option Invisibility occurs when a relevantly real or transformably reachable alternative is absent from the current orientation.

A simple case is:

\[ Available_a(o,t) \land \neg Visible_a(o,t). \]

But the category can also include alternatives that are not presently available but are realistically reachable through existing second-order capacity. In such cases, the invisible possibility is not a current first-order option but a transformable one.

Option Invisibility can arise through several structural mechanisms:

  • role-bounded access to information;
  • learned legibility that naturalizes the current field;
  • overly coarse granularity;
  • a frame that excludes a relevant actor or relation;
  • asymmetry in who sees which consequences or affordances;
  • temporal compression that makes a route practically disappear from attention before it disappears structurally;
  • attractor stabilization that makes deviation cease to appear as a real path.

The concept should not be psychologized. VECTOR does not need to claim that the actor is irrational, defensive, or unaware in a clinical sense. It needs only to show that the option's real status and its legibility diverge.

Option Invisibility is therefore a condition of legibility, not by itself an option-status error.

\[ \boxed{ OptionInvisibility \neq OptionBlindness. } \]

An actor, role, or analyst may genuinely fail to see an option without that fact alone establishing mistake, avoidability, responsibility, or any stronger negative claim about the option itself. The category records a divergence between legibility and relevant possibility. Whether that divergence is itself attributable, correctable, or practically consequential requires additional conditions.

ROT (Rotation) can correct this divergence. A new role position can make an option visible. A rival model can reveal a path the original model did not encode. A temporal reconstruction can show that a route existed earlier and was missed.

But the correction must preserve temporal discipline. If an option becomes visible only after it has become lost, the later insight does not retroactively restore its earlier visibility or actionability.

\[ \boxed{ \text{later discovery of an option} \neq \text{earlier effective possession of that option}. } \]

This rule will later matter for responsibility. Here it protects the option field from retrospective inflation.

9.7.1 Option Blindness

Option Blindness occurs when a relevantly real current option or transformably reachable alternative is represented as absent from the relevant possibility field without sufficient support for that exclusion.

The paradigmatic error is to treat invisibility as evidence of non-availability or genuine lack of alternatives:

\[ \boxed{ \neg Visible_a(o,t) \not\Rightarrow \neg Available_a(o,t). } \]

The category is broader than this single inference. Blindness can also occur when uncertainty is silently resolved into unavailability, when a blocked path is treated as nonexistent, when present non-actionability is inflated into non-transformability, or when a real distributed transformation is excluded because no single actor can realize it alone.

The governing error is therefore unsupported exclusion, not mere failure to see.

[
\boxed{
OptionBlindness

UnsupportedExclusion.
}
]

This formulation preserves the distinction between option status and evidence status. Unknown remains Unknown; it must not be rewritten as unavailable merely because the analyst cannot currently establish availability. Likewise, a blocked or presently non-actionable route may still matter to the reconstruction as a blocked path or as a transformably reachable possibility, depending on the relevant actor, role, frame, temporal window, dependencies, and effective capacity. Neither status may be silently promoted into present actionability, but neither may it be erased as non-existence without support.

Option Blindness is not a psychological diagnosis and does not by itself establish responsibility. The error may belong to an analyst, an institutional representation, a role-bound orientation, or a later reconstruction. A responsibility claim would require the additional attribution conditions developed later in the paper.

9.8 Option Illusion

Option Illusion is the positive counterpart to Option Blindness within option-field representation.

It occurs when an alternative is treated as part of the relevant effective option field although one or more conditions required for its practical existence are absent, altered, role-inaccessible, or imported from another frame.

The general structure is:

\[ \boxed{ Visible_a(o,t) \not\Rightarrow EffectiveOption_a(o,t). } \]

Several forms recur.

Frame illusion

An option is possible only after the analyst has removed or changed a constitutive condition of the original frame:

\[ o\in\mathcal O(F^*) \]

but:

\[ o\notin\mathcal O(F_0). \]

Treating (o) as though it belonged to \(F_0\) is frame laundering.

Capacity illusion

The option exists at system level, but the relevant actor lacks the means, authority, access, or temporal capacity to realize it.

Temporal illusion

The option was available at \(t_0\) or becomes available at \(t_2\), but it is treated as available at \(t_1\) when the decisive window is closed.

Role-transfer illusion

An option available to actor A is attributed to actor B simply because both occupy the same scene.

Relational illusion

The option appears available only because dependencies among options are ignored. Realizing \(o_i\) may destroy, require, or transform \(o_j\), making the isolated representation misleading.

Cost-blind illusion

A route is treated as viable because the real conditions required to produce it—resources, coordination, reversal, or transferred load—have disappeared from the frame.

Option Illusion is particularly dangerous because it often appears as creativity. The analyst can always construct a cleaner alternative if enough constraints are removed. VECTOR's task is not to suppress such construction but to type it correctly.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Option status must be earned under the relevant original conditions.} } \]

A better imagined world is not yet an available branch of the current one.

Option Blindness and Option Illusion can therefore be stated as opposite forms of Option-Field Misrepresentation:

\[ \boxed{ \begin{aligned} OptionBlindness &: \text{ unsupported exclusion from the relevant effective or transformable option field},\ OptionIllusion &: \text{ unsupported inclusion in the relevant effective option field}. \end{aligned} } \]

and, at the umbrella level:

[
\boxed{
OptionFieldMisrepresentation

UnsupportedExclusion
\lor
UnsupportedInclusion.
}
]

The umbrella does not turn option status into a binary real/unreal test. Conceivable, visible, available, actionable, viable, blocked, deferred, lost, transformably reachable, and evidentially unknown remain distinct relations. The misrepresentation occurs when the represented field assigns or denies a status more strongly than the actor-, role-, time-, frame-, dependency-, capacity-, and evidence-bound conditions support.

9.9 Special-Case Construction — Valid Probe, Invalid Option

The distinction between conceivable possibility and effective option becomes most useful in deliberate special-case construction.

Let the original frame be:

\[ F_0. \]

The analyst then constructs:

\[ F^*, \]

where one relevant condition has been changed or removed. Under (F*), a new alternative (o*) becomes possible.

Then:

\[ o *\in\mathcal O(F *) \]

may be true while:

\[ o^*\notin\mathcal O(F_0). \]

The construction can still be analytically powerful because the difference between \(F_0\) and \(F^*\) identifies what carries the constraint.

Suppose an organizational conflict appears to have only two viable responses. The analyst imagines a third response under a modified authority structure. The third response is not a real option if no actor can alter that authority structure in the relevant window. But the construction may show that authority distribution, rather than interpersonal disagreement, is the condition blocking the route. Orientation improves even though the option set does not.

\[ \boxed{ \text{A hypothetical option may increase orientation without increasing the real option set.} } \]

This is why special-case construction is neither automatically legitimate nor automatically invalid. It is legitimate as a probe when the altered condition is declared and the insight concerns the difference produced by that alteration. It becomes invalid when the result is silently transported back into the original frame as a real option or as same-claim robustness.

The distinction connects Part III directly to ROT:

\[ \boxed{ \text{orientation gain} \neq \text{option-status change}. } \]

9.10 Branch Status

Even after an option is recognized as real, its temporal status matters. Unrealized alternatives do not all occupy the same relation to the current trajectory.

VECTOR therefore distinguishes at least five branch statuses.

9.10.1 Realized

A realized option is one that has entered actual praxis.

Realization can change the status of other options, alter costs, harden bindings, or transform later corridors. It is therefore not merely a historical label. It marks a transition after which the field may be different.

9.10.2 Rejected

A rejected option is a real option that was sufficiently available to the relevant actor or decision process and was not selected.

The category should be used narrowly.

\[ \boxed{ \text{not chosen} \neq \text{rejected}. } \]

An alternative that was invisible, blocked, or unavailable should not be retrospectively described as rejected. Rejection presupposes that the option stood meaningfully within the relevant choice field.

9.10.3 Blocked

A blocked option is structurally identifiable but inaccessible because a real condition prevents entry into the path.

Blocking conditions can include missing authority, missing cooperation, institutional rules, resource constraints, closed access, or a binding that cannot be revised within the relevant window.

Blocked does not mean impossible in all worlds. It means not currently available under the declared conditions.

9.10.4 Deferred

A deferred option is one whose selection or rejection has been postponed.

Deferral is often treated as preservation. That inference is unsafe.

\[ \boxed{ \text{deferred} \neq \text{preserved}. } \]

Time can change the field during deferral. Another actor can act. A resource can disappear. A review window can close. Costs can accumulate. The option may therefore move from deferred to lost without any explicit rejection.

9.10.5 Lost

An option is lost when it was relevantly available at an earlier temporal position and is no longer available at the current one.

\[ Available_{t_0}(o) \land \neg Available_{t_1}(o). \]

Loss can result from an actor's own praxis, another actor's decision, external events, temporal closure, or accumulated binding. The category says nothing by itself about fault.

The distinction between rejection and loss becomes especially important later. An actor may bear consequences of a lost option without ever having rejected it. Conversely, a deliberately rejected option may remain technically available while no longer belonging to the current maintained trajectory.

Branch status is therefore historical and relational, not merely categorical.

9.11 Unrealized Alternatives Are Not Free Counterfactual Fiction

Praxeological analysis often becomes weakest precisely where hindsight becomes strongest.

After an outcome is known, alternative pathways proliferate. Analysts can identify interventions, negotiations, design choices, timing changes, or institutional mechanisms that appear superior to what actually occurred. Some of these counterfactuals are valuable. Others are retrospective fiction.

VECTOR requires unrealized alternatives to carry the same typing discipline as realized ones.

A serious unrealized alternative must be supportable as relevantly available or transformably reachable at the time and for the role to which it is attributed.

\[ \boxed{ \text{unrealized} \neq \text{imagined after the fact}. } \]

This rule does not forbid counterfactual analysis. It makes the claim narrower. A counterfactual can reveal a missed structural relation without establishing that a specific actor could have enacted the alternative. It can show what changed when a window closed without proving that the earlier actor knew or could use the window. It can identify a system-level possibility without converting that possibility into individual capacity.

The distinction protects both sides of the analysis. It prevents easy blame through hindsight, and it prevents fatalistic reconstruction in which the realized path is treated as the only path that ever existed. Where that exclusivity is asserted without sufficient option-status support, it is an Option Blindness risk rather than a mere absence of imagination.

A path can have been unrealized and real.

It can also have been imaginable and unreal.

VECTOR needs to know which.

9.12 Action Corridors

Options rarely matter as isolated nodes. Their significance lies partly in what they make reachable next.

VECTOR therefore uses action corridor to describe a bounded relational pattern of future reachability opened, preserved, narrowed, or closed by current conditions and praxis.

Schematic notation may be written:

\[ Corridor_t \subseteq \text{reachable future configurations}. \]

The term does not imply a fully enumerable decision tree. It marks a relational fact: an action can alter which later actions remain realistically reachable.

STRATA-PATH provides a strong methodological precedent. Temporal order can change the meaning of the same nominal event because adoption, objection, reconsideration, and reopening do not carry the same costs or accessibility relations in every sequence. A procedural objection before adoption may alter the decision directly; the same objection after adoption may require reopening, additional justification, or a higher-cost reversal path. The nominal act is similar while its corridor relation differs.

VECTOR generalizes the relevance of this observation beyond analytical path reconstruction. An action corridor can be altered by praxis itself.

A commitment may close several future routes while stabilizing one. A pilot program may preserve later reversal. A public announcement may increase coordination while raising the cost of reorientation. A delayed decision can preserve an option if the window is actively maintained, or destroy it if time continues to alter the field.

The corridor concept therefore links first-order choice to second-order consequence:

\[ select(o_t) \rightarrow \Delta\mathcal O_{t+1} \]

may occur even when the actor intended only the immediate choice.

This is one reason option count is a poor proxy for agency. Two fields may contain the same number of nominal alternatives while differing radically in what each branch makes possible later.

9.13 Shrinking and Expanding Possibility under Θ

Possibility changes through time even without a deliberate attempt to transform it.

\[ \boxed{ \mathcal O_t \neq \mathcal O_{t+1} } \]

is therefore normal.

Θ can structure shrinking possibility through closing windows, accumulated cost, hardened commitments, changed expectations, institutional deadlines, or irreversible effects. A route that was cheap to correct at \(t_0\) may remain technically possible at \(t_1\) while becoming prohibitively costly. Another route may disappear entirely.

But VECTOR must not turn temporality into a theory of inevitable option decay. Time can also expand possibility. New information can arrive. Other actors can cooperate. Resources can become available. A legal or technical change can open paths. A previously blocked relation can become actionable.

The correct generalization is therefore not:

\[ \Theta \Rightarrow |\mathcal O|\downarrow. \]

It is:

temporal movement can change the status, relation, cost, and reachability of options.

This yields a final boundary for Chapter 9:

\[ \boxed{ \text{non-selection} \neq \text{option preservation}. } \]

To do nothing can preserve a branch, but only where the conditions that sustain the branch actually remain. In many fields, time itself is sufficient to change the possibility structure.

Chapter 9 has therefore moved from a simple choice set to a temporally indexed and role-sensitive field. The next question is whether actors merely inhabit that field or can sometimes alter it deliberately.

10. Option-Space Transformation and Second-Order Agency

Chapter 9 treated the option field as temporally indexed, role-sensitive, and structurally conditioned. That account is still incomplete if the actor is represented only as a selector among options that already exist.

Praxis can sometimes alter the field itself.

This claim requires careful calibration. If stated too weakly, agency collapses into navigation through a fixed tree. The actor becomes a chooser inside conditions they never affect. If stated too strongly, every constraint becomes provisional in principle and every tragic field becomes evidence of insufficient creativity. Both errors distort praxis.

VECTOR therefore distinguishes first-order agency from second-order agency.

First-order agency concerns selection among currently effective alternatives. Second-order agency concerns effective capacity to alter the availability, accessibility, sequencing, temporal viability, or relations of relevant options.

\[ \boxed{ \text{second-order agency} \neq \text{sovereignty over possibility}. } \]

The distinction allows a more realistic middle position: actors are neither passive points moving through a naturalized option field nor sovereign authors of every condition that limits them.

10.1 Agency beyond Selection

A conventional choice representation begins with a set:

\[ \mathcal O_t={o_1,o_2,\ldots,o_n} \]

and asks which \(o_i\) the actor selects.

This is often useful. It is also often incomplete.

An actor can sometimes change the conditions under which later choice occurs. They can negotiate access, create a procedure, recruit another actor, preserve a review window, remove a blocking dependency, sequence decisions differently, or intentionally close an option in order to stabilize a trajectory.

The relevant movement is then not only:

\[ select(o_i\in\mathcal O_t), \]

but potentially:

\[ \mathcal O_t \rightarrow \mathcal O_{t+1}. \]

This transition can occur unintentionally as a consequence of first-order action. VECTOR uses second-order agency more narrowly for cases in which the actor or distributed actor configuration possesses effective capacity to alter the possibility field in a way relevant to the current praxis question.

Agency beyond selection is therefore not equivalent to creativity. A person may imagine many alternative worlds and possess no capacity to produce them. Conversely, a routine procedural act may exercise significant second-order agency by keeping a later branch open even though it appears uncreative.

The question is structural:

Which conditions of later possibility can this actor or actor configuration actually alter?

10.2 First-Order Agency — Selecting an Option

First-order agency remains necessary. VECTOR does not replace choice with field transformation.

Write schematically:

\[ select_a(o_i,t). \]

The actor chooses, refuses, defers, or otherwise responds within the effective option field available at the relevant time.

Even ordinary selection can have second-order effects. Choosing one supplier can create dependency. Signing one agreement can close another route. Publicly committing to a policy can raise the later cost of reversal. Refusing an intervention can allow an external process to close the relevant window.

But it is useful to keep the analytical focus distinct.

First-order agency asks:

Which currently available branch enters praxis?

Second-order agency asks:

Which conditions governing later branches can be altered?

A single enactment can instantiate both, but the questions remain different. This distinction becomes important when attribution is later considered. An actor may have capacity to select among current options without capacity to redesign the field. Conversely, an actor may lack authority to choose the final outcome while possessing substantial capacity to alter the procedure through which outcomes become available.

The difference prevents a common overclaim: if an actor cannot produce the ideal option, it does not follow that they possess no agency. Their real agency may concern preservation, sequencing, access, or relation change rather than final selection.

10.3 Second-Order Agency — Altering the Option Field

Second-order agency can be defined as:

effective capacity to alter the availability, accessibility, temporal viability, or relations of relevant options.

The word effective carries the same burden as in the option-status distinctions. A merely imaginable transformation is not second-order agency.

For a material second-order-agency claim, effectiveness requires a traceable transformation mechanism. The claim should identify, at the level required by the question:

  1. the actor or distributed actor configuration;
  2. the target change in the option field;
  3. the mechanism by which the change would be produced;
  4. the effective authority or capacity required;
  5. dependencies on other roles or conditions;
  6. the relevant temporal window;
  7. the evidence supporting those relations; and
  8. the resulting change in option nodes and/or relations.

A compact audit form is therefore:

TXT
TransformationClaim
├── actor / actor configuration
├── target change
├── mechanism
├── effective capacity / authority
├── dependencies
├── temporal window
├── evidence
└── ΔN and/or ΔR_O

The record is a proposed audit device, not a new VECTOR component. Its governing boundary is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{conceivable field transformation} \neq \text{effective second-order agency}. } \]

The transformed field may involve a change in option nodes:

\[ N_t \rightarrow N_{t+1}, \]

or a change in the relations among existing options:

\[ R_{O,t} \rightarrow R_{O,t+1}. \]

The second form is particularly important because option-space transformation does not require increasing the number of options. A sequence change can make two previously exclusive options compatible. A new dependency can make two previously independent options mutually constraining. An institutional rule can convert a reversible branch into a one-way commitment without adding or removing any nominal option.

This gives second-order agency a more precise target than “creating possibilities.” It concerns the architecture of effective possibility.

The actor's capacity may still be partial. A project lead may be able to preserve a review window but not change the legal constraint that defines the project. A group may be able to redistribute tasks but not alter an external deadline. An institution may change a procedure but not reverse effects already produced outside it.

Second-order agency is therefore always bounded by the part of the field the actor can actually modify.

10.4 Creating Options

The strongest form of option-space transformation is option creation.

A newly created option satisfies a relation such as:

\[ \neg Available_{t_0}(o_{new}) \]

and, after a real transformation:

\[ Available_{t_1}(o_{new}). \]

The critical word is real.

An option is not created because the analyst imagines it. It is created when praxis changes conditions such that a previously unavailable path becomes effectively available.

Examples include:

  • establishing a new procedural route;
  • developing a technical workaround;
  • obtaining resources that make an intervention possible;
  • recruiting cooperation required for a collective action;
  • changing a contract so that a previously excluded sequence becomes admissible;
  • constructing a temporary bridge that preserves later choice.

Option creation can therefore require multiple prior enactments. It may itself be costly, slow, uncertain, or dependent on other actors.

The boundary to Special-Case Construction is strict:

\[ \boxed{ \text{option creation} \neq \text{analytical construction of a case in which the option exists}. } \]

A counterfactual may identify what would have to change. Second-order agency exists only if the relevant actor configuration can actually produce enough of that change within the relevant window.

10.5 Opening Blocked Options

Not every newly accessible option is newly created.
