  • explicit treatment of scope-specific non-capture;
  • new external evidence where the enlarged claim depends on facts outside VECTOR.

The rule can be stated simply:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Convergence does not scale for free.} } \]

The existence of an ecosystem-level resultant would therefore not authorize a universal praxeological rank. It would establish, at most, a bounded meta-direction across the tested structures and under the conditions that survived.

This closes Chapter 13 with a deliberately non-triumphal result.

VECTOR can reconstruct convergent orientation. It can also discover dependence, multidirectionality, incomparability, or absence of a robust resultant. The test is successful when it discriminates among these possibilities—not when it always returns convergence.

That prepares the more difficult question.

Even if a convergence claim is responsibly built, what happens when the same pressure logic is applied to a position one already holds?

14. Bounded Test-Burden Closure

Chapter 13 tested relations among local directional claims. Chapter 14 turns inward.

The object is now a particular position:

\[ S_0. \]

The question is not whether the analyst can produce arguments for \(S_0\). Nor is it whether the position feels coherent after repeated reflection. The question is how far \(S_0\) can be exposed to materially relevant loss conditions before it is responsibly treated as robust enough for the current bounded claim.

VECTOR calls the primary technical status bounded test-burden closure. The older phrase near-final testing is retained here only as a secondary explanatory label for continuity with the earlier draft.

The status does not denote proximity to final truth. It denotes bounded closure of a currently declared test burden without closure of the object, the field, future evidence, rival space, or future revision.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Bounded Test-Burden Closure} \neq \text{final closure}. } \]

The architecture is designed to avoid two failures at once.

The first is premature closure: a coherent claim is treated as settled because known pressure has not yet been applied.

The second is permanent suspension: because finality is unavailable, no claim is ever allowed to become more robust than any other.

VECTOR rejects both.

14.1 Test-Burden Closure Does Not Mean Finality

Test-burden closure is procedural, not ontological.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Test-burden closure is procedural, not ontological.} } \]

A claim under bounded test-burden closure is not almost absolutely true. It has no hidden probability attached to it. VECTOR does not mean:

\[ P(S_0=true)=0.99. \]

It means something more bounded:

within the declared reference, frame, question, perspective, temporal position, and current material test set, no known claim-critical pressure remains unaddressed without being explicitly carried as residue.

That status can be lost tomorrow.

New evidence can arrive. A new actor can change the field. A previously invisible option can become legible. A rival model can emerge. An external fact can defeat a premise. A cost relation can become observable. A new Δ can alter the relevant scene.

Bounded test-burden closure therefore closes only the current declared test obligation.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Test-burden closure is closure of current test obligation, not closure of the object.} } \]

This is why bounded closure can coexist with non-finality. A theory does not need metaphysical closure in order to say that a particular claim has been tested enough to be provisionally carried at its current scope.

14.2 Declare the Reference, Frame, Question, and Baseline Claim

A self-test cannot be serious if the claim under test is allowed to move invisibly.

Before pressure begins, VECTOR therefore fixes the baseline:

\[ \boxed{ (O,F,Q,\pi,t,S_0). } \]

The reference (O), frame (F), question (Q), perspective (\pi), and temporal position (t) were introduced earlier as claim-bearing conditions. Chapter 14 adds \(S_0\): the actual proposition, directional relation, or bounded position being tested.

This requirement prevents one of the easiest forms of self-immunization.

Without a baseline, the analyst can respond to every objection by narrowing or reframing the claim and then retrospectively asserting that the revised version was what the original position meant all along.

VECTOR therefore requires:

\[ \boxed{ \text{A claim must be stable enough to lose before its robustness can be tested.} } \]

Stability does not mean immutability. Revision is allowed and often desirable. But revision must remain visible as revision.

A good baseline statement should therefore identify:

  • what is being claimed;
  • what comparison or direction is involved;
  • what scope is intended;
  • which conditions are explicitly excluded;
  • which external premises the claim depends on.

The stronger the claim, the more important this baseline becomes.

14.3 Declare Relevant Ω and Θ

Before perspective is rotated, the relevant asymmetry and temporality of the scene have to be declared.

Otherwise ROT can create false symmetry.

Ω matters because different roles may possess different capacity, authority, exposure, information, cost, and reversibility. Θ matters because the same nominal scene can occupy a different trajectory after commitments, delays, costs, or lost options have accumulated.

The self-test therefore asks at minimum:

  • Who can act?
  • Who is affected?
  • Who bears which cost?
  • Who controls relevant access or authority?
  • Which options have already been lost?
  • Which bindings already exist?
  • Which temporal windows are open or closing?

These conditions do not have to be known exhaustively. Where they are unknown, the correct representation is residue:

\[ \Lambda. \]

The important point is that relevant Ω and Θ are not allowed to disappear during rotation merely because a symmetric reconstruction is analytically convenient.

A later role rotation may show the scene differently. It cannot erase a real prior asymmetry or a cost already carried.

14.4 Establish the Current DIR

The baseline claim must then be inspected for direction.

What exactly is \(S_0\) asserting?

Some positions contain a mixture of descriptive, comparative, causal, and normative language. VECTOR requires the directional component to be isolated rather than smuggled through implication.

A current DIR may be written:

\[ X\prec_{V,Q}Y. \]

Or it may be a conditional relation:

\[ DIR(S_0\mid G). \]

If no directional relation exists, that itself is an important result. VECTOR should not manufacture DIR simply because Chapter 14 is a VECTOR test.

The self-test needs to know what would count as weakening, reversal, trade-off, incomparability, or collapse. Without an explicit DIR, those loss conditions remain vague.

This also establishes the proper relation between confidence and testability. A strongly held position is not necessarily strongly directional in VECTOR terms. Conversely, a carefully bounded directional claim may be robust even where the analyst remains personally uncertain.

14.5 Apply Relevant ROT Families

The first active pressure is ROT.

The requirement is not to apply every imaginable rotation. That would make testing infinite and often meaningless. The requirement is to apply the materially relevant rotations capable of changing the claim.

The positive method from Chapter 5 remains active:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Rotation selection follows claim vulnerability, not desired outcome.} } \]

The analyst should identify which dependency of \(S_0\) could materially fail, then choose a sufficiently reference-preserving and materially distinct ROT (Rotation) with a credible route to that loss. Repeated pressure on the same dependency does not become stronger merely by increasing the number of rotations.

Depending on \(S_0\), these may include:

  • role rotation;
  • actor/affected-role rotation;
  • inside/outside rotation;
  • intention/effect rotation;
  • temporal rotation;
  • Ω-position rotation;
  • counterfactual probe;
  • rival-model rotation;
  • granularity rotation.

The rotation contract remains active. The test should specify what changed, what was held constant, and whether the operation is robustness-bearing or primarily exploratory.

Friendly rotation is not enough.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Relevant ROT must retain claim-loss capacity.} } \]

At the same time, endless rotation is not required. A perspective that is merely different but not materially relevant to the claim adds little. ROT is a discriminative burden, not a ritual of viewpoint accumulation.

The self-test therefore asks:

Which perspective, if taken seriously, could most plausibly reveal that my current DIR depends on an unexamined position?

That question is often more valuable than applying many weak rotations.

14.6 Vary the Source Conditions of Orientation–Enactment Separation

ROT changes declared viewing conditions. A different test concerns the actual conditions that determine how much practical room remains before enactment, binding, or costly reversal. Because DIST is now a derived descriptor, the self-test must vary those source conditions, not DIST as an independent dial.

A valid probe therefore proceeds in five steps:

  1. identify the source relations that generate the current orientation–enactment separation;
  2. select a materially relevant dependency, such as a review window, public commitment, (Ψ) (self-binding), available reversibility, authority/capacity, dependency load, or already-borne cost;
  3. construct a declared counter-scene or probe that changes that dependency while stating what remains fixed;
  4. reconstruct the resulting practical revision/enactment relation;
  5. use DIST only as optional shorthand for comparing the resulting states.

Would the claim change if a review window were genuinely open rather than closing? Would it change if public commitment had already hardened the field? Would the same direction remain carryable if one additional materially distinct ROT (Rotation) were still cheap, but a full reframing process no longer fit the available window? Would lower binding or higher reversibility change what correction remains practically reachable?

This pressure is useful because many claims silently assume comfortable revision conditions. They appear robust only because the analyst imagines sufficient time, low binding, low public reliance, or easy reversibility to correct error later.

But analytical variation of source conditions does not create those conditions in the real scene.

\[ \boxed{ \text{analytically imagined source conditions} \neq \text{actual scene conditions}. } \]

A hypothetical long review window may expose what a claim depends on. It does not prove that the relevant actor possessed that window. Imagining compressed timing can expose brittleness under urgency; it does not prove that the actual case was urgent.

The test is therefore partly exploratory. Derived DIST may summarize the contrast between the probe and the actual scene, but the warrant remains in the declared source relations.

The useful question is:

Which parts of my orientation depend on having more or less real correction room before binding than the case actually provides?

14.7 Map the Option Field

The self-test next maps the relevant option field.

Chapter 9 already established the necessary distinctions. They are not redefined here.

The task is to identify which alternatives the current position assumes to be:

  • conceivable;
  • visible;
  • structurally available;
  • effectively actionable;
  • structurally viable;
  • blocked;
  • deferred;
  • lost.

This is especially important because many directional claims hide their strongest assumptions in the option set rather than in the comparison itself.

A position may appear dominant only because one competing option has been excluded from the frame. A policy may appear necessary only because a transformably reachable alternative has not been considered. A criticism may appear irresponsible only because the relevant actor's actual capacity has been understated. Conversely, a proposed alternative may appear attractive only because its material preconditions have not been tested.

The self-test therefore asks:

Which options must exist for \(S_0\) to remain plausible, and what is their actual status?

The mapping should remain role-sensitive. An option available to an institution may be unavailable to an individual. A path visible to an analyst at \(t_1\) may not have been visible or actionable to an actor at \(t_0\).

Thus:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Retrospective discovery of an option} \neq \text{retrospective capacity to enact it.} } \]

The purpose is not to enumerate every imaginable branch. It is to prevent a claim from gaining robustness by relying on an option field that has never itself been tested.

14.8 Test for Option Invisibility, Option Blindness, and Option Illusion

Option mapping is insufficient unless the self-test separates discovery pressure from the two directions of option-field status error. Option Invisibility is not itself an error. Option Blindness and Option Illusion are the negative and positive forms of Option-Field Misrepresentation established in Chapter 9.

Option invisibility

The discovery question is:

What relevantly real or transformably reachable alternative might my current orientation fail to see?

A role rotation, granularity shift, counter-scene, or rival model may expose an option that the baseline frame obscured.

If such an option becomes visible, the original DIR may need to be revised. A previously necessary-looking choice may become one among several. A trade-off may replace dominance. A claimed constraint may weaken.

But discovery does not by itself establish that the newly visible route was previously available, actionable, or transformably reachable for the relevant actor. The status still has to be earned.

Option blindness

The exclusion-side test asks:

Which alternatives, statuses, or transformation routes has the current analysis excluded, downgraded, or treated as absent without sufficient support from the declared frame, role, time, capacity, dependency, and evidence conditions?

The test should pressure at least the following invalid shortcuts where they are material:

\[ \neg Visible(o) \not\Rightarrow \neg Available(o), \]
\[ Unknown(o) \not\Rightarrow Unavailable(o), \]
\[ Blocked(o) \not\Rightarrow Nonexistent(o), \]

and:

\[ \neg Actionable_{now}(o) \not\Rightarrow \neg TransformablyReachable(o). \]

Blindness can therefore concern either an unsupported exclusion from the current effective option field or an unsupported exclusion from the realistically transformable field. These claims must remain distinct. A route that is not currently actionable does not thereby become a current option merely because it may be transformable, and a merely imaginable transformation does not defeat a bounded negative result.

Option illusion

The inclusion-side test asks:

Which alternatives in my current analysis exist only because I have changed the frame, imported later capacity, ignored cost, transferred another actor's authority, or treated a hypothetical construction as a real option?

The governing boundary remains:

\[ \boxed{ \text{orientation gain} \neq \text{automatic option-status change}. } \]

A counterfactual special case may reveal a dependency. It does not place the constructed alternative into the original option field.

\[ insight(F^*) \not\Rightarrow option(F_0). \]

The three pressures perform different work. Option Invisibility asks what the orientation may fail to see. Option Blindness asks whether the analysis excludes more possibility than the evidence warrants. Option Illusion asks whether it includes more effective possibility than the conditions warrant.

\[ \boxed{ \text{discover more} \neq \text{count more}. } \]

A robust position should therefore survive three questions:

What am I failing to see?

What am I excluding without sufficient warrant?

What am I pretending is available when it is not?

14.9 Test Real Option-Space Transformability

The next question concerns second-order agency.

A position may correctly map the current option field and still fail because it treats that field as fixed when relevant actors possess effective capacity to change it.

The self-test therefore asks whether the current nodes \(N_t\) or relations \(R_{O,t}\) can be changed in practice.

Relevant questions include:

  • Can a blocked option be opened?
  • Can a closing window be preserved?
  • Can a new sequence make two previously exclusive options compatible?
  • Can an institution change a rule that currently constrains the field?
  • Is the required capacity individual or distributed?
  • Can the relevant transformation occur inside the actual temporal window?
  • What resources, authority, cooperation, and costs would it require?

A claimed necessity weakens if a realistic transformation can expand the effective response field.

But VECTOR must equally permit the bounded negative result:

No currently evidenced effective transformation is available within the declared window, capacity field, and relevant dependency structure.

This result is stronger than no transformation has yet been imagined and weaker than an ontological claim that transformation is impossible. It should be supported by a traceable transformation audit: actor or distributed actor configuration, target change, mechanism, effective capacity or authority, dependencies, window, evidence, and the expected change in (N) and/or \(R_O\).

The bounded negative is not Option Blindness merely because it excludes a transformation. Blindness arises only where the exclusion outruns the audit or where uncertainty, invisibility, blockage, or current non-actionability is converted into a stronger negative status than the evidence supports.

This matters because second-order agency can become a powerful form of self-immunization. Every tragic or constrained field could otherwise be answered with the claim that some sufficiently creative transformation must exist.

That would turn agency into a metaphysical escape hatch.

The self-test instead preserves:

\[ \boxed{ second\text{-}order\ agency \neq sovereignty\ over\ possibility. } \]

If no actor or distributed configuration possesses sufficient capacity within the relevant window, the constrained field must be allowed to remain constrained.

14.10 Map the Consequence Topology

A position can survive option pressure and still fail by mislocating consequence.

Chapter 11 established the consequence topology \(\mathcal K_t\). The self-test now applies it.

The relevant questions include:

  • What direct costs follow from the position?
  • What costs follow from non-action?
  • Which opportunities are lost?
  • Which windows close?
  • Which bindings become more expensive to revise?
  • Which costs are externalized?
  • Which option relations change?
  • Which consequences are delayed or downstream?
  • Which consequences are observed or directly established?
  • Which depend on external support?
  • Which are traceably inferred?
  • Which are merely hypothetical?
  • Which remain unknown or residual (\Lambda) (non-event/remainder)?

One question is especially useful under Ω:

Which relevant costs of my position are not borne by me?

This question often reveals why an orientation that feels locally coherent appears very different from an affected-role position.

The self-test must also preserve the Chapter 11 boundary:

\[ \boxed{ orientation\ effect \neq orientation\ truth. } \]

A position may already have changed behavior, expectations, institutional procedure, or option visibility. Those real effects do not prove the position true. Conversely, discovering that a claim is false does not erase the costs it has already produced.

The consequence map should therefore distinguish practical effect from epistemic warrant.

It must also distinguish unknown consequence from invented consequence.

\[ \boxed{ unseen \neq zero, \qquad unseen \neq invented. } \]

A self-test is not allowed to save \(S_0\) by populating Λ with hypothetical hidden costs that conveniently favor the baseline claim.

14.11 Rivalize

ROT pressures a claim from different viewing conditions. Rivalization pressures the model of the claim itself.

A relevant rival is not simply a contrary opinion.

It is the strongest available alternative account capable of materially changing the current position.

The rival may contest:

  • the evidence;
  • the causal relation;
  • the frame;
  • the option status;
  • the cost topology;
  • the chosen comparison criteria;
  • the need for a directional claim at all.

The essential condition is that the rival can win.

\[ \boxed{ \text{A rival that cannot defeat the claim is not a serious rival test.} } \]

This does not require adopting the rival's assumptions. It requires giving those assumptions enough integrity to test whether they discriminate the object better.

A rival may produce several outcomes.

It may fail. It may reveal an omitted condition. It may show that VECTOR and the rival capture different aspects. It may force scope reduction. It may defeat one component while leaving another intact. It may make VECTOR redundant for the question.

All of these outcomes are legitimate.

The prohibited move is automatic absorption:

the rival is actually another instance of VECTOR, therefore VECTOR survives.

Translation can be analytically useful. It cannot substitute for defeat conditions.

Rivalization therefore keeps open one of the strongest possible results:

\[ Rival\succ_Q S_0. \]

If that relation is warranted, \(S_0\) must reduce.

14.12 Counter-Scene and Counter-Position

Rivals change the explanatory or conceptual account. Counter-scenes change the relevant configuration.

The distinction matters.

A counter-scene asks what happens when a materially relevant structural condition changes.

A counter-position asks what happens when another plausible position within the same scene is taken seriously.

These tests are especially useful for non-discrimination.

If \(S_0\) yields the same result under materially different configurations, the claim may be too general.

\[ \boxed{ framework\ stability \neq discrimination. } \]

Consider a theory that always interprets delay as irresponsibility. A counter-scene in which delay preserves the only low-cost correction path should change the result. If it does not, the theory is not sensitive to the distinction it claims to track.

Likewise, a theory that always favors reversibility should react to a scene where durable commitment is constitutive of reliable coordination. A theory that always favors more options should react to a scene where additional nominal options are illusory or where commitment requires closure.

The point of a counter-scene is therefore not to invent exotic exceptions. It is to ask whether the current claim changes where its own declared conditions change.

A counter-position performs a related function at the level of argument. It asks whether a coherent alternative stance inside the same field exposes a dependency the baseline had treated as neutral.

Both tests remain bounded by reference continuity. A radically different object may generate a useful new claim, but not a robustness result for the original object.

14.13 Test Provenance and Dependency

A claim can appear heavily supported because many reasons are listed for it.

But the reasons may depend on the same parent assumption.

Suppose:

\[ r_1,r_2,r_3,r_4 \leftarrow A. \]

If (A) fails, the apparent multiplicity of support can collapse at once.

The self-test therefore audits dependency, not just source count.

The guiding question is:

How many materially distinct burdens has this claim survived, rather than how many supporting statements can be produced for it?

This extends Convergence Provenance from Chapter 13 to the internal architecture of one claim.

The test should identify:

  • shared premises;
  • imported source assumptions;
  • claims that depend on the same empirical fact;
  • conclusions that inherit one untested option assumption;
  • normative implications that depend on one unresolved constraint;
  • apparent evidence that is merely restatement.

The central rule can be stated:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Dependency transmits vulnerability, not automatic warrant.} } \]

If a parent claim weakens, dependent child claims may have to weaken with it. The reverse does not hold automatically: several child claims do not strengthen the parent simply because they can all be derived from it.

This is also why source provenance and claim dependency must remain visible when a position is revised. Otherwise the analyst may remove one explicit premise while preserving several downstream conclusions that still rely on it implicitly.

14.14 Test Invariance, Variation, Emergence, Collapse, and Trade-Off

After the pressures have been applied, VECTOR needs more than a binary verdict.

The outcomes introduced for ROT now become diagnoses of the whole position.

Invariance

The claim-relevant core relation remains stable under materially relevant pressure.

This is bounded invariance, not metaphysical invariance.

Variation

The claim survives only with changed scope, strength, role relation, or temporal condition.

Variation may be legitimate. A genuinely role-relative or time-relative relation should vary when the relevant position changes.

Emergence

Pressure reveals a new relation not contained in \(S_0\).

The new relation becomes a candidate claim. It does not retroactively count as support for the original claim merely because it arose during the test.

Collapse

The baseline's core directional relation no longer holds.

Collapse is a legitimate self-test result.

Trade-Off

Multiple relevant directional relations survive and no non-compensatory single DIR is justified.

This diagnosis is especially important where the analyst entered the test expecting a single orientation.

The purpose of the classification is not to protect the claim from a negative result. It is to preserve the kind of change pressure actually produced.

A claim that changes scope has not survived unchanged. A claim that becomes multidirectional has not remained dominant. A new insight does not mean the baseline was robust.

That distinction leads directly to the central rule of the chapter.

14.15 Revision Is Not Robustness

Suppose pressure changes the position:

\[ S_0 \xrightarrow{pressure} S_1. \]

Suppose further that \(S_1\) is better: narrower, more accurate, more discriminating, less inflated, or more sensitive to consequence.

This is a successful revision.

It is not survival of \(S_0\).

[
\boxed{
S_0\rightarrow S_1

revision,
\quad
not\ survival.
}
]

This distinction is indispensable because a sophisticated reflective system can otherwise immunize itself by improving continuously while describing every improvement as confirmation.

The stronger rule is:

\[ \boxed{ Revision \neq robustness. } \]

And equally:

\[ \boxed{ Revision \neq failure. } \]

A position may improve precisely because its earlier form failed.

\[ \boxed{ S_0\rightarrow S_1 \text{ may improve orientation precisely because }S_0\text{ failed.} } \]

VECTOR should therefore preserve version lineage:

\[ S_0 \rightarrow S_1 \rightarrow S_2. \]

The record should make visible:

  • which claim was lost;
  • which condition caused the loss;
  • what was narrowed or added;
  • whether the revised claim then faced new pressure;
  • which residue remains.

This is not bureaucratic bookkeeping. It is what allows correction to remain correction rather than becoming retrospectively rewritten confidence.

The distinction also matters for the theory itself. If VECTOR changes after a strong critique, the new version may be better. That does not mean the criticized version passed the critique.

14.16 Preserve Λ

A successful self-test must not end by making uncertainty disappear.

After ROT, DIST variation, option mapping, transformability tests, consequence mapping, rivalization, counter-scenes, and dependency audit, relevant residue may remain.

\[ \boxed{ \Lambda_{remaining}. } \]

Examples include:

  • unknown downstream consequences;
  • uncertain capacity;
  • incomplete historical evidence;
  • an unresolved rival account;
  • unobserved role conditions;
  • genuine incomparability;
  • external facts not yet established.

The correct response is not to fill the residue with the most plausible continuation of the current theory.

\[ \boxed{ \text{A robust claim carries its relevant }\Lambda\text{; it does not erase it.} } \]

Residue can affect the result in different ways. Minor non-critical residue may remain compatible with Robust Orientation. Significant residue may reduce the result to Bounded Orientation. Claim-critical residue may require Suspend.

The important point is that (\Lambda) remains active in claim typing.

A theory that always converts unknowns into coherent extensions of itself does not preserve residue. It absorbs it.

14.17 Bounded Test-Burden Closure

Bounded test-burden closure requires a stopping condition.

Without one, the process becomes endless. Every ROT (Rotation) suggests another ROT. Every rival suggests another rival. Every unknown suggests another research task. The impossibility of final closure would become indistinguishable from inability to act on any claim.

VECTOR therefore uses bounded test-burden closure as the explicit stopping status for the current declared burden.

A claim may be considered saturated enough for its current scope where at least the following conditions have been addressed:

  1. the reference, frame, question, perspective, temporal position, and baseline claim are declared;
  2. relevant Ω and Θ conditions are visible or explicitly residual;
  3. the current DIR (Direction) is stated;
  4. materially relevant ROT (Rotation) families have been applied;
  5. the source conditions of orientation–enactment separation have been tested where relevant, with derived DIST used only as optional shorthand;
  6. the option field has been mapped at the necessary level;
  7. the option field has been pressured for invisibility, unsupported exclusion (Option Blindness), and unsupported inclusion (Option Illusion);
  8. real option-space transformability has been tested;
  9. the relevant consequence topology has been mapped;
  10. at least one serious rival has been allowed real winning conditions;
  11. relevant counter-scene or counter-position pressure has been applied;
  12. provenance and dependency have been audited;
  13. no known claim-critical untested question remains hidden;
  14. relevant Λ is preserved.

This list is not a checklist that mechanically validates a claim. The content and severity of each burden remain question-bound.

The theoretical point is the distinction:

\[ \boxed{ test\text{-}burden\ closure \neq truth\ closure. } \]

Bounded test-burden closure means that the currently declared test obligation has been sufficiently discharged to permit a bounded result.

It does not mean that all possible tests have been exhausted.

The stronger statement is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Finality is unavailable; bounded test-burden closure is not.} } \]

This gives VECTOR a principled middle position between dogmatic closure and permanent skepticism.

Closure is itself calibration-sensitive. The question is not whether an abstract number of tests has been completed, but whether known claim-critical vulnerabilities have received materially distinct pressure at the current scope. Remaining rotations may be recorded as redundant, irrelevant, dependent on unavailable external evidence, or belonging to a stronger new claim. Two conforming analyses can therefore disagree about closure where their threshold bases differ; such disagreement must be exposed as calibration divergence rather than hidden behind a common status label.

The test can stop without the world being finished.

14.18 Possible Results

The self-test ends by typing the result rather than producing a single confidence verdict.

The result classes are deliberately non-hierarchical in the sense that not every case should seek the first category. A correct Suspend can be more adequate than an inflated Robust Orientation. A correct Non-Capture can be more discriminating than a forced comparison.

14.18.1 Robust Orientation

A claim qualifies as Robust Orientation where its claim-relevant core survives the materially relevant current pressures without requiring material revision.

\[ \boxed{ RobustOrientation(S_0\mid O,F,Q,\pi,t). } \]

The scope matters.

\[ \boxed{ robust\ within\ scope. } \]

Never robust simpliciter.

Residual uncertainty may remain, provided it is not claim-critical at the current scope.

14.18.2 Revised Robust Orientation

Where \(S_0\) materially fails but a revised claim \(S_1\) subsequently survives the relevant pressure, the correct result is:

\[ \boxed{ Revised\ Robust\ Orientation(S_1). } \]

This category preserves the distinction established in 14.15.

It gives credit to successful correction without rewriting history.

14.18.3 Bounded Orientation

A claim may be sufficiently directional for the declared question without earning a stronger robustness status.

Reasons may include:

  • limited evidence;
  • narrow scope;
  • unresolved but non-fatal rival pressure;
  • significant residue;
  • insufficiently diverse ROT;
  • external dependencies not yet established.

The result remains useful:

\[ \boxed{ BoundedOrientation(S\mid C). } \]

It says enough to orient without saying more than the current burden carries.

14.18.4 Revision Required

The current claim does not survive, but the test suggests that a materially altered version may.

This is not yet Revised Robust Orientation because no revised claim has completed the necessary pressure.

The correct result is:

\[ \boxed{ RevisionRequired(S_0). } \]

The distinction prevents an intended repair from being counted as a completed one.

14.18.5 Multidirectional / Trade-Off

Several relevant DIR relations survive, but no single non-compensatory resultant is justified.

\[ \boxed{ TradeOff_V(S). } \]

This result preserves structured conflict rather than treating it as failure to decide.

14.18.6 Incomparable

A meaningful comparison space exists, but the current conditions do not support a warranted dominance relation.

\[ \boxed{ X\parallel_VY. } \]

Incomparability does not imply equality. It means no justified rank has been earned within the declared comparison.

14.18.7 Suspend

Suspend applies where the question remains legitimate but current warrant is insufficient.

\[ \boxed{ Suspend_V(C). } \]

The claim may later be reopened when evidence, capacity, temporal conditions, or rival pressure change.

Suspend means:

not enough yet.

It is not the same as Non-Capture.

14.18.8 Non-Capture

Non-Capture applies where VECTOR should not produce the requested directional relation at all.

\[ \boxed{ \varnothing_V. } \]

This may occur because the reference does not remain coherent, the question is badly typed, the comparison would create an illegitimate person-worth rank, the relevant structure is not responsibly compressible, or the necessary claim conditions cannot be preserved.

Non-Capture means:

this is not an appropriate VECTOR operation here.

That is a positive discriminative outcome.

What the result set excludes

There is no VECTOR result named:

[
AbsoluteTruth,
]

[
CertifiedCorrect,
]

or:

[
FinalPosition.
]

The absence is architectural, not rhetorical.

14.19 Self-Application without Final Self-Arbitration

The test developed in Chapter 14 applies to VECTOR itself.

VECTOR can ask of its own claims:

  • Is DIR genuinely discriminative or merely renamed preference?
  • Does ROT do more than ordinary reframing?
  • Does DIST add explanatory value beyond existing PMS structures?
  • Are option distinctions calibratable?
  • Does second-order agency distinguish real field transformation from rhetorical possibility?
  • Does Cost Topology reveal structure or merely multiply categories?
  • Does convergence survive provenance audit?
  • Does the self-test produce real losses?
  • Does the Ought typology later add discrimination or merely new notation?

This self-application is required by the theory's own commitments.

But it cannot become final self-arbitration.

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR(VECTOR) \neq final\ validation\ of\ VECTOR. } \]

A theory can be internally consistent about its own boundaries and still be redundant, empirically wrong, undercalibrated, or inferior to a rival.

Self-application therefore has one legitimate direction: increased vulnerability.

If applying VECTOR to VECTOR can only generate reasons why VECTOR survives, the operation has failed.

The test must be able to produce:

  • narrower scope;
  • component reduction;
  • terminology reduction;
  • loss of standalone status;
  • domain non-capture.

Part VI will make these reduction conditions explicit.

This relation is continuous with the pressure established by PMS UNDER LOAD. Self-application is valuable where it creates more ways for the framework to become smaller. It is dangerous where it becomes a mechanism for internal certification.

The governing rule is therefore:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Self-application must preserve the possibility of self-reduction.} } \]

14.20 VECTOR Robustness Is Not External Warrant

The final boundary of Part IV concerns the relation between structural testing and facts outside the framework.

A claim can survive every relevant VECTOR pressure and still depend on evidence VECTOR does not provide.

An historical claim may require historical evidence. A medical claim may require medical knowledge. A technical claim may require measurements. A legal claim may require current law. A causal claim may require empirical data. A statement about another actor's capacity may require information not contained in the structural reconstruction.

VECTOR can test whether those dependencies are visible, whether the claim changes if they fail, whether the option and consequence relations have been typed correctly, and whether the inference exceeds its evidence.

It cannot manufacture the evidence itself.

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR\ robustness \neq external\ warrant. } \]

The strongest formulation is simple:

VECTOR can test the structure of a claim without supplying evidence the claim requires from outside VECTOR.

This distinction prevents a final form of framework inflation.

A beautifully structured orientation is not therefore true about the world. A coherent rival-sensitive model is not therefore empirically confirmed. A well-completed self-test is not a substitute for evidence. Formal tractability, provenance, calibration, and internal robustness can raise the quality of a claim while remaining insufficient for its external factual content.

The appropriate result may therefore be paradoxical only in appearance:

\[ \boxed{ \text{structurally robust} + \text{externally under-warranted}. } \]

In such a case, VECTOR should not weaken its structural analysis merely because the external evidence is incomplete. It should weaken the claim status that depends on the missing evidence.

The correct response may be Bounded Orientation or Suspend.

Part IV → Part V Attribution Gate

The practical transition requires one further boundary:

\[ \boxed{ StructuralRobustness \not\Rightarrow PracticalAttribution. } \]

This boundary applies especially where Part V claims that orientation itself changes the actor's later attribution conditions. A structurally robust VECTOR claim cannot produce that attribution increase if a material premise about actor capacity, consequence relation, option status, role position, maintained constraint, temporal condition, or another factual dependency remains externally under-warranted.

A candidate orientation-mediated attribution increase therefore depends, where relevant, on:

[
RobustOrientation
+ExternalPremiseSupport
+ActorUptake
+EffectiveCapacity
+ConsequenceRelation
+Role/TimeConditions.
]

For a constraint-level claim, it also depends on a genuinely operative maintained condition (G) and a correctly typed effective option field. This is a dependency boundary, not a deductive moral formula.

The distinction does not repeal the baseline responsibility logic of PMS-LOGIC (responsibility logic). LOGIC can attribute consequences where effective capacity and consequence relation are structurally present even if the actor did not consciously represent them. VECTOR adds a narrower second question: whether a later orientation-mediated uplift in structural attributability is warranted because the orientation has actually entered the actor's praxis situation. That additional claim requires uptake and the relevant support bridge.

Structural robustness alone cannot upgrade practical attribution where actor capacity, consequence relation, option status, role position, maintained constraint, or another material premise depends on missing external warrant.

This closes Part IV.

VECTOR has now established a way to distinguish recurrence from convergence, convergence from echo, robustness from revision, bounded test-burden closure from finality, self-application from self-arbitration, and structural robustness from external warrant.

The result is not final knowledge.

It is something more useful for the next problem: an orientation whose remaining limits are explicit enough that its practical consequences can be asked without pretending that uncertainty has disappeared.

That question changes the register of the paper.

Part IV has asked:

\[ \boxed{ \text{How robust is this orientation?} } \]

Part V asks:

\[ \boxed{ \text{What changes once sufficiently warranted orientation is actually available within an actor's praxis situation?} } \]

If relevant options, capacities, constraints, costs, and closing windows have become sufficiently supported, actor-accessible, and praxis-available under the required bridge, selection and non-selection may no longer occupy the same structural position they occupied before that orientation.

That is where the problem of necessity begins.

Part V — Orientation, Structural Attributability, and Praxis

Part IV ended with an orientation that may be robust without being final, internally well-tested without becoming external warrant, and under bounded test-burden closure without becoming immune to revision. That epistemic discipline creates the condition for the next question but does not answer it.

The next question is practical.

What changes once a sufficiently warranted orientation actually becomes actor-accessible, understood enough for contest or response, and praxis-available inside the relevant window?

Until this point, VECTOR has mostly asked what can responsibly be said about direction, perspective, derived orientation–enactment separation, reversibility, possibility, consequence, timing, and robustness. Part V asks what happens when these distinctions no longer remain only features of analysis. A relation may become actor-accessible regarding a real option, an illusory alternative, an externalized cost, a closing window, a maintained binding, effective capacity, or a constrained transformation field. But the analyst's formulation does not itself create this actor-relative state. Only where the relevant warrant, external-premise, uptake, capacity, consequence, and role/time conditions carry may later action and non-action occupy a differently attributable structural position because of the orientation.

The move is delicate because it approaches the boundary between structural reconstruction and normativity.

PMS-LOGIC (responsibility logic) already supplies an important constraint on how this boundary must be crossed. It reconstructs structural attributability under effective, frame-bound capacity, asymmetry, temporality, and consequence while refusing to derive a prescriptive ought. Crucially, LOGIC does not require conscious awareness as a general precondition of that baseline relation: consequences can remain structurally attributable where effective capacity is present even if the actor did not explicitly represent the relation. Its older language of loss of innocence of non-action names non-neutrality, not guilt or duty.

VECTOR preserves that firewall and must not rewrite it.

VECTOR adds a narrower second problem. Orientation can change which capacity, option, consequence, and constraint relations become available within the actor's own praxis situation. If orientation itself is claimed to increase attribution, the theory needs an additional bridge: the orientation must actually reach the actor in a form that can be understood, contested, verified, refused, or otherwise responded to. Thus baseline structural attributability and an orientation-mediated increase in structural attributability are different claims.

\[ \boxed{ BaselineAttributability_{LOGIC} \neq \Delta Attributability_{orientation}. } \]

Part V therefore distinguishes several different structures that everyday language often compresses into ought.

a categorical choice command is one thing;

accountability for what has become relevantly implicated is another;

constraint-relative practical necessity is another;

moral obligation is another again.

The central rule is:

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{choice} \neq OUGHT_{account} \neq OUGHT_{constraint} \neq OUGHT_{moral}. } \]

The OUGHT_* notation is retained deliberately because Part V is distinguishing several must-like relations that ordinary language compresses into ought. It is not a moral operator family:

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_*\neq moral\ operator\ by\ default. } \]

OUGHT_account and OUGHT_constraint are typed praxeological relations. Their notation does not import moral legitimacy, legal duty, or authority.

This distinction allows VECTOR to make a stronger practical claim without becoming a moral decision procedure.

The governing ceiling of Part V is therefore:

\[ \boxed{ \text{VECTOR may reconstruct how orientation changes attribution, constraint, and practical necessity.} } \]

It does not determine the morally correct action.

The tension can be stated more sharply:

\[ \boxed{ \text{No moral automation} \neq \text{no practical consequence}. } \]

Chapter 15 develops the consequence side of this claim. Chapter 16 then asks what kind of praxis stance can carry these consequences without converting orientation into certainty, heroism, person rank, or authority over others.

15. Orientation, Structural Attributability, and Constraint-Relative Necessity

The word ought is dangerous here because it carries several traditions at once: moral prescription, practical necessity, role expectation, institutional requirement, accountability, prudential advice, and ordinary-language pressure can all be expressed through the same modal vocabulary.

VECTOR therefore does not begin by asking whether an ought can be derived from structural description in general.

It asks a narrower question:

What kinds of must-like relations can become structurally reconstructable once orientation, effective capacity, consequence, temporal closure, and maintained constraints are jointly visible?

The answer requires more typing, not less.

A robust DIR (Direction) may show that one reconstruction carries the declared question better than another. That alone does not command a choice. An actor may possess effective capacity whose exercise or non-exercise produces attributable consequences. That can alter accountability without determining a morally correct outcome. A maintained commitment may restrict the remaining response field until some response becomes practically necessary for the commitment to continue. That necessity can be real even if the commitment itself is illegitimate or the remaining options are all bad.

These are structurally different situations.

VECTOR's contribution is to prevent them from being collapsed into one undifferentiated normativity claim.

15.1 Orientation Can Become an Actor-Relative Praxis Event

Earlier parts treated orientation primarily as a structured relation to praxis. Chapter 8 showed that orientation can enter recursive feedback with later conditions. Part III showed that orientation can change the visibility of options and consequences. Part IV showed that a sufficiently warranted orientation can survive serious pressure while remaining bounded and corrigible.

Part V now makes a stronger but explicitly conditional claim:

\[ \boxed{ \text{orientation can become an actor-relative praxis event}. } \]

An orientation written by an analyst is not yet such an event for the actor. The relevant uptake states must be distinguished:

\[ \boxed{ Orientation_{presented} \neq Orientation_{actor\text{-}accessible} \neq Orientation_{understood} \neq Orientation_{praxis\text{-}available} \neq Orientation_{enacted}. } \]

Presented means that a claim has been communicated or placed in the actor's environment. Actor-accessible means the actor can actually access it in a usable form. Understood means the relevant claim and its support burden are intelligible enough to be engaged. Praxis-available means the relation has entered the actor's practical question-space sufficiently to be contested, checked, rejected, accepted, refused, or acted upon. It does not mean agreement with the analyst. Enacted means that subsequent praxis actually carries some response to it.

The anti-abuse boundary is categorical:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Being told is not uptake.} } \]

More precisely:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Being told by an analyst} \not\Rightarrow \Delta Attributability_{orientation}. } \]

This qualification concerns the additional attribution claimed because orientation supposedly changed the actor's praxis situation. It does not erase PMS-LOGIC's separate baseline attribution of effective capacity and consequence where those relations are otherwise established.

A praxis event is relevant where a change becomes structurally consequential for the actor's subsequent relation to the field. Orientation can satisfy that condition only after the relevant uptake bridge exists. A role may then discover that a downstream cost is traceably produced by its current decision path, that an apparently open option is illusory, that a real intervention remains available, or that presumed authority is absent. In each case, the actor's later praxis can occur under a different relation to the field.

The relation can be represented schematically as:

\[ Orientation_{presented} \rightarrow? Uptake \rightarrow? \Delta Legibility \rightarrow? PraxisSituation_{t+1}. \]

The question marks are substantive. Access can fail. Understanding can fail. The premise can be rejected on good grounds. External evidence can remain missing. Timing can make the orientation practically irrelevant. The actor can understand and disagree. None of these branches is an uptake defect merely because VECTOR does not obtain its desired practical consequence.

This claim must preserve the Chapter 11 boundary:

\[ \boxed{ \text{orientation effect} \neq \text{orientation truth}. } \]

A false orientation can alter praxis. Practical effect therefore cannot validate the orientation that produced it. Conversely, a structurally robust orientation whose external factual premise remains under-warranted cannot use its own robustness to create an actor-level attribution increase.

VECTOR does not say that reality changes because it is seen. It says that the actor's structured relation to reality can change where a sufficiently supported orientation actually becomes available within subsequent praxis.

15.2 Orientation Changes the Actor's Praxis Situation

The most important consequence of orientation is not that an analyst has produced more information. It is that, after sufficient uptake, the actor may occupy a different relation to the same field.

At scene time \(t_s\), a role may lack actor-accessible legibility of a relevant option, consequence, capacity, or dependency. At a later scene time, after admissible rotation, new evidence, or reconstruction has become praxis-available, the external configuration may remain largely unchanged while the actor's relation to it changes. The visible option field may change; the actor's understanding of effective capacity may change; the consequence topology may become differently legible; the practical value of further reflection may change under the actual timing and binding conditions; and available reversibility may become differently understood. Derived DIST (Distance) may summarize a changed orientation–enactment relation, but it does not itself explain the change.

These changes matter only at the claim type they support. Analyst legibility at analysis time \(t_a\) does not retroactively become actor legibility at \(t_s\). Nor does an orientation that was merely presented become part of the actor's praxis situation by assertion.

The orientation-mediated transition can therefore be written more carefully as:

\[ SupportedOrientation +Uptake \rightarrow \Delta Legibility(\mathcal O_t,\mathcal K_t,C_{eff},G) \rightarrow? \Delta AttributionConditions. \]

The final arrow is conditional on effective capacity, a traceable consequence relation, relevant role and temporal conditions, and any external premises the attribution claim requires.

The important term remains conditions. VECTOR is not saying that responsibility has increased whenever orientation increases. Better orientation can show that the actor had less capacity than assumed, that the option was unavailable, that a cost relation was hypothetical, that another role held the relevant authority, or that a window had already closed. In such cases, improved orientation can reduce attribution.

\[ \boxed{ \text{more orientation} \not\Rightarrow \text{more structural attributability monotonically}. } \]

The actor may become more implicated, less implicated, differently implicated, or still insufficiently related for an orientation-mediated attribution claim. The significance of orientation is therefore discriminative: it can change the supportable attribution relation in either direction, but only after the bridge between analysis and actor praxis is itself warranted.

15.3 From Visibility to Effective Capacity

The move from legibility to attribution must pass through capacity. Part III already established:

\[ conceivable \neq visible \neq available \neq actionable \neq viable. \]

Part V does not redefine those statuses. It asks which of them are sufficiently evidenced to bear an attribution claim.

The first barrier is simple:

\[ \boxed{ visibility \neq effective\ capacity. } \]

An actor can see an option and still be unable to realize it. The missing condition may be access, authority, information, resources, cooperation, physical possibility, procedural permission, practical control, relevant cost, or time. PMS-LOGIC (responsibility logic) is particularly strict here: potential is situated, frame-bound, temporally effective capacity rather than abstract possibility.

A later analyst must therefore not reason:

the option is visible to us now → the option was available then → the actor could have acted → non-action is attributable.

Every arrow can fail. In retrospective work, the temporal distinction is explicit:

\[ \boxed{ Legibility_{analyst}(t_a) \not\Rightarrow Capacity_{actor}(t_s). } \]

The relevant capacity may already exist and become newly actor-accessible. It may be transformably reachable through a traceable second-order-agency mechanism. Or it may be absent. A transformation imagined by the analyst is not capacity unless the actor or distributed actor configuration had the required mechanism, authority, dependencies, resources, and window.

These possibilities can be described without turning them into a rigid new taxonomy:

  • existing effective capacity;
  • newly actor-legible existing capacity;
  • transformably reachable capacity with an evidenced mechanism;
  • absent, insufficient, or unknown capacity.

Unknown is a legitimate status. It is not permission to infer capacity from institutional possibility or to infer incapacity from missing evidence.

This prevents retroactive responsibility inflation. It also prevents the opposite error: where effective capacity really is established at the relevant scene time, later analysis may not protect non-action merely by redescribing that capacity as hypothetical.

The attribution question therefore remains structurally demanding:

What effective capacity existed, for whom, under which frame, at which scene time, through which actual mechanism, and with which evidential support?

Only after that burden is met can VECTOR ask whether orientation itself has changed structural attributability.

15.4 Structural Attributability — “Loss of Structural Innocence” as Shorthand

PMS-LOGIC (responsibility logic) uses the language of loss of innocence of non-action for a specific structural transition. Once effective, frame-bound potential exists under (\Omega) (asymmetry) and (\Theta) (temporality), action and non-action may both produce consequences that remain structurally attributable. LOGIC explicitly introduces no prescriptive moral demand, and the baseline relation does not depend on the actor first becoming consciously aware of it.

VECTOR should therefore use a more technical primary term for its own additional claim:

\[ \boxed{ \text{increased structural attributability}. } \]

The orientation-specific question is whether a sufficiently warranted and externally supported relation has actually become actor-accessible, understood, and praxis-available such that subsequent praxis occurs under changed attribution conditions. Where that bridge is established, orientation can contribute to an orientation-mediated increase in structural attributability.

This must not be confused with merely telling the actor what the analyst believes. Nor does praxis-available mean agreement. An actor may understand the orientation, understand its warrant, contest it, reject it, or refuse the response while the relevant consequence relation remains open to analysis.

The older phrase loss of structural innocence is retained only as secondary shorthand and as lineage language connecting VECTOR to LOGIC. Its scope is narrow:

a relevant capacity, consequence, option, or constraint relation can no longer be reconstructed as absent from the actor's praxis situation where the required warrant, uptake, capacity, consequence, role, and temporal conditions actually carry.

The firewall is explicit:

\[ \boxed{ loss\ of\ structural\ innocence \neq guilt \neq blame \neq liability \neq moral\ fault. } \]

And:

\[ \boxed{ increased\ structural\ attributability \neq increased\ authority. } \]

The relation can also decrease. Better orientation may show that a presumed option was not actionable, that capacity belonged to another role, that an external premise was unsupported, or that the temporal window had already closed. VECTOR must record those reductions rather than treating orientation as an accusation ratchet.

The technical primary claim is therefore not more knowledge means more guilt. It is:

Under sufficient warrant, uptake, capacity, consequence, role/time, and external-premise conditions, more orientation can increase structural attributability without increasing authority.

In the paper's secondary shorthand: more orientation can mean less structural innocence.

15.5 OUGHT_choice — What Does Not Automatically Follow

The strongest and most familiar form of ought can be written as:

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{choice}(X)=\text{Choose }X. } \]

This is a prescriptive outcome claim.

VECTOR does not derive it automatically.

Not from DIR:

\[ X\prec_{V,C}Y \not\Rightarrow OUGHT_{choice}(Y). \]

Not from robust orientation:

\[ RobustOrientation(Y) \not\Rightarrow OUGHT_{choice}(Y). \]

Not from effective capacity:

\[ Capacity_a(Y) \not\Rightarrow OUGHT_{choice}(Y). \]

Not from cost visibility:

\[ VisibleCost(X) \not\Rightarrow OUGHT_{choice}(\neg X). \]

And not from the existence of a maintained constraint unless the constraint actually reduces the effective response field accordingly.

Why is the firewall necessary?

Because the premises that make one structure more visible or one response more constrained do not settle every value, legitimacy, authority, or residual question involved in action.

A robustly oriented actor may still face multiple viable responses. A structurally superior reconstruction may not determine the morally preferred distribution of unavoidable losses. A response that preserves an institution may do so under an illegitimate institutional constraint. An actor may lack authority to impose the response on others. A tragic field may contain no good option. External legal, ethical, political, technical, or domain-specific premises may be necessary.

VECTOR therefore blocks the shortcut:

\[ \boxed{ robust\ orientation \not\Rightarrow categorical\ choice\ command. } \]

This negative boundary is not the end of Part V. It creates the room for weaker but still real practical claims.

The first is not a command to choose.

It is a requirement not to erase what has become relevant.

15.6 OUGHT_account — Accountability for What Has Become Relevant

OUGHT_account is VECTOR's first positive Ought type. It does not select an outcome and it does not replace baseline PMS-LOGIC (responsibility logic) attribution. It names a narrower orientation-mediated relation: what may no longer be erased from subsequent praxis because a sufficiently supported orientation has actually become praxis-available to the actor.

Write:

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{account}(R)= \text{R must remain accounted for in subsequent praxis.} } \]

The object (R) can be a relevant consequence relation, capacity relation, option relation, asymmetry, dependency, or other structure that has become sufficiently warranted, actor-accessible, understood, praxis-available, capacity-relevant, and consequence-relevant.

The actor may still choose the costly branch, reject the analyst's conclusion, judge another constraint more important, refuse to act, challenge the frame, revise the goal, or accept a tragic outcome. OUGHT_account decides none of these.

In this sense, account means neither obey nor solve. It means:

\[ \boxed{ \text{do not erase a sufficiently warranted and praxis-available relevant relation without claim loss}. } \]

The difference from description is praxeological. Description can establish that a relation exists. OUGHT_account adds that, where the orientation-mediated attribution gate is met, later analysis of the actor's praxis cannot reconstruct the relation as though it had never become available for response.

The category therefore cannot be triggered by mere publication, warning, confrontation, or exposure to information. Nor can the actor's disagreement be redescribed automatically as failed uptake. Understanding and rejection remain possible together.

This is why OUGHT_account needs a calibrated dependency threshold rather than a rhetorical moment of notification.

15.7 Accountability Has a Calibration Threshold

The dangerous version of OUGHT_account would be:

you have heard about a concern, therefore you are now responsible for it.

VECTOR explicitly rejects that rule.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Being told is not uptake.} } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ visible \neq accountable. } \]

For an orientation-mediated increase in structural attributability, a minimally plausible dependency structure is:

\[ \boxed{ RelevantOrientation +ExternalPremiseSupport +ActorUptake +EffectiveCapacity +ConsequenceRelation +Role/TimeConditions. } \]

The formula is not an algorithm and not a moral inference. It records dependencies that can each fail.

Relevant Orientation means the relation actually bears on the actor's role or praxis and is sufficiently warranted for the current claim. Its warrant sources and unresolved dependencies must remain visible.

External Premise Support means that empirical, legal, technical, historical, causal, capacity, or other factual premises required by the attribution claim are supported outside VECTOR where VECTOR cannot supply them. Structural robustness cannot substitute for that support.

Actor Uptake means more than presentation. The orientation must be sufficiently actor-accessible, understood, and praxis-available to be engaged. Uptake does not mean agreement, obedience, or acceptance of VECTOR's vocabulary.

Effective Capacity means the actor can actually do something relevant with respect to the relation, directly or through an evidenced second-order-agency mechanism.

Consequence Relation means action or non-action is structurally connected to outcomes in a way that makes attribution meaningful.

Role/Time Conditions preserve who possessed what relation at scene time \(t_s\). Analyst knowledge at \(t_a\) cannot silently create earlier actor legibility, authority, or capacity.

The threshold remains bounded and calibration-sensitive. VECTOR supplies no universal number such as:

\[ AccountabilityScore>0.73. \]

A low-stakes reversible decision and an irreversible person-near intervention can require different support thresholds. A technical domain may provide measurements; a legal setting may supply authority rules; another domain may leave the threshold genuinely contested. Two VECTOR-conform analyses can therefore remain calibration-divergent without one being malformed, provided the threshold disagreement is explicit.

The relation is falsifiable and non-monotonic:

\[ \boxed{ orientation\uparrow \not\Rightarrow structural\ attributability\uparrow. } \]

Better orientation can reveal that a supposed consequence was hypothetical, that the actor lacked capacity, that another role held authority, that the window had already closed, or that the external premise was unsupported. Improved orientation can therefore reduce attribution rather than increase it.

This is essential because otherwise orientation would become an accusation ratchet. OUGHT_account is not such a ratchet. It is a calibrated orientation-mediated account relation whose dependencies must remain independently defeasible.

Finally, none of this removes LOGIC's separate baseline attribution relation. Where effective capacity and attributable consequence are otherwise structurally established, baseline attribution need not wait for conscious uptake. Uptake is required for the additional claim that VECTOR orientation itself changed the actor's attribution conditions.

15.8 Shrinking Option Space

Accountability occurs in time.

The option field available when a relation first becomes visible may not remain the field available later.

Part III already established:

\[ \mathcal O_t \neq \mathcal O_{t+1} \]

and that deferral does not imply preservation.

Part V now asks what this means for practical necessity.

Suppose an actor at \(t_0\) possesses four effective response branches:

\[ \mathcal O_{t_0}^{effective}={A,B,C,D}. \]

Binding, delay, external action, resource loss, or temporal closure may later reduce the field:

\[ \mathcal O_{t_1}^{effective}={C,D}. \]

The earlier possibility of A or B does not make them present options at \(t_1\).

\[ \boxed{ logical\ possibility \neq remaining\ practical\ option. } \]

This matters because some situations that initially permitted broad discretion can become constraint-relative necessity structures later.

A role may once have had several ways to maintain a continuity condition. After a deadline, only one procedure remains. A system may once have had multiple carriers for an infrastructural load. After staff departure or resource loss, only a narrower response class remains. A relationship may once have had several low-cost repair paths. After public escalation, only costly routes remain.

The reduction of options does not establish moral legitimacy.

\[ \boxed{ shrinking\ options \neq legitimate\ obligation. } \]

It does, however, alter what can practically happen while some other condition continues to be held.

This is the bridge from OUGHT_account to OUGHT_constraint.

15.9 OUGHT_constraint — Constraint-Relative Practical Necessity

OUGHT_constraint is the strongest genuinely praxeological Ought type introduced by VECTOR.

Let (G) denote a maintained operative condition. (G) can be a self-binding, a role requirement, an infrastructure continuity condition, an externally imposed requirement, or another constraint whose operation is relevantly established.

Let \(R_G\) denote the set or class of effective responses compatible with maintaining (G) under the current field.

Then:

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{constraint}(G,R_G) } \]

means:

If (G) remains operative, subsequent praxis must fall within some still-effective response class \(R_G\) if (G) is to be maintained.

The force of the relation is conditional.

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{constraint} \text{ is conditional on the continued operation of }G. } \]

This is practical necessity, not categorical moral obligation.

If \(R_G\) contains several options, VECTOR does not determine which one must be selected. It only reconstructs the restricted response field.

If:

\[ R_G={X}, \]

then (X) is practically necessary for maintaining (G) under the current conditions.

But:

\[ \boxed{ required\ for\ G \neq morally\ required. } \]

A strong constraint claim also requires an explicit constraint assignment audit. At the level material to the claim, VECTOR should keep visible:

  1. who maintains (G);
  2. who can revise (G);
  3. who claims authority over (G);
  4. who bears the costs of maintaining (G);
  5. who possesses exit and under what conditions;
  6. which alternative carriers or mechanisms exist;
  7. which second-order transformation routes are actually evidenced;
  8. what follows if (G) is abandoned;
  9. what remains unknown; and
  10. which legitimacy question remains external to VECTOR.

These relations do not add a new VECTOR component. They prevent a system-level necessity from silently becoming a person-level assignment.

The distinction can be illustrated with a simple continuity case. Suppose an institution commits to keeping a critical service continuously available. Under normal conditions, several staffing and technical responses can satisfy that goal. A major outage and resource loss later reduce the field until only one emergency procedure remains capable of maintaining service within the relevant window.

VECTOR can reconstruct:

if continuity condition (G) remains operative, emergency response (X) is currently required.

It cannot infer from this alone:

the institution is morally justified in maintaining (G) at any cost;

the particular burdens imposed by (X) are legitimate;

a specific person is morally obligated to carry (X);

(X) is morally good.

Those are different claims.

The strength of OUGHT_constraint is exactly that it can name practical necessity without laundering the source or legitimacy of the constraint.

That distinction becomes the next requirement.

15.10 Constraint Source and Legitimacy Are Distinct

A real constraint is not necessarily a legitimate constraint.

This is not a secondary ethical caveat. It is a structural type boundary.

\[ \boxed{ operative\ constraint \neq legitimate\ constraint. } \]

A constraint may arise from several sources.

It may be self-bound: an actor has adopted and maintained a commitment.

It may be role-bound: a function or office carries an operative requirement.

It may be infrastructure-bound: system continuity depends on some function being performed.

It may be externally imposed: law, coercion, authority, dependency, or another actor constrains the field.

These sources have different normative meanings.

VECTOR's structural reconstruction must therefore keep at least two questions separate:

  1. Does (G) actually constrain the current option field?
  2. Is (G) legitimate, justified, authorized, desirable, or morally binding?

For person-level carriage or burden claims, two further questions are mandatory:

  1. Who maintains or can revise (G), and through what authority?
  2. Why should this bearer rather than another carrier, mechanism, exit, redesign, or transformation route be treated as practically assigned?

The first can often be reconstructed praxeologically.

The second may require normative, legal, political, institutional, or domain-specific premises outside VECTOR.

The dangerous inference is:

\[ G\text{ binds} \Rightarrow G\text{ ought to bind}. \]

VECTOR rejects it.

\[ \boxed{ G\text{ binds} \not\Rightarrow G\text{ ought to bind}. } \]

This protects the Ought typology from becoming a theory of obedience to existing structures.

An exploitative institution can impose real constraints. A coercive relationship can narrow actual options. An illegitimate rule can create practical necessity for avoidance, compliance, resistance, exit, concealment, or other responses. To say that the constraint is operative is not to endorse it.

Conversely, an actor may voluntarily maintain a legitimate commitment whose practical consequences are burdensome. Its legitimacy does not erase the costs or make every carrier assignment fair.

VECTOR therefore permits a structurally important combination:

\[ \boxed{ \text{real necessity} + \text{contested legitimacy}. } \]

This is especially important for carriage and fixpoint structures, where a system-level requirement can easily be converted into a person-level demand if constraint source and authority are not kept separate.

15.11 Carriage Is Not Always Optional

PMS-CONFLICT provides one of the strongest local precedents for practical necessity without moral prescription: infrastructure-coupled conflict: carriage is not optional.

The point is not that some person should nobly carry every burden.

It is that some systems contain loads whose existence does not depend on anyone's willingness to acknowledge them.

Infrastructure can include logistics, care, coordination, institutional continuity, operational reliability, procedural throughput, or other functions that must continue if the surrounding system is to continue.

When such a load exists, it can be:

  • carried;
  • transferred;
  • deferred;
  • externalized;
  • allowed to fail;
  • or transformed if effective second-order agency exists.

But it does not disappear merely because all actors prefer not to carry it.

This yields a system-level necessity relation.

It must not be converted into a person-level obligation.

\[ \boxed{ \text{system-level carriage necessity} \neq \text{person-level obligation}. } \]

This distinction is source-critical. PMS-CONFLICT explicitly refuses the claim that its structural analysis means anyone should carry. The theory describes load-bearing geometry, not virtue, sacrifice, or entitlement.

VECTOR preserves that reader guard.

Its contribution is to connect carriage to the already developed option, capacity, and consequence architecture.

Suppose a service requires one active carrier role during a crisis. The current system therefore contains a carriage requirement. But several questions remain open:

  • Which roles possess effective capacity?
  • Which assignment preserves dignity and relevant boundaries?
  • Who possesses legitimate authority to allocate the role?
  • Can the load be transformed, shared, automated, postponed, or reduced?
  • What costs does each possible assignment create?
  • What happens if no one carries it?

The existence of the system requirement does not answer these questions.

It only prevents one false description:

if no one voluntarily accepts the load, there is no longer a load.

Where the load remains, non-carriage often becomes carriage transfer.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Non-carriage may become a mode of carriage transfer.} } \]

If one role stops carrying a necessary function, another role may absorb it. The load may move into overtime, delayed maintenance, system failure, customer harm, future repair, unpaid care, or another institutional location. In some cases the system simply fails and the resulting cost is carried as failure consequence.

This does not make every refusal wrong.

It makes the transfer relation visible.

15.12 Cost Shifting, Deferral, and Externalization

Chapter 11 established that costs are distributed across bearers and time and are non-fungible by default. Chapter 15 now applies that topology to practical necessity.

A common error is to treat refusal, delay, or non-selection as though they eliminate the relevant load.

Sometimes they do. A goal can be abandoned. A process can be stopped. A harmful system can be dismantled. A demand can be rejected.

But where the underlying consequence or carriage relation persists, non-performance can merely move the cost.

Schematic examples are:

\[ K_A(t_0) \rightarrow K_B(t_1), \]

or:

\[ K_{current} \rightarrow K_{future}. \]

Hence:

\[ \boxed{ deferral \neq cost\ elimination. } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ refusal \neq consequence\ disappearance. } \]

The practical relevance of these relations depends on the same calibration conditions as OUGHT_account. Cost-shifting cannot be invented simply to defeat refusal. A claim that delay transfers burden must be supported by an actual consequence relation.

Where that relation is supported, however, non-action cannot be described as consequence-free merely because the actor did not initiate a positive intervention.

This is a direct extension of the non-action logic already present in PMS-LOGIC and ANTICIPATION. Under effective capacity and real consequence relation, omission can be structurally active in the trajectory.

The strongest safe claim is therefore not:

refusal is wrong.

It is:

refusal may itself occupy a cost-distributing branch whose consequences remain attributable where the relevant conditions are met.

This distinction will become central to the Chapter 16 stance of corrigible consequence-carrying praxis. That stance must permit refusal while preventing refusal from becoming a fiction of consequence erasure.

15.13 Revising the Binding

A constraint-relative ought depends on a maintained condition (G).

This immediately raises an escape route:

if the constraint creates an unacceptable requirement, revise the constraint.

VECTOR allows this.

A self-binding is not metaphysically permanent. Roles can change. Goals can be abandoned. Institutions can be redesigned. Commitments can be narrowed. Rules can be challenged. Agreements can be renegotiated.

Formally:

\[ \Psi(G) \rightarrow Revision(G) \]

is a possible praxis transition.

This prevents OUGHT_constraint from becoming a trap in which any past commitment permanently dictates all future action.

But revision of a binding must be treated as praxis rather than magic.

Chapter 7 already established:

\[ \boxed{ ROT\ does\ not\ erase\Psi. } \]

A change in orientation can make revision appear justified or necessary. It does not itself undo the consequences produced by an existing binding.

Similarly:

\[ \boxed{ revisability\ of\ binding \neq costless\ revision. } \]

A binding may have created expectations, dependencies, institutional arrangements, reputational commitments, contractual structures, or third-party reliance. Revising it can be the correct practical move while still producing real costs.

This point also preserves the temporal structure of responsibility. If a binding was maintained for long enough to close other actors' options, later revision cannot simply restore the earlier field.

Revision changes the trajectory.

It does not reset it.

15.14 “Then Abandon G” Is Still a Praxis Decision
