False invariance occurs when only harmless rotations are admitted. A claim can then appear robust because the test family was selected so that failure was practically impossible.

Rival domestication occurs when a rival model is translated so thoroughly into PMS/VECTOR terms that its external challenge disappears. The framework then appears to have survived a rival that was never allowed to remain genuinely rival.

Probe-to-warrant laundering occurs when a counterfactual or special-case probe produces insight under changed conditions:

\[ insight(F^*) \]

and that insight is silently treated as warrant for the original frame:

\[ warrant(F_0). \]

The valid relation is weaker:

\[ insight(F^*) \not\Rightarrow warrant(F_0). \]

A probe can reveal why the original field is constrained without creating an original-frame option or validating the original claim.

The governing boundary is therefore:

\[ \boxed{ \text{perspectival freedom} \neq \text{freedom to alter the test}. } \]

ROT is valuable because it can change what is seen while keeping the change itself inspectable. Once the change is hidden, ROT loses the very discipline that distinguishes it from arbitrary reframing.

18.3 DIST and Reversibility Drift

DIST and reversibility are especially vulnerable to value inflation because ordinary language already treats distance and reversibility as desirable in many reflective contexts.

Distance inflation turns a wider derived DIST relation into more maturity, truth, or adequacy. The theory then becomes an endorsement of detachment.

Distance collapse produces the reverse romance: immediacy, spontaneity, or proximity to action is treated as intrinsically more authentic or correct.

Neither direction follows from VECTOR. DIST is a derived description of effective orientation–enactment separation, not a virtue axis or independent cause.

Reversibility fiction appears when renewed reflection, a reopened review window, or a configuration summarized by higher DIST (Distance) is treated as though it could undo already accumulated (\Theta) (temporality) and (\Psi) (self-binding). A late ability to revise orientation becomes a fantasy of restored history.

Reversibility maximization turns the correction budget into a universal goal:

\[ REV\uparrow \Rightarrow better\ praxis. \]

This ignores the role of binding, reliability, continuity, and coordination.

Binding pathology by definition makes the same mistake from the opposite side. Every stable (\Psi) is interpreted as suspect closure. Durable commitment becomes failure merely because it reduces future options.

The required boundary is the one already established in Chapter 7:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Reversibility is a budget, not a virtue score.} } \]

A budget can be too small for correction or deliberately spent for reliable commitment. VECTOR's task is to make that change legible, not to prescribe maximal optionality.

18.4 Possibility and Agency Drift

Possibility and agency drift can take several different forms. Some failures misrepresent the option field itself. Others are mechanisms that can generate such misrepresentation, possibilist pressures that do not yet assign a concrete option status, or downstream attribution errors. These types must not be collapsed.

The central option-field error is Option-Field Misrepresentation:

[
\boxed{
OptionFieldMisrepresentation

UnsupportedExclusion
\lor
UnsupportedInclusion.
}
]

Its two directions are Option Blindness and Option Illusion.

Option Invisibility is not itself an option-field failure. An option may be relevantly real or transformably reachable while remaining absent from the current orientation. Invisibility becomes a blindness case only when that lack of visibility is converted into an unsupported exclusion, downgrade, or claim of non-existence, non-availability, or genuine lack of alternatives.

Option Blindness is the negative direction of Option-Field Misrepresentation. It occurs when the analysis excludes more current or transformable possibility than the declared frame, role, time, capacity, dependency, and evidence conditions warrant. The paradigmatic move is:

\[ \neg Visible(o) \rightarrow \neg Available(o), \]

or more strongly:

\[ \neg Visible(o) \rightarrow NoAlternative. \]

But blindness is not limited to visibility error. It can also arise where Unknown is treated as unavailable, where a blocked path is treated as nonexistent, or where present non-actionability is treated as proof that no effective transformation route exists.

Option Illusion is the positive direction of Option-Field Misrepresentation. It occurs when an alternative is included in the relevant effective option field although the conditions required for its availability, actionability, viability, or transformability are not supported. Visibility, conceivability, analyst construction, later capacity, or system-level possibility do not by themselves earn effective option status.

A failure mechanism is not automatically identical to the option-field error type it can produce.

Option-space naturalization treats the current option field as given. The actor becomes a chooser among fixed alternatives even where real second-order transformation may be available. Naturalization produces Option Blindness when that field assumption is used to exclude a sufficiently supported transformation route. A bounded negative after serious transformability testing is not blindness merely because it leaves the current field constrained.

Option-creation blindness is a specialized blindness case concerning Second-Order Agency. Real effective capacity to alter option availability or relations is ignored, and the field is represented as non-transformable despite adequate support for the transformation mechanism, authority or capacity, dependencies, and temporal window.

Distributed agency erasure can generate the same negative error where the analysis looks only for individual capacity even though the relevant transformation is available only through coordinated roles. The resulting collective possibility must still be evidenced; distributed agency is not inferred merely because coordination can be imagined.

On the positive side, Special-case laundering occurs when:

\[ o\in\mathcal O(F^*) \]

is silently imported into:

\[ o\in\mathcal O(F_0) \]

without any real transformation of the original field. The laundering mechanism produces Option Illusion when the constructed alternative is then counted as an effective option under the original frame.

Capacity inflation converts conceptual possibility into effective power:

\[ conceivable \rightarrow capacity. \]

This is invalid even when the imagined transformation is attractive. It becomes Option Illusion when the inflated capacity claim is used to promote the imagined route into the relevant effective option field.

Infinite Option Fantasy is related but not identical to Option Illusion. It responds to every tragic or non-integrable configuration with the demand that some further alternative must exist. If no good path survives, the theory assumes that analysis or creativity must be deficient. Constraint is thereby defined out of existence. The possibilist pressure may generate Option Illusion when a concrete unsupported alternative is assigned effective status, but the fantasy can occur before any particular option has been so misassigned.

Distributed responsibility smearing is downstream of the option field rather than another form of Option-Field Misrepresentation. Because a transformation was collectively possible, every member of the collective is treated as though they possessed equal individual capacity and equal responsibility. This converts a distributed agency relation into undifferentiated attribution and must remain distinct from the prior question of which options were effectively available to whom.

These distinctions preserve a common boundary:

\[ \boxed{ \text{constraint-bound agency} \neq \text{fatalism} \neq \text{possibilism}. } \]

Blindness can make a field falsely necessary by contracting possibility beyond the evidence. Illusion or possibilist drift can erase genuine constraint by expanding effective possibility beyond the evidence. Neither direction is licensed merely by the attractiveness of its conclusion.

The actor is not merely carried by the field, but neither is the actor sovereign over the field.

18.5 Consequence and Timing Drift

Cost and timing make orientation practically serious, but seriousness can easily become speculative inflation.

Transformation-cost blindness sees the benefit of creating or opening an option while ignoring the changes imposed on the rest of the field. Altering \(R_O\) can create new dependencies, exclusions, or reversal costs even when the new option is real.

Downstream cost blindness treats local benefit as trajectory adequacy. A result that works now is assumed to work across later (\Theta) without examining what it stabilizes or externalizes.

Hidden-cost invention abuses residue. Because not all consequences are visible, the analyst invents unspecified costs that conveniently preserve the preferred DIR. The correct pair of claims is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{unseen} \neq \text{zero} } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ \text{unseen} \neq \text{invented}. } \]

Unknown consequence cannot be treated as known support.

Cost fungibility drift converts heterogeneous costs into one aggregate score without warrant. Externalized burden, opportunity loss, reversal cost, delayed effect, and relation change may not be substitutable.

Timing erasure treats the same nominal act as the same praxis relation regardless of when it occurs. It ignores changed bindings, windows, exposure, and option relations.

Delay neutralization treats non-action as automatic preservation of the status quo. Where windows close or costs continue, delay can itself alter the field.

Window fatalism takes the fact that a window is now closed and retroactively treats its earlier existence as proof that an actor could and should have used it. This imports later visibility into earlier capacity and turns trajectory analysis into retrospective guilt.

The governing boundary is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{consequence sensitivity} \neq \text{consequence omniscience}. } \]

VECTOR can require traceable attention to consequence without pretending to know every downstream effect.

18.6 Convergence and Self-Test Drift

Part IV introduced the theory's strongest epistemic machinery. That machinery can also become its most powerful self-confirmation device if its distinctions are allowed to drift.

Convergence illusion treats recurrence as convergence.

Echo convergence counts several claims separately even though they materially depend on the same untested source.

Convergence by abstraction erases local differences until all source domains appear to point in the same trivial direction.

Scope inflation takes a local or bounded resultant and expands it to the whole ecosystem or to praxis in general without increasing the burden.

AI convergence inflation treats stable model output as independent warrant. An AI repeatedly reproducing PMS-consistent orientation may demonstrate learned framework stability, not independent support. VECTOR needs no broader theory of AI here; the problem is purely epistemic.

Closure inflation turns bounded test-burden closure into approximate truth.

Closure laundering declares a claim sufficiently tested despite known claim-critical pressures remaining open.

Revision-as-survival records:

\[ S_0\rightarrow S_1 \]

as evidence that \(S_0\) survived, even though the original claim had to change materially.

Residue erasure allows (\Lambda) to disappear after successful testing. Uncertainty is rhetorically compressed because the claim now feels robust.

The two central boundaries remain:

\[ \boxed{ \text{test survival} \neq \text{truth} } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ \text{revision} \neq \text{survival}. } \]

A self-test that cannot record genuine loss is not a test of robustness. It is a formatting device for confidence.

18.7 Ought, Constraint, and Stance-Label Drift

Part V deliberately permitted stronger practical language than the preceding theory. It therefore creates some of VECTOR's most serious type-jump risks.

Ought inflation converts:

\[ OUGHT_{account} \]

into:

\[ OUGHT_{moral}. \]

The fact that a consequence must remain accounted for becomes the claim that a specific action is morally required.

Ought conflation collapses choice, account, constraint, and moral back into an undifferentiated obligation vocabulary, undoing the purpose of the typology.

Responsibility inflation assumes that more orientation always means more responsibility. In fact, better orientation can reveal missing capacity, false option attribution, or distributed dependence and thereby reduce individual attribution.

Constraint-legitimacy laundering moves from:

\[ G\text{ operatively binds} \]

to:

\[ G\text{ is legitimate}. \]

A constraint can be real and unjustified, self-chosen and later rejected, institutionally imposed and contestable, or simply material. Operativity does not establish legitimacy.

Carriage inflation moves from:

the function must be carried

to:

you must carry it.

The second claim requires an assignment relation not supplied by the first.

Fixpoint authoritarianism moves from a functional stabilization requirement to a claim of deserved authority for a particular bearer:

\[ FixpointRequired \Rightarrow ActorX\text{ deserves authority}. \]

Nothing in the fixpoint relation licenses that inference.

Tragedy denial treats any non-integrable field as evidence of insufficient creativity, insufficient ROT, or insufficient second-order agency.

Heroism drift interprets greater orientation as greater obligation to absorb costs personally.

Adultness drift converts the supporting “Adult Orientation” shorthand into a person rank, maturity verdict, developmental hierarchy, or dignity judgment. Its reduced theoretical status makes this drift less defensible, not less important to police.

Other-binding drift treats strong self-orientation as authority to produce (\Psi) for others.

The common boundary is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Structural consequence can increase without producing moral or political authority.} } \]

This is the practical analogue of the paper's earlier epistemic boundary. More relation does not mean more jurisdiction.

18.8 Meta-Authority and Self-Immunization Drift

The final failure family concerns VECTOR as a whole.

Resultant coercion takes a robust common DIR and turns it into a political or moral command. A field-level direction becomes an entitlement to force action.

Tribunal drift applies structural comparison to persons or groups as though VECTOR could issue verdicts on worth, maturity, or legitimacy.

Meta-authority capture treats VECTOR as the highest PMS layer because it comes late and relates other structures. Relational second-order status becomes hierarchy.

Warrant inheritance treats the authority of Base, STRATA, UNDER LOAD, DISCIPLINE, or domain papers as evidence for VECTOR merely because VECTOR descends from them.

Self-immunization appears when criticism of VECTOR is explained away as evidence that the critic has not rotated enough, does not understand the frame, or is insufficiently oriented. The framework converts disagreement into a defect in the observer.

Framework capture is the most general form. Every external critique is translated into VECTOR terms until nothing remains outside the framework that could count against it. Translation becomes absorption.

A mandatory counterrule therefore follows:

\[ \boxed{ \text{A critique of VECTOR must be allowed to remain external to VECTOR.} } \]

A rival does not have to accept VECTOR's vocabulary in order to challenge VECTOR. An empirical result does not have to be expressible in DIR (Direction)–ROT (Rotation) terms to reduce a VECTOR claim. A conceptual critique can reject the architecture itself rather than merely occupy one of its result statuses.

This is not a concession added after the theory. It is a condition of avoiding self-immunization.

Theoretical vulnerability now has to become operationally consequential. Chapter 19 therefore asks the harder question: if these pressures reveal that a part of VECTOR does not discriminate, does not add explanatory value, cannot calibrate, or loses to a rival, what exactly must become smaller?

19. Falsifiability and Reduction Conditions

Chapter 18 described ways in which a legitimate VECTOR move can drift into a stronger claim than the architecture supports. That is not yet a theory-level failure. A method can be misapplied while remaining useful.

The harder question is whether VECTOR itself earns the conceptual space it occupies.

A theory of orientation cannot satisfy this burden by announcing that it is falsifiable in principle. It must specify what would be reduced if a component does not discriminate, if a distinction cannot be calibrated, if an existing PMS structure already does the work, or if a rival account performs better.

The operative question throughout Chapter 19 is therefore:

What becomes smaller if this condition does not carry?

This makes reduction more important than dramatic declarations of falsification. Some failures should eliminate one bounded claim. Others should narrow scope. Others should remove a component. A sufficiently deep failure could eliminate VECTOR's standalone status altogether.

That hierarchy is not a defense mechanism. It prevents two opposite errors: treating every negative case as a collapse of the whole theory, and treating every theory-level defect as merely another bounded exception.

19.1 Negative Result Is Not Theory Failure

VECTOR is designed to produce negative results.

A case can legitimately end with:

[
NoDIR,
]

[
TradeOff,
]

[
Incomparable,
]

[
Suspend,
]

or:

[
NonCapture.
]

None of these outcomes falsifies VECTOR merely by being negative.

If a claimed direction collapses under affected-role rotation, the local directional claim failed. If two structures remain non-compensatorily opposed, a single resultant failed. If the evidence is insufficient, the current claim is suspended. If the requested comparison itself violates reference continuity or compresses a person-worth judgment into structural ranking, non-capture is the correct output.

In each case the theory may have discriminated successfully.

\[ \boxed{ \text{A failed directional claim can be a successful VECTOR result.} } \]

Indeed, a VECTOR practice that almost never returns trade-off, incomparability, suspension, or non-capture would be suspicious. It would suggest that the architecture is structured to manufacture direction rather than test it.

Theory failure begins elsewhere: when the distinctions VECTOR claims to provide do not remain distinguishable, when its components add no explanatory purchase, when its tests cannot produce real loss, when its outputs cannot be calibrated even in principle, or when a rival account consistently does the same work better.

This distinction between negative case result and theory-level reduction is essential to everything that follows.

19.2 Standalone Redundancy

The strongest paper-level challenge to VECTOR is redundancy.

Suppose DIR (Direction) turns out to be only new language for bounded comparison already adequately handled by DISCIPLINE and STRATA. Suppose ROT (Rotation) adds nothing beyond ordinary recontextualization, counterfactual reasoning, or PROJECT_AS. Suppose DIST (Distance) can be replaced without loss by direct reference to \(Χ,\Theta,\Psi,\Omega,Α\). Suppose option status, consequence topology, and the Ought distinctions are already fully discriminated by existing PMS structures without requiring a new orientation architecture. Suppose, finally, that the “Adult Orientation” label adds no discrimination beyond uptake, capacity, structural attributability, consequence, constraint, corrigibility, residue, refusal, binding, timing, and agency relations already established elsewhere in the paper.

In that case VECTOR may remain a useful synthesis, but it would not earn standalone theoretical status.

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR\text{ should not survive as a standalone theory if its distinctions add no standalone discrimination.} } \]

The appropriate reduction might be a synthesis chapter, conceptual note, appendix, or cross-ecosystem vocabulary rather than a separate paper.

This possibility matters because late theories are particularly vulnerable to novelty by recombination. A framework can appear original because it names relations among mature prior concepts. Naming can be useful. It does not by itself establish theoretical necessity.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Novel naming without novel discrimination is not sufficient reason for a new PMS layer.} } \]

VECTOR therefore earns its existence only if the coordinated architecture makes distinctions, exposes failures, or clarifies recursive relations that are materially harder to express without it.

19.3 DIR Discrimination Failure

DIR claims to distinguish structural direction from mere difference without requiring universal ranking.

That contribution fails if DIR cannot stably separate:

  • difference from direction;
  • local advantage from universal superiority;
  • dominance from trade-off;
  • incomparability from equality;
  • directional claim from person or value ranking.

Two symmetric failure patterns matter.

If almost every comparison can be made directional after enough framing, DIR is too permissive. It becomes a vocabulary for preference.

If almost no comparison can ever support direction because every countercondition blocks commitment, DIR is too weak. It becomes a vocabulary for indefinite qualification.

The component therefore needs both positive and negative discrimination:

\[ \boxed{ DIR \Rightarrow \text{positive discrimination} + \text{negative discrimination}. } \]

If this cannot be achieved in relevant cases, DIR (Direction) must be narrowed or removed. The failure is not repaired by insisting that direction is always subtle. A concept that cannot produce inspectably different outputs under different structures has not earned the role assigned to it.

19.4 ROT Discrimination Failure

ROT claims more than the generic observation that perspective matters.

It claims that perspective and other declared viewing conditions can be treated as testable variables under reference continuity, with a meaningful distinction between robustness-bearing rotation and exploratory probe.

ROT therefore loses independent theoretical value if:

  • every ROT is indistinguishable from ordinary reframing;
  • no admissible ROT can materially reduce a claim;
  • rotation families have no stable functional differences;
  • reference continuity cannot be maintained well enough to know whether the same claim was tested;
  • robustness-bearing ROT and exploratory probe cannot be distinguished in practice;
  • rotational results do not change warrant, scope, or discovery in any way not already captured by existing methods.

A failure here need not eliminate the whole theory. It may require a narrower ROT definition, removal of some rotation families, or reduction of ROT from core component to supporting method.

What is not permissible is keeping ROT as a central component merely because “consider multiple perspectives” sounds epistemically attractive.

A core component must do discriminative work.

19.5 DIST Redundancy Test and Reduction

DIST faces the strongest redundancy burden in the paper because PMS already contains several structures capable of determining whether an orientation remains practically separable from enactment:

\[ Χ, \Theta, \Psi, \Omega, Α. \]

The relevant question is not whether DIST is a convenient phrase. It is whether removing it causes a real discriminative loss rather than merely requiring the source relations to be stated explicitly.

Matched-case test

Consider two bounded scenes with the same current DIR (Direction), the same nominal twenty-four-hour interval before activation, comparable role access, and substantial (Χ) (distance) in the sense that the relevant actors can still step back, inspect the claim, and understand a serious affected-role objection.

In Scene A, the orientation remains private and provisional. No material (Ψ) (self-binding) has yet been created, the review procedure still contains a valid stop, no affected party has reorganized around the decision, and the relevant (Θ) (temporality) window remains practically open. An affected-role ROT (Rotation) can still alter whether the current orientation is enacted at all.

In Scene B, the same reflective (Χ) (distance) and the same nominal clock interval remain, but the trajectory is already loaded by prior (Ψ) (self-binding): contracts have been activated, public commitments have created reliance, and affected roles have begun irreversible preparation. The publicness overlay has increased exposure and hardened the relevant (Θ) (temporality) effects; available reversibility is now materially lower. The same affected-role ROT (Rotation) remains analytically meaningful, but it no longer functions as the same low-cost pre-enactment test. It bears primarily on reversal, mitigation, renegotiation, or later correction.

The practical inference therefore differs:

\[ \boxed{ ROT_{affected}\text{ remains pre-enactment actionable in A} } \]

while:

\[ \boxed{ ROT_{affected}\text{ is primarily reversal/correction-relevant in B}. } \]

A DIST notation can summarize the contrast as:

\[ DIST_A>DIST_B. \]

But the notation is not doing indispensable discriminative work. The same result follows directly from the source relations and their actor-relative practical consequences:

\[ Χ_{high} + \Theta_{open} + \Psi_{low} + StopCapacity \Rightarrow ROT_{pre\text{-}enactment\ actionable}, \]

whereas:

\[ Χ_{high} + \Theta_{path\text{-}loaded} + \Psi_{high} + Publicness_{high} + REV_{low} \Rightarrow ROT_{reversal/correction\ relevant}. \]

The contrast remains fully expressible without introducing an independently necessary third architecture component. Indeed, if the analysis removes DIST (Distance) and then has to reconstruct the same source relations in order to justify the shorthand, no information has been lost. If it retained only the shorthand and hid those relations, information would be lost.

Reduction result

The necessity test therefore does not support DIST as an independent core component.

\[ \boxed{ DIST \rightarrow \text{derived supporting construct}. } \]

The revised architecture is:

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR:=\langle DIR,ROT\rangle. } \]

DIST (Distance) remains useful as a compact descriptor of effective orientation–enactment separation, especially in prose and case comparison. Its use must remain reducible to the actual relations that generate the separation. It cannot add warrant, create a new threshold by itself, or replace the need to specify (Χ) (distance), (Θ) (temporality), (Ψ) (self-binding), (Ω) (asymmetry), (Α) (attractor), publicness, option status, capacity, and reversibility where those relations matter.

This is a genuine architecture reduction rather than a terminological defense of the original triad. The reduction does not show that the underlying practical distinction was false. It shows that VECTOR did not need to own that distinction as a core component in order to preserve it.

19.6 Option-Space Discrimination Failure

Chapter 9 now distinguishes several different kinds of option-field relation that must not be collapsed.

The core option-status predicates are:

\[ conceivable, \quad visible, \quad available, \quad actionable, \quad viable. \]

Branch status is separately represented through relations such as:

\[ realized, \quad rejected, \quad blocked, \quad deferred, \quad lost. \]

Option Invisibility is different again. It is a legibility condition in which a relevantly real or transformably reachable alternative is absent from the current orientation. Option Blindness and Option Illusion are not additional statuses or branch labels. They are opposite forms of Option-Field Misrepresentation: unsupported exclusion from the relevant current or transformable option field, and unsupported inclusion in the relevant effective option field.

These distinctions matter only if their assignment is corrigibly auditable. A richer vocabulary does not by itself improve discrimination.

VECTOR does not require a universal numerical threshold for each status. Non-metric does not mean arbitrary. Analysts should be able to identify the conditions that make an option structurally available but not actionable, blocked rather than lost, currently non-actionable but still transformably reachable, or conceivable only under a changed frame. They should also be able to distinguish evidence insufficiency from negative option status:

\[ \boxed{ Unknown \neq Unavailable \neq Available. } \]

If two careful analyses of the same scene repeatedly produce incompatible option statuses and there is no way to trace the disagreement to frame, evidence, role, temporal position, capacity, dependency, or calibration assumptions, the option architecture is undercalibrated.

The same burden now applies to the distinction between Option Invisibility and Option Blindness. If VECTOR cannot distinguish a case in which an option was simply not legible from a case in which the analysis converted that lack of legibility into unsupported exclusion, then the Blindness category adds terminology without adding discriminative purchase.

\[ \boxed{ OptionInvisibility \neq OptionBlindness } \]

must therefore remain an operational distinction, not merely a definition.

A second failure appears if Option Blindness cannot be distinguished from a warranted bounded negative. After relevant option, role, capacity, dependency, temporal, and transformation pressure, an analysis may responsibly conclude:

No currently evidenced effective transformation is available within the declared window, capacity field, and relevant dependency structure.

That conclusion is not Option Blindness merely because it excludes a transformation. Blindness begins where the exclusion outruns the evidence or the relevant pressure actually applied. If every bounded negative can be destabilized by naming another conceivable route, the category has become possibilistically overinclusive and must be narrowed.

The positive side has an equivalent burden. A special-case construction, hypothetical route, or altered-frame alternative may remain analytically valid as a probe without belonging to the effective option field. If VECTOR cannot distinguish:

[
Probe(o)
]

from:

[
EffectiveOption(o),
]

then Option Illusion becomes too coarse and analytical construction is penalized merely for being hypothetical. The required boundary remains:

\[ \boxed{ invalid\ as\ option \not\Rightarrow invalid\ as\ probe. } \]

Option discrimination must also remain bidirectional in practice. A successful Blindness test may require the represented field to expand because a real or transformably reachable alternative was unsupportedly excluded. A successful Illusion test may require the represented field to contract because a merely conceivable, role-inaccessible, temporally unavailable, or otherwise non-effective alternative was unsupportedly included.

Thus:

\[ \boxed{ \text{option-field correction must permit both warranted expansion and warranted contraction.} } \]

A framework that systematically discovers only more options risks possibilism. A framework that systematically removes options risks fatalistic closure. Neither direction is privileged by the taxonomy itself.

The strongest failure condition is reflexive. Option Blindness and Option Illusion must not become post hoc labels by which VECTOR preserves a preferred result regardless of what the option field contains. If one interpretation can always be dismissed as Blindness, the opposite as Illusion, a demand for further search as Infinite Option Fantasy, and a bounded negative as option-space naturalization, then the framework has acquired a stabilization vocabulary rather than a discriminative architecture.

That possibility must be allowed to count against VECTOR. The proper response is not to add another failure label. It is to simplify, narrow, recalibrate, or remove the option distinction that failed to discriminate.

The theory must therefore demonstrate:

\[ \boxed{ \text{corrigible bidirectional option-status discrimination}. } \]

This means more than consistent terminology. The option architecture must be able to expose its evidential basis, change its assignments when pressure succeeds, preserve Unknown where evidence does not resolve status, and lose theoretical complexity where the added distinctions do not survive application.

If it cannot, the option layer must be simplified or scoped more narrowly.

19.7 Second-Order Agency Failure

Second-order Agency claims to distinguish selection within a field from effective capacity to alter the field or relations among its options.

The distinction fails if every consequential action automatically counts as second-order agency. Ordinary first-order choice often changes later conditions; that fact alone cannot make every action a field transformation in the theoretically relevant sense.

The concept also fails if it cannot distinguish:

  • real option creation from hypothetical special-case construction;
  • opening a blocked option from merely redescribing it;
  • preserving an option from merely delaying a decision;
  • changing \(R_O\) from changing only the analyst's representation;
  • distributed effective capacity from an abstract collective possibility.

If these distinctions do not survive real cases, Second-order Agency should be narrowed. It may remain useful only for explicit institutional redesign, resource creation, authority redistribution, or other strongly evidenced transformations.

The concept earns breadth only by showing that it identifies a real form of praxis not adequately captured by selection alone.

19.8 Cost-Topology Failure

Cost Topology must do more than restate that actions have consequences.

Its value lies in making relevant relations inspectable:

  • who bears a cost;
  • when it occurs;
  • whether it is direct or indirect;
  • whether it is externalized;
  • whether it arises through reversal;
  • whether it results from changing option relations;
  • whether it remains residual rather than supportable.

If every analysis can always generate an indefinite cloud of “possible downstream costs,” (\mathcal K) becomes a rhetoric of seriousness rather than a discriminative structure.

The strongest safeguard is already built into Chapter 11:

\[ \boxed{ \text{unseen} \neq \text{invented}. } \]

VECTOR cannot rescue a weak DIR by populating (\Lambda) with convenient danger. Residual consequence is uncertainty, not hidden evidence.

If Cost Topology does not produce materially different attributions of bearer, timing, externalization, reversal, or relation-change compared with simpler consequence description, its theoretical scope must shrink.

19.9 Convergence Failure

The ecosystem-level ambition of VECTOR depends on more than the local usefulness of DIR (Direction)–ROT (Rotation).

Chapter 13 argues that separately warranted and distinctly constrained local DIR (Direction)s may sometimes support a bounded resultant orientation. That claim must reduce if serious provenance analysis shows that the apparent directions are largely products of shared genealogy, repeated design assumptions, or framework habituation.

Suppose:

\[ d_1,\ldots,d_n \]

appear to converge, but their relevant directional content can be traced mainly to one common source assumption (A):

\[ d_i\leftarrow A \quad\forall i. \]

Then the ecosystem does not provide (n) meaningfully different burdens supporting the same meta-direction. It may provide one assumption repeated across domains.

The appropriate consequence is scope reduction:

\[ \boxed{ \text{ecosystem convergence failure} \Rightarrow \text{possible local VECTOR survival}. } \]

VECTOR could remain useful as a local orientation architecture while losing the stronger claim that it reconstructs convergent direction across the PMS ecosystem.

\[ \boxed{ ScopeReduction \neq TotalFalsification. } \]

But the stronger convergence claim must actually be surrendered if its provenance fails.

19.10 Self-Test Failure

Bounded Test-Burden Closure claims to provide more than a well-organized reflection checklist.

It should reveal errors that unaided coherence would miss. It should preserve a baseline claim long enough to record real loss. It should distinguish revision from survival. It should expose option illusion, rival superiority, hidden dependency, and claim-critical residue. It should provide a meaningful stop condition in bounded test-burden closure.

The contribution weakens if the procedure:

  • almost always confirms the starting claim;
  • produces no additional discrimination beyond ordinary careful reflection;
  • allows baseline claims to be rewritten invisibly;
  • records revision as survival;
  • confuses analyst fatigue with test-burden closure;
  • systematically compresses (\Lambda) at the end;
  • cannot state which known test would still be claim-critical.

In that case the closure procedure may still be practically useful, but its theoretical status should reduce to something like a structured reflection or audit checklist.

That is a smaller claim than the current self-test theory, and VECTOR must be able to accept that reduction.

19.11 Ought-Typology Failure

The Ought typology exists to prevent a genuine structural relation from being flattened either into categorical morality or into total normative silence.

Its value therefore depends on the stable difference among:

\[ OUGHT_{choice}, \quad OUGHT_{account}, \quad OUGHT_{constraint}, \quad OUGHT_{moral}. \]

The typology must reduce if OUGHT_account and OUGHT_constraint cannot be distinguished in relevant cases, or if OUGHT_constraint is fully and more cleanly captured by existing CONFLICT and LOGIC concepts without loss.

Likewise, if analysts routinely disagree about whether a case crosses from account to constraint and no audit of capacity, maintained (G), option structure, or consequence relation resolves the disagreement, the distinction is undercalibrated.

Beautiful notation is not an independent reason to preserve a concept.

The typology earns its place only if it prevents recurrent category mistakes—for example, distinguishing “this consequence must remain accounted for” from “maintaining (G) now requires some response in \(R_G\)” and both from “choose (X).”

If the cases do not discriminate that way, the notation must yield.

Current decision after the revised Part V and adversarial cases: retain the OUGHT_* notation provisionally with the canonical firewall that it denotes typed must-like relations rather than a moral operator family. E.14 and E.15 still produce materially different outputs, while the strengthened uptake and constraint records block the two most dangerous shortcuts. This is not final immunity: if public excerpts continue to collapse account, constraint, and moral prescription, the formal aliases should be neutralized in a later revision.

19.12 Adult Orientation Redundancy and Reduction

The “Adult Orientation” label carries one of the highest publicness and person-ranking burdens in the paper. It therefore cannot survive merely because Chapter 16 gives it a careful definition. The relevant test is deletion.

The question is:

What discriminative work disappears if the Adult Orientation construct is deleted while the underlying VECTOR/PMS relations remain?

The comparison can be performed on the same bounded praxis situation in two ways.

Analysis A uses the Chapter 16 shorthand. It describes a praxis stance as corrigible, consequence-carrying, capacity-bounded, residue-preserving, rotatable where possible, capable of refusal and binding, sensitive to distributed and Second-Order Agency, and unwilling to convert orientation into authority.

Analysis B deletes the label and reconstructs the same situation directly through:

  • actor uptake;
  • bounded DIR (Direction) and relevant ROT (Rotation);
  • effective capacity;
  • structural attributability;
  • option status and consequence relation;
  • maintained constraint and assignment structure;
  • (\Lambda) (non-event/remainder) / residue;
  • timing, reversibility, binding, and the source relations behind orientation–enactment separation;
  • refusal;
  • self-binding versus other-binding;
  • Second-Order and distributed agency where evidenced.

The current paper does not obtain a different option status, attribution result, constraint result, refusal relation, binding relation, ROT result, or residue state from Analysis A. Every claim-critical distinction survives Analysis B. What disappears is the compact integrative name.

That is useful, but it is not independent discrimination.

\[ \boxed{ \text{integrative shorthand} \neq \text{independent theoretical discrimination}. } \]

The label also fails a stronger necessity test. If Adult Orientation were defined as whatever it means to carry all prior VECTOR relations well, the definition would protect the label by making every lower-level success evidence for the higher-level construct. That would reproduce novelty by recombination rather than add a new loss-capable distinction.

The reduction result is therefore:

\[ \boxed{ AdultOrientation \rightarrow \text{supporting stance label only}. } \]

Chapter 16 remains useful because it collects the practical consequences of the preceding architecture in one place. The reduction changes its claim type: the chapter is a synthesis of already discriminated praxis relations, not evidence for an additional VECTOR construct.

The publicness boundary remains necessary. The label may still be heard as maturity or person rank. Its supporting status therefore does not license stronger circulation or person-near use; it lowers the amount of theoretical work the label is allowed to carry. If even that shorthand repeatedly produces ranking drift, the next reduction is simple: remove or rename it without loss of the underlying praxis distinctions.

This is a second genuine reduction in the current paper. DIST lost core-component status; Adult Orientation loses independent theoretical status while the stance it abbreviates remains descriptively available.

19.13 Calibration Failure

VECTOR deliberately begins relationally and ordinally before metric. That decision protects the theory from premature scores. It does not protect it from calibration demands.

\[ \boxed{ \text{non-metric} \neq \text{arbitrary}. } \]

The Calibration Boundary established in Part I means that two VECTOR-conform analyses may remain calibration-divergent while agreeing on reference, claim type, evidence status, and ROT (Rotation) discipline. Threshold disagreement alone is therefore not a theory failure.

A calibration failure occurs instead where a material threshold is hidden, unsupported, silently shifted, or treated as though VECTOR generated it endogenously; where analysts cannot state which threshold basis produced the divergence; or where unresolved calibration disagreement is reported as a common robust status.

Relevant threshold-bearing claims include:

  • DIR warrant or scope;
  • ROT relevance/material distinctness;
  • option status;
  • effective capacity;
  • derived DIST comparisons where the shorthand is used;
  • convergence;
  • test-burden closure;
  • structural-attribution uplift;
  • constraint-relative necessity.

A strong analysis should therefore be able to say:

We agree on the VECTOR structure but disagree on a domain threshold.

That result may require narrower scope, explicit calibration divergence, Suspend, or rival-sensitive comparison. It does not automatically mean one analyst violated VECTOR.

Calibration failure begins where the divergence becomes untraceable or is concealed.

\[ \boxed{ CalibrationFailure \Rightarrow ClaimReduction\ \text{or}\ Suspend, } \]

not because disagreement itself is forbidden, but because the claim exceeded the calibration it could actually support.

The stronger the intended scope, the stronger the calibration burden.

19.14 Rival Superiority

VECTOR must be able to lose to another model.

A rival may describe the same reference with fewer concepts, stronger discrimination, better calibration, clearer counterfactual sensitivity, or less framework-specific machinery. It may expose that a VECTOR distinction is redundant, that a claimed option relation is artifactually constructed, or that the DIR (Direction) result depends on one unearned frame choice.

Where that happens:

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR\text{ must yield locally.} } \]

Local yielding can mean replacing a VECTOR reconstruction for that case, narrowing one concept, or accepting that the rival explains the relevant relation better.

If rival superiority becomes systematic—if the same competing architecture repeatedly performs the relevant discriminative work more simply and robustly—then VECTOR as a whole must reduce.

The current paper now records two different losses. Appendix E.21 shows domain-task deference: a calibrated queueing model wins a specialist delay-minimization burden. Appendix E.23 supplies the harder test: a calibrated non-PMS MCDA addresses the same bounded orientation question and carries the commensurable, externally weighted comparison more directly than DIR (Direction)–ROT (Rotation). The result narrows DIR and ROT in that class and reduces the standalone scope:

\[ \boxed{ StandaloneVECTOR \not\supset \text{all calibrated commensurable multi-criteria orientation problems}. } \]

Where an external decision architecture already represents the relevant criterion weights and their sensitivity with better calibration and less machinery, VECTOR may still audit omitted roles, false commensurability, option illusion, external warrant, or practical-attribution boundaries. It does not regain primary orientational status merely by redescribing the rival's sensitivity analysis as ROT.

The prohibited response is:

the rival is actually a special case of VECTOR.

That move may sometimes be analytically true, but it cannot be the default defense against defeat. A rival must remain capable of demonstrating that VECTOR is unnecessary.

19.15 Failure of Self-Reduction

This is the strongest internal condition of the paper.

VECTOR repeatedly requires bounded claims, visible revision, downgrade, non-capture, and the possibility that a direction collapses. Those requirements become empty if they cannot be turned on VECTOR itself.

Self-application must therefore permit:

  • scope reduction;
  • component removal;
  • terminology reduction;
  • domain non-capture;
  • loss of ecosystem-convergence claims;
  • loss of standalone status.
\[ \boxed{ \text{VECTOR must be capable of making VECTOR smaller.} } \]

If VECTOR(VECTOR) is structurally allowed to produce only reasons why VECTOR survives, self-application has become immunity. The framework may still be coherent. It would no longer satisfy its own theory of loss-capable orientation.

The purpose of self-reduction is not ritual humility. It is to make the theory's epistemic commitments costly enough to matter.

19.16 Reduction Ladder

Not every failure warrants the same response. VECTOR therefore needs a reduction ladder rather than a binary survive/falsify switch.

Level 1 — Claim Reduction

A bounded claim fails.

One DIR may collapse under rotation. One option may be reclassified from actionable to illusory. One cost relation may lose support.

The architecture remains unchanged.

Level 2 — Scope Reduction

The claim survives only under narrower coordinates:

\[ Q,F,t,\pi. \]

A relation thought to hold across roles may hold only for one role. An ecosystem-level direction may reduce to a domain-specific one.

Level 3 — Component Reduction

A component or supporting concept must be narrowed, retyped, or partly removed.

ROT may lose some families. DIST has, in the current revision, already been reduced from core component to derived supporting construct. The “Adult Orientation” construct has likewise been reduced to a supporting stance label after its deletion test found no independent discrimination. Second-order Agency may apply only to strongly evidenced field transformations.

Level 4 — Domain Non-Capture

VECTOR may be inadequate for a domain or class of objects:

\[ \varnothing_{V,domain}. \]

The proper conclusion is not that the domain failed VECTOR but that VECTOR should not orient there.

Level 5 — Architecture Reduction

The remaining core:

\[ \langle DIR,ROT\rangle \]

may still fail to stand as an independently useful theoretical architecture. DIR (Direction) or ROT (Rotation) could remain as vocabulary or method while VECTOR loses its claim to coordinated standalone necessity. The present reduction of DIST (Distance) demonstrates that this level is not merely ceremonial; a stronger future failure could reduce the core further.

Level 6 — Standalone Reduction

VECTOR may fail to earn independent paper or theory status.

The remaining material could be reduced to:

  • synthesis;
  • conceptual note;
  • appendix;
  • cross-ecosystem vocabulary;
  • a late section inside another PMS work.

The purpose of the ladder is not to make failure sound less serious. It is to make the consequences exact.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Reduction makes falsifiability more precise, not more dramatic.} } \]

A theory is better tested when it specifies what must be surrendered at each level rather than treating every objection as either irrelevant or catastrophic.

19.16.1 Provisional Current Burden Assessment

The reduction ladder must be applied to the current draft rather than left as a hypothetical possibility. The following table records the present burden status after the revised adversarial cases. It is not a verdict that VECTOR “passes.”

Element Current status Reason / remaining burden
DIR Retain, scope-narrowed Distinguishes direction from difference, trade-off, incomparability, and non-capture; E.23 shows that in externally calibrated commensurable multi-criteria comparisons the carried ranking may be better generated by the rival decision model, with VECTOR DIR dependent on rather than primary over that calibration.
ROT Retain, scope-narrowed Collapse cases still show more than generic reframing, but E.23 shows that where perspective/priority variation is already externally calibrated and represented by sensitivity analysis, general ROT is supplementary unless it exposes a relation the rival model omits.
DIST Supporting only Failed the standalone necessity test; retained as derived shorthand, removed from the core architecture.
Option taxonomy Retain, evidence-typed Useful discrimination remains, but Unknown must stay distinct from Available/Unavailable and retrospective claims remain limited.
Second-Order Agency Retain, narrow E.11 demonstrates collapse when mechanism/authority/window fail; only traceably evidenced transformations carry.
Cost topology Retain, narrow Non-fungible consequence distinctions remain useful; unsupported hidden costs stay Unknown rather than becoming positive claims.
Convergence Retain as test architecture Echo control remains discriminative; no ecosystem resultant may be inferred from genealogy or recurrence alone.
Bounded Test-Burden Closure Retain, calibration-sensitive Provides a stop rule without finality; threshold basis must remain visible and can diverge.
Ought typology Retain with firewall E.14/E.15 discriminate account from constraint; semantic/publicness burden remains the highest in the paper.
Adult Orientation Supporting only Deletion test preserves all claim-critical discrimination through lower-level relations; retained only as an integrative stance label, with publicness/ranking burden still material.
Standalone VECTOR Provisional retain, scope-reduced E.23 supplies same-burden rival superiority: calibrated commensurable multi-criteria orientation is ceded where the rival carries the declared directional and sensitivity burden more directly. Remaining standalone status is limited to discrimination not losslessly supplied by such rivals.

The table records one actual architecture change, several narrowed claim ceilings, and several unresolved burdens. It therefore satisfies the minimal condition for non-ceremonial self-assessment:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Current self-assessment matters only because it can change the architecture.} } \]

It already has.

19.16.2 Final Component Decision Table

The current revision therefore makes the following explicit retain/reduce decisions. A mark under Narrow means the construct remains but with a lower claim ceiling or stronger evidence condition than the earlier draft.

Element Retain Narrow Supporting only Remove Reason
DIR ✓ ✓ Still produces positive and negative discrimination, but E.23 narrows primary DIR status where an externally calibrated rival already carries the commensurable ranking burden.
ROT ✓ ✓ Retained as more than ordinary reframing, but E.23 narrows its role in calibrated commensurable domains where sensitivity analysis already represents the relevant priority variation; granularity retyping remains excluded from same-claim evidence.
DIST ✓ Failed standalone necessity test; useful shorthand remains, core status removed.
Option taxonomy ✓ ✓ Retained with explicit evidence status and Unknown; retrospective/person-near claims are narrower.
Second-Order Agency ✓ ✓ Requires traceable actor, mechanism, authority/capacity, dependencies, window, and evidence; E.11 shows collapse when these fail.
Cost topology ✓ ✓ Retained as non-fungible consequence discrimination; speculative hidden costs remain Hypothetical/Unknown rather than positive support.
Convergence ✓ ✓ Retained with echo control and calibration-sensitive compatibility; recurrence/genealogy cannot produce a resultant by themselves.
Test-burden closure ✓ ✓ Retained as bounded stopping architecture; no “near-final” truth status and no endogenous threshold guarantee.
Ought typology ✓ ✓ Retained with explicit non-moral operator firewall, orientation-uplift gate, and strengthened constraint audit.
Adult Orientation ✓ Deletion test found no independent discriminative loss; retained only as supporting shorthand for corrigible consequence-carrying praxis and remains explicitly distinct from MIP maturity, EDEN Adult Reciprocity, Adult Person, and dignity rank.
Standalone VECTOR ✓ ✓ Provisionally retained only after scope reduction: E.23 yields calibrated commensurable multi-criteria orientation to a same-burden non-PMS rival where it performs the directional and sensitivity work more directly.

The table does not establish standalone success. It records what the present revision has actually surrendered: DIST core status, Adult Orientation independent status, and part of DIR–ROT/standalone scope under a same-burden rival. Further systematic rival superiority, failure of DIR (Direction)/ROT (Rotation) discrimination, or continuing publicness failure in the Ought layer can still force stronger reduction.

19.17 The Permissible Conclusion: No Robust VECTOR

The reduction ladder reaches its strongest test when the theory permits the conclusion that no robust VECTOR exists at the relevant level.

For a case:

\[ \boxed{ No\ robust\ VECTOR\ available\ for\ this\ case. } \]

For a domain:

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR\ not\ adequate\ here. } \]

For an ecosystem meta-claim:

\[ \boxed{ No\ robust\ ecosystem\ resultant. } \]

For the theory itself:

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR\ does\ not\ earn\ standalone\ status. } \]

These conclusions cannot be ceremonial possibilities that the architecture would never actually allow. They must remain live outcomes of serious application, calibration, rivalry, and self-reduction.

The strongest expression of this requirement is simple:

A theory of orientation that cannot orient away from itself has failed its strongest claim.

This does not mean that VECTOR should seek its own disappearance. It means that its continued existence must remain conditional on the work its distinctions actually perform.

Only after that reduction pressure is explicit can the paper state what it positively claims.

20. Positive Boundary — What Remains after Reduction

A theory composed only of prohibitions would not justify its own complexity.

After Chapters 18 and 19, VECTOR therefore has to answer a positive question:

\[ \boxed{ \text{If VECTOR survives only in bounded form, what can it still positively claim?} } \]

The answer should be smaller than the full path of the paper. It should identify capacities that remain useful even after global rank, final truth, universal scale, moral automation, and authority inflation have been removed.

The positive boundary is not a summary of every earlier chapter. It is the remainder after reduction.

The present reduction already excludes a class in which VECTOR does not retain primary orientational status. Appendix E.23 shows that where a bounded multi-criteria problem is genuinely commensurable, externally calibrated, and more directly carried by a non-PMS sensitivity model, DIR (Direction), ROT (Rotation), and the standalone claim narrow accordingly. The positive claims below therefore apply only where VECTOR adds discrimination not losslessly supplied by the relevant rival.

20.1 Bounded Directional Discrimination

VECTOR can distinguish among:

  • difference;
  • direction;
  • dominance;
  • trade-off;
  • incomparability;
  • non-capture;

under declared conditions.

That is already a substantive result.

A non-final framework need not choose between universal ranking and flat equivalence. It can support local directional discrimination while allowing the absence of rank to remain meaningful.

\[ \boxed{ \text{direction without requiring total order}. } \]

This is the minimal positive answer to the problem posed in the Introduction.

20.2 Perspective as a Testable Variable

VECTOR does not solve perspectival dependence by imagining a view from nowhere.

It makes perspective explicit enough to test.

A claim can be examined for:

  • which position initially carries it;
  • which role rotation weakens it;
  • which affected-role view reveals externalized cost;
  • which temporal or granularity shift changes its scope;
  • which rival model exposes an assumption the original frame made invisible.

The positive contribution is therefore not relativism but inspectability:

\[ \boxed{ \text{perspective can become a testable variable}. } \]

A directional claim can gain warrant by surviving materially relevant perspectival change and can lose warrant by failing it. Where an externally calibrated rival already represents the relevant priority variation and its sensitivity without loss, ROT (Rotation) is supplementary unless it exposes a relation the rival omits.

20.3 Reversibility-Sensitive Orientation

VECTOR can treat orientation as path-sensitive rather than static.

It can ask not only whether a direction currently carries, but how much effective separation remains before enactment, what reversal or correction remains practically available, what costs have sedimented, and how binding changes later freedom.

This adds a temporal and practical dimension to orientation without making reversibility a universal good.

\[ Reversibility \neq ValueToMaximize. \]

The positive claim is instead that the remaining correction budget is often relevant to how an orientation should be understood, tested, and enacted.

20.4 Bidirectional Possibility Discrimination

VECTOR can distinguish different statuses of possibility:

\[ conceivable, \quad visible, \quad available, \quad actionable, \quad viable. \]

It can also distinguish branch status from option status, preserve Unknown where evidence does not resolve the relation, and separate a condition of legibility from an error of option-field representation.

In particular:

\[ \boxed{ OptionInvisibility \neq OptionBlindness. } \]

An alternative may be real or transformably reachable while absent from the current orientation. That divergence is not by itself a failure. Option Blindness begins only when a relevant possibility is unsupportedly excluded, downgraded, or treated as absent. Option Illusion is the opposite error: a possibility is unsupportedly included in the relevant effective option field despite missing frame, role, capacity, temporal, dependency, or other constitutive conditions.

The contribution is therefore bidirectional. VECTOR can pressure a represented option field for unsupported contraction and unsupported expansion:

[
\boxed{
OptionBlindness

UnsupportedExclusion,
\qquad
OptionIllusion

UnsupportedInclusion.
}
]

This does not mean that VECTOR guarantees correct reconstruction of the real option field. Chapter 19 makes the opposite burden explicit: if the distinctions cannot be calibrated, corrected, distinguished from warranted bounded negatives and legitimate probes, or exposed to rival pressure without becoming post hoc stabilization vocabulary, the option architecture must be reduced.

The gain is narrower. VECTOR provides a typed and corrigible architecture capable of asking whether an apparent absence is evidentially warranted and whether an apparent possibility is effectively supported. It can therefore reveal that a necessity claim excluded too much, that a creativity claim included too much, or that the available evidence warrants neither conclusion and must remain Unknown.

This matters because orientation quality cannot be inferred from the number of alternatives an analyst can name. A larger field may be inflated by imaginary, role-inaccessible, temporally unavailable, or special-case alternatives. A smaller field may be artificially narrowed by invisibility, fixed-field assumptions, ignored distributed capacity, or unsupported necessity claims. Conversely, a genuinely constrained or tragic field need not become defective merely because no further effective option survives appropriate pressure.

The positive contribution is therefore possibility discrimination, not option maximization. More precisely, it is the possibility of corrigible bidirectional option-status discrimination under declared actor, role, frame, time, capacity, dependency, and evidence conditions.

20.5 Constraint-Bound Second-Order Agency

VECTOR can describe actors as more than selectors of a fixed option set.

Where effective capacity exists, praxis can alter:

\[ \mathcal O \]

or:

\[ R_O. \]

Actors can sometimes create options, open blocked paths, preserve windows, close alternatives, alter sequences, or change relations among choices.

That claim remains bounded:

\[ \boxed{ Agency \text{ remains capacity-, role-, time-, and constraint-bound.} } \]

The gain is a middle position between fatalism and voluntarism. The field is not necessarily fixed, and the actor is not sovereign over it.

20.6 Consequence without Total-Cost Conversion

VECTOR can make consequence topology more explicit without converting heterogeneous effects into one utility score.

It can identify:

  • externalized cost;
  • delayed cost;
  • opportunity loss;
  • reversal cost;
  • relation-change cost;
  • role- and time-specific burden;
  • residual unknown consequence.

This supports richer orientation where local benefit hides downstream structure.

But:

\[ \mathcal K_t \neq TotalUtilityScore. \]

The positive claim is consequence sensitivity with preserved non-fungibility.

20.7 Bounded Test-Burden Closure without Finality

VECTOR proposes a practical middle between dogmatic certainty and permanent skepticism.

That middle is bounded test-burden closure.

\[ \boxed{ \text{bounded test-burden closure} } \]

means that the currently relevant declared burden has been worked through far enough to stop this inquiry responsibly while material residue, unresolved calibration, missing external evidence, and future reopening remain visible.

This is stronger than saying that certainty is impossible. It gives a reason for a bounded inquiry to stop without converting stopping into final truth.

\[ \boxed{ \text{test-burden closure} \neq \text{truth closure}. } \]

The ability to stop responsibly is itself a positive epistemic capacity.

20.8 Practical Consequence without Moral Automation

VECTOR's strongest philosophical contribution may lie in allowing structural consequence to matter without turning structure into a moral machine.

It can say:

this consequence is no longer structurally neutral for the actor

without saying:

therefore the actor is morally forbidden to choose otherwise.

It can say:

maintaining (G) now requires some response within \(R_G\)

without saying:

therefore (G) is morally legitimate or this response is morally required simpliciter.

The theory therefore does not merely reject Ought. It types different kinds of practical necessity so that they do not collapse into one another.

That makes room for consequence without categorical moral automation.

20.9 Corrigible Consequence-Carrying Praxis

Chapter 16 collects the practical stance that remains after the preceding distinctions are carried into enactment. “Adult Orientation” is retained only as supporting shorthand for this stance; Chapter 19 finds no independent discriminative loss when the label is removed.

It is corrigible without being permanently suspended. It preserves relevant (\Lambda) (non-event/remainder) without using uncertainty as an automatic escape from action. It remains open to ROT (Rotation) while recognizing that binding can be real. It handles review, binding, timing, reversibility, and enactment conditions without reifying derived DIST (Distance). It carries consequence, recognizes second-order and distributed agency, admits capacity limits, can refuse, can bind, and distinguishes self-binding from authority over others.

The stance remains praxeological; the following formula polices only the supporting shorthand:

\[ \boxed{ AdultOrientation \neq AdultPerson. } \]

The formula polices the shorthand; it does not restore independent theoretical status.

Its positive function is not to rank people. It names a way of relating orientation to consequence under non-finality.

20.10 Legitimate Non-Capture

VECTOR includes:

\[ \varnothing_V \]

as a real output.

This is not merely a defensive escape for difficult cases. A theory that can identify when its own comparison would destroy reference continuity, create an illegitimate person-worth rank, compress essential remainder, or exceed available warrant can discriminate more strongly than one that always returns direction.

\[ \boxed{ \text{A theory that knows when not to orient can discriminate more strongly than one that always produces direction.} } \]

Non-capture therefore belongs to VECTOR's positive capacity set. It marks a bounded refusal to generate a claim the architecture cannot responsibly carry.

20.11 Compact Reduction Set

The central boundaries of VECTOR can now be stated without reopening their full arguments.

Direction

\[ \text{direction} \neq \text{destiny} \]
\[ \text{local superiority} \neq \text{universal superiority} \]
\[ \text{incomparability} \neq \text{equality}. \]

Perspective

\[ \text{rotation} \neq \text{relativism} \]
\[ \text{probe} \neq \text{original-frame warrant}. \]

Time and possibility

\[ \text{distance} \neq \text{superiority} \]
\[ \text{reversibility} \neq \text{reset} \]
\[ \text{conceivable} \neq \text{available} \]
\[ \text{availability} \neq \text{capacity} \]
\[ \text{option transformation} \neq \text{unlimited agency}. \]

Consequence

\[ \text{cost} \neq \text{total score} \]
\[ \text{unseen} \neq \text{invented}. \]

Normativity

\[ \text{capacity} \neq \text{obligation} \]
\[ \text{constraint} \neq \text{legitimacy} \]
\[ \text{necessity} \neq \text{moral goodness} \]
\[ \text{self-binding} \neq \text{authority to bind others}. \]

Epistemic status

\[ \text{invariance} \neq \text{final truth} \]
\[ \text{convergence} \neq \text{consensus} \]
\[ \text{test-burden closure} \neq \text{truth closure}. \]

Human boundary

\[ VECTOR \neq \text{person ranking} \]
\[ VECTOR \neq \text{dignity ranking} \]
\[ \text{Adult Orientation shorthand} \neq AdultPerson \]
\[ VECTOR \neq \text{tribunal}. \]

These are not twenty separate theories. They are the compressed boundary set generated by the paper's positive distinctions.

20.12 Structural Attributability without Added Authority

Two statements carry the practical remainder of VECTOR.

The first is unconditional as a theory boundary:

\[ \boxed{ \text{More orientation does not mean more authority.} } \]

A stronger DIR (Direction), greater rotational robustness, clearer option field, richer consequence topology, or more complete current test-burden closure does not grant additional decision rights, political jurisdiction, moral status, or entitlement to bind other actors.

\[ Orientation\uparrow \not\Rightarrow DecisionAuthority\uparrow. \]

The second statement is conditional and narrower. Where a new VECTOR orientation is claimed to increase attribution, the relevant relation must include warrant, external-premise support where needed, actor uptake, effective capacity, consequence relation, and the relevant role and scene-time conditions.

\[ Orientation\uparrow + ExternalPremiseSupport + ActorUptake + EffectiveCapacity + ConsequenceRelation + Role/TimeConditions \Rightarrow? \Delta Attributability_{orientation}\uparrow. \]

The question mark matters. Better orientation can also reduce attribution by showing that an apparent option was illusory, that the actor lacked effective capacity, that a window had already closed, that the consequence relation was unsupported, or that the relevant agency was distributed elsewhere.

This orientation-mediated relation must also remain separate from baseline PMS-LOGIC attributability, which can be structurally supported by effective capacity and consequence without an awareness requirement.

\[ \boxed{ BaselineAttributability_{LOGIC} \neq \Delta Attributability_{orientation}. } \]

The technical primary conclusion is therefore:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Orientation can increase structural attributability without increasing decision authority.} } \]

The paper may retain the older rhetorical shorthand only secondarily:

More orientation can mean less structural innocence.

But its meaning is fixed by the firewall:

\[ \boxed{ loss\ of\ structural\ innocence \neq guilt \neq blame \neq liability \neq moral\ fault. } \]

That is the final positive boundary before the conclusion.

21. Conclusion — Direction, Rotation, and Transformable Possibility

PMS–VECTOR began from a narrow problem with wide consequences. PMS can render heterogeneous praxis structures legible while remaining non-final, bounded, and resistant to universal rank. Yet once structural differences are visible, a further question appears: can some of those differences warrant direction without turning direction into total order, perspective into relativism, consequence into morality, or framework coherence into authority?

VECTOR's answer is conditional rather than universal.

Some structural relations can carry bounded direction. That direction becomes stronger only when it remains vulnerable to admissible perspectival pressure. The practical significance of orientation depends partly on the derived orientation–enactment separation and on how much correction or reversal the prior trajectory still permits. Orientation then encounters possibility, capacity, consequence, binding, and time—not as additional VECTOR coordinates, but as the praxis field in which the DIR (Direction)–ROT (Rotation) core becomes consequential.

The result is not a universal quality scale. It is a praxeology of orientation whose claims remain typed, bounded, revisable, and capable of non-capture.

This matters because the original problem cannot be solved responsibly by choosing one of two easy exits. The first exit would be finalism: if some directions are better warranted, then one universal ranking must ultimately be recoverable. The second would be flattening: if no final ranking is available, then no directional discrimination is justified. VECTOR rejects both. A relation can be directional without becoming total, robust without becoming final, consequential without becoming morally self-executing, and revisable without becoming arbitrary.

The price of that middle position is continuous type discipline. Every expansion of scope creates a new burden. Every perspective test must preserve enough continuity to remain informative. Every claim about possibility must distinguish imagination from effective capacity. Every practical necessity must disclose the constraint on which it depends. Every positive result must remain capable of reduction. The theory therefore ends not with a master criterion but with a compact architecture whose usefulness depends on how well those burdens remain visible in actual reconstruction.

21.1 Minimal VECTOR

The architecture can therefore end smaller than it began.

The core is:

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR:=\langle DIR,ROT\rangle. } \]

A concrete tested state is:

[
\boxed{
V_t\(O\mid F,Q,\pi\)

\langle DIR_t,RP_t\rangle.
}
]

DIR is bounded structural direction under declared comparison conditions.

ROT is admissible perspectival testing and discovery under declared continuity and change. Its current test state is represented by RP (ROT Profile), not by treating the operation itself as a stored coordinate.

DIST (Distance) remains available as a derived descriptor of effective orientation–enactment separation, but it no longer belongs to the core. The redundancy test showed that its practical discrimination can be reconstructed directly through (Χ) (distance), (Θ) (temporality), (Ψ) (self-binding), (Ω) (asymmetry), (Α) (attractor), publicness, option status, capacity, and reversibility. Keeping the shorthand does not restore a third component.

The same reduction discipline applies to Chapter 16. “Adult Orientation” remains only supporting shorthand for corrigible consequence-carrying praxis because deletion of the label removes no claim-critical discrimination. The stance remains describable; the label carries no independent theoretical status.

The remaining DIR (Direction)–ROT (Rotation) core is also scope-bounded by rivalry. Appendix E.23 shows that where a multi-criteria orientation problem is genuinely commensurable, externally calibrated, and better carried by a non-PMS sensitivity architecture, VECTOR cedes primary directional and perspectival status to the rival for that class. DIR and ROT remain core functions only within the scope in which they add discrimination not losslessly supplied by the rival.

Option Space, Cost Topology, effective capacity, Ought typing, result status, calibration, uptake, and DIST (Distance) are necessary or useful for understanding what happens when orientation enters praxis. They do not turn the two-part core into an expandable list of dimensions.

The same restraint applies mathematically. DIR (Direction) and ROT (Rotation) remain heterogeneous and relational before metric. The name VECTOR does not create a norm, magnitude, angle, or total scale; derived DIST (Distance) likewise carries no default metric.

21.2 Extended Praxeological Cycle

The minimal state becomes practically meaningful inside a larger open cycle:

\[ \boxed{ V_t \rightarrow (\mathcal O_t,R_{O,t},\mathcal K_t) \xrightarrow[\text{constraints}]{C_{eff}} E/\neg E \xrightarrow{\Theta,\Psi,\Omega} (\mathcal O_{t+1},R_{O,t+1},\mathcal K_{t+1}) \rightarrow V_{t+1} } \]

where:

[
C_{eff}

\text{effective capacity}.
]

This is a dependency schema, not a deterministic update law.

\[ \boxed{ \text{dependency schema} \neq \text{closed dynamics}. } \]

External (\Delta) (difference), other actors, institutional change, material events, new evidence, and unknown conditions can enter at any stage. The actor does not calculate the future field from the current VECTOR state. The schema shows only that orientation, possibility, capacity, enactment, temporality, binding, asymmetry, and later orientation can become recursively related.

This open structure matters because VECTOR would otherwise reproduce the very closure it rejects. A theory of orientation cannot make the future fully endogenous to its own categories without converting non-capture into fiction.

21.3 Orientation as Recursive Praxis

The central movement of the paper can be stated more simply:

\[ \boxed{ \text{orientation} \rightarrow \text{praxis} \rightarrow \text{changed conditions} \rightarrow \text{new orientation}. } \]

Orientation does not necessarily remain a description outside the field it describes.

A newly visible option can change deliberation. A newly visible cost can change attribution. A changed DIR (Direction) can alter commitment, publicness, testing, binding, and other praxis relations; these can widen or narrow effective orientation–enactment separation, optionally summarized by derived DIST (Distance). A recognized false orientation can trigger revision while leaving its prior effects intact. A maintained binding can make later ROT (Rotation) more expensive. A decision can close one corridor and create another. A refusal can transfer carriage. A delayed decision can preserve a window, close it, or leave the relevant structure unchanged depending on the actual field.

Orientation can therefore become an actor-relative praxis event even before an overt external action occurs, but only where the orientation has become actor-accessible, sufficiently understood for contest or response, and praxis-available inside the relevant window. An analyst's statement alone does not produce this transition.

This does not collapse knowledge into action. It means that orientation, once sufficiently warranted and taken up and where it is connected to capacity and consequence, can become one of the conditions under which later action is interpreted. Baseline structural attributability that exists independently under PMS-LOGIC remains a separate relation.

21.4 The Actor Does Not Merely Move through the Field

A purely first-order model treats the actor as a selector:

\[ selector(\mathcal O_t). \]

VECTOR allows a stronger but bounded possibility. Under effective capacity, an actor or coordinated set of actors can sometimes transform:

\[ \mathcal O_t \]

or:

\[ R_{O,t}. \]

A blocked path can be opened. A window can be preserved. A new institutional procedure can create an option that did not previously exist. An exclusion relation can become a sequence relation. A commitment can deliberately close options in order to create reliability.

Yet this capacity remains partial.

\[ \boxed{ Actor \neq sovereign\ creator. } \]

Material constraints, other actors, law, institutions, history, asymmetry, temporality, and unknown conditions remain part of the field. Some possibilities are transformable, some merely imaginable, some already lost. Some conflicts remain tragic even after second-order agency has been taken seriously.

The actor therefore occupies neither the position of passive passenger nor total author. Praxis can transform possibility without owning possibility.

21.5 Praxis Changes Future Orientation Conditions

The recursive consequence extends beyond options.

Praxis can change:

  • cost visibility;
  • role position;
  • (\Psi);
  • available ROT;
  • reversibility;
  • learned legibility;
  • future orientation–enactment and revision conditions;
  • institutional access;
  • which consequences remain attributable;
