  • which rivals become visible or impossible to ignore.

Thus:

\[ \boxed{ praxis_t \rightarrow conditions\ of\ orientation_{t+1}. } \]

This is one of the strongest consequences of the paper because it changes the meaning of orientation itself.

The actor is not repeatedly facing the same fixed world with progressively better information. Their own praxis can alter the world of later orientation. Binding can make correction harder. Refusal can shift carriage. Public commitment can change the asymmetry of revision. A successful intervention can create downstream dependencies. A failed intervention can expose a relation that was previously invisible. Even accurate orientation can produce new conditions that require new orientation.

Orientation is therefore recursive without becoming self-grounding.

The fact that praxis changes later orientation conditions does not mean VECTOR can predict those conditions exhaustively. It means only that an orientation theory that ignores their endogenous transformation is structurally incomplete.

21.6 Non-Finality Does Not Imply Equivalence

The final epistemic boundary returns to the first claim of the paper.

More orientation does not eliminate remainder:

\[ Orientation\uparrow \not\Rightarrow \Lambda=0. \]

A robust claim can remain vulnerable to new evidence. A bounded test burden can be closed for the current claim without the world being exhausted. A well-mapped option field can omit future emergence. A cost topology can preserve unknown downstream consequence. A rival not yet available can later outperform the current account; Appendix E.23 shows that rival superiority can also be present now and require immediate scope reduction.

Non-finality remains real.

But the converse inference still does not follow:

\[ \Lambda>0 \not\Rightarrow all\ orientations\ are\ equivalent. \]

A claim that survives relevant rotation is not epistemically identical to one that collapses immediately. A real option is not identical to an imagined one. A traceable consequence is not identical to invented hidden cost. A bounded directional relation is not identical to a preference that cannot state its loss conditions. A theory capable of non-capture discriminates differently from one that always returns its preferred answer.

Thus the argument closes on the distinction that motivated it:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Non-finality does not imply equivalence.} } \]

The point is not to recover certainty through another route. It is to preserve the possibility of justified difference under uncertainty.

21.7 What Has Become Relevantly Available Cannot Always Become Neutral Again

The practical conclusion follows only after every preceding limit remains in place.

Not everything visible is actor-accessible. Not everything actor-accessible is understood. Not everything understood is actionable. Not every actionable option is viable. Not every capacity produces obligation. Not every constraint is legitimate. Not every necessity is morally good. Not every robust orientation grants authority. Not every consequence can be known in advance.

PMS-LOGIC can already make some praxis structurally attributable where effective capacity and consequence relations exist; VECTOR does not insert an awareness condition into that baseline. Its additional claim concerns what happens when a new orientation itself enters the actor's praxis situation.

Where a VECTOR orientation is sufficiently warranted, externally supported where required, actor-accessible, understood enough for contest or response, praxis-available inside the relevant scene-time window, and connected to effective capacity and consequence, the actor's later praxis situation may become differently attributable.

\[ \boxed{ BeingTold \not\Rightarrow \Delta Attributability_{orientation}. } \]

The relation can also move in the opposite direction. Better orientation may show that an assumed option was illusory, that effective capacity was absent, that a window had already closed, that the consequence relation was under-warranted, or that relevant agency was distributed elsewhere.

What changes is therefore not necessarily the answer or the amount of blame. It is the typed relation among orientation, uptake, capacity, consequence, and subsequent praxis.

\[ \boxed{ \text{increased structural attributability} \neq \text{increased authority}. } \]

And:

\[ \boxed{ loss\ of\ structural\ innocence \neq guilt \neq blame \neq liability \neq moral\ fault. } \]

The oriented actor does not merely move through a fixed field. Where real Second-Order Agency exists, praxis can alter the field through which future orientation becomes possible; where it does not, tragedy, refusal, or non-capture may remain legitimate results.

To see more is not to know everything. What becomes structurally attributable depends on what was actually warranted, available to the actor, and effectively connected to praxis—not on the analyst's ability to say more.

Appendix A — Formal Notation

The notation in this appendix is a notation contract, not an additional theory layer. Its purpose is to stabilize claim types that have already been defined in the main text and to make later records, cases, and comparisons auditable.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Formalization clarifies claim type; it does not manufacture precision.} } \]

VECTOR remains relational and ordinal before metric. A symbol does not imply that the represented relation is measurable, globally ordered, transitive, additive, or complete. Where a later domain supports stronger formalization, that stronger formalization requires its own calibration and warrant.

A.1 VECTOR Object

VECTOR denotes the two-part core orientation architecture:

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR:=\langle DIR,ROT\rangle. } \]

A concrete VECTOR evaluation is indexed to a reference object, declared frame, question, perspectival position, and temporal position:

[
\boxed{
V_t\(O\mid F,Q,\pi\)

\langle DIR_t,RP_t\rangle.
}
]

with:

\[ \boxed{RP_t:=ROTProfile_t,\qquad ROT\neq RP.} \]

The coordinates are:

\[ O=\text{reference object}, \]
\[ F=\text{declared frame}, \]
\[ Q=\text{question / claim scope}, \]
\[ \pi=\text{perspectival position}, \]
\[ t=\text{temporal position / evaluation index}. \]

Where retrospective analysis materially separates event and later evaluation, use:

\[ t_s=\text{scene time}, \qquad t_a=\text{analysis time}. \]

For compact reference, the claim conditions may be written:

\[ C=(O,F,Q,\pi,t). \]

The core is relational and heterogeneous. It does not form a numerical vector space:

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR\neq\mathbb R^n } \]

by default.

The full geometry firewall is:

\[ \boxed{ \begin{aligned} VECTOR &\neq \text{vector space by default},\ DIR &\neq \text{signed scalar},\ ROT &\neq \text{geometric rotation},\ DIST &\neq \text{metric distance},\ DIR_R &\neq \text{vector sum}. \end{aligned} } \]

ROT need not be invertible, angle-bearing, norm-preserving, or group-composable. No norm, dot product, average, vector addition, or global ordering is implied.

DIST remains a derived supporting descriptor:

\[ \boxed{ DIST_t := \text{effective orientation--enactment separation descriptor}, \qquad DIST\notin VECTOR_{core}. } \]

It has no default unit and cannot replace the source relations that justify it.

The core type boundaries are:

\[ \boxed{ DIR\neq\nabla, \qquad ROT\neq\Phi, \qquad ROT\neq PROJECT_AS, } \]

and the supporting/index boundaries remain:

\[ \boxed{ DIST\neqΧ, \qquad \pi\neq P_{MIP}, \qquad t\neq\Theta. } \]

Warrant, evidence, calibration, residue, option fields, consequence fields, results, uptake, DIST, and stop conditions are not additional VECTOR components:

\[ \boxed{ \text{claim-support fields and derived relations} \neq \text{VECTOR components}. } \]

They may condition, qualify, or summarize a VECTOR claim without extending the core.

A.2 DIR Relations

A local directional relation may be written:

\[ \boxed{ X\prec_{V,C}Y. } \]

Read:

Under the declared VECTOR conditions (C), the relevant comparison carries direction toward (Y) over (X).

The reverse relation is:

\[ Y\prec_{V,C}X. \]

The notation does not by itself imply:

\[ v(Y)>v(X) \]

for some universal scalar (v), nor does it imply that (X) and (Y) are globally ranked.

No default transitivity is assumed:

\[ X\prec_{V,C_1}Y \land Y\prec_{V,C_2}Z \not\Rightarrow X\prec_{V,C_3}Z. \]

Even where the same symbols recur, the reference, question, frame, perspective, temporal position, or non-compensatory conditions may differ. A domain may establish a stronger order relation, but that is an additional domain claim.

The primary non-dominance outputs are represented as follows.

Trade-Off

\[ TradeOff_V(X,Y\mid C). \]

The comparison yields materially relevant directions that cannot responsibly be collapsed into one non-compensatory dominance relation.

Incomparability

\[ \boxed{ X\parallel_{V,C}Y. } \]

A meaningful comparison context exists, but no warranted dominance relation is available.

Non-Capture

\[ \boxed{ \varnothing_{V,C}. } \]

The VECTOR comparison itself should not be produced under the current claim type or conditions.

These outputs remain distinct:

\[ \boxed{ TradeOff \neq Incomparable \neq NonCapture. } \]

Incomparability does not imply equality:

\[ X\parallel_{V,C}Y \not\Rightarrow X=Y. \]

Nor does absence of rank imply equal quality, value, or human worth.

Where useful for audit, support for a DIR claim may be denoted:

\[ W(DIR\mid C). \]

(W) is not an additional VECTOR component and does not need to be numeric. It simply identifies the warrant status carried by a declared directional relation.

A.3 ROT Notation

A rotation transforms declared viewing conditions:

\[ \boxed{ ROT_{\rho}(C_0)\rightarrow C_1. } \]

The rotation family or contract is denoted (\rho). A minimal contract is:

\[ \boxed{ \rho= (\Delta C,\ C_{\text{held}},\ purpose). } \]

where:

  • (\Delta C) identifies what changes;
  • \(C_{\text{held}}\) identifies what is intended to remain sufficiently stable;
  • purpose states whether the operation primarily tests an existing claim, explores a dependency, or generates a new relation candidate.

A canonical role rotation may be represented:

\[ ROT_{role}: \pi_A\rightarrow\pi_B \]

while:

[
O,F,Q,t
]

remain sufficiently stable.

A rotation can be robustness-bearing only where enough claim-relevant conditions remain continuous for the same directional claim to be under pressure. Other rotations function primarily as exploratory probes.

Therefore:

\[ \boxed{ \text{orientation gain} \neq \text{same-claim robustness gain}. } \]

The primary ROT outcome types are:

\[ Invariant, \qquad Variant, \qquad Emergent, \qquad Collapse. \]

Invariant means bounded survival under the applied pressure, not metaphysical invariance.

Variant means the claim changes in direction, scope, form, or strength.

Emergent means a new relation becomes visible and must itself be typed and tested.

Collapse means the original DIR no longer carries under the relevant rotation.

A split or trade-off may occur as a particular form of variation or emergence and then be typed at the DIR level.

ROT must retain genuine loss capacity:

\[ \boxed{ ROT\text{ must be capable of reducing the claim it tests.} } \]

However, loss capacity is not a requirement that the result be negative. It is a requirement that a negative result remain possible.

A.4 DIST and Reversibility

DIST is retained as a derived supporting descriptor of effective orientation–enactment separation:

\[ \boxed{ DIST_t := \text{effective orientation–enactment separation at }t, \qquad DIST\notin VECTOR_{core}. } \]

DIST is not elapsed time, reflective sophistication, or the PMS operator (Χ) (distance). Its use must remain traceable to the actual source relations that generate the separation.

\[ \boxed{ DIST\neqΧ. } \]

DIST may be role-relative:

\[ DIST_A(t)\neq DIST_B(t) \]

within the same scene.

Available reversibility is represented separately:

\[ \boxed{ REV_{avail}(a,t\mid F,Q) } \]

and denotes the remaining practical correction, redirection, exit, modification, or partial-recovery budget relevant to actor or role (a).

The distinction is strict:

\[ \boxed{ DIST\neq REV_{avail}. } \]

Reversibility is path-dependent and must not be equated with restoration:

\[ \boxed{ reversibility \neq restoration. } \]

A trajectory may remain corrigible without allowing return to its prior state.

Rotation cost and reversal cost are also distinct:

\[ \boxed{ C_{ROT}\neq C_{REV}. } \]

\(C_{ROT}\) denotes practical friction involved in reorientation.

\(C_{REV}\) denotes cost involved in materially changing already enacted or bound praxis.

Neither DIST nor \(REV_{avail}\) is a default quality scale:

\[ DIST\uparrow\not\Rightarrow adequacy\uparrow, \]
\[ REV_{avail}\uparrow\not\Rightarrow praxis\ quality\uparrow. \]

Where ordinal comparison is justified, it remains local to the declared question and conditions.

A.5 Option Space

A bounded praxeological option space for actor or role (a) at time (t) may be written:

\[ \boxed{ \mathcal O_{a,t}(O\mid F,Q). } \]

VECTOR distinguishes several option-status predicates:

\[ Conceivable_a(o,t), \]
\[ Visible_a(o,t), \]
\[ Available_a(o,t\mid F), \]
\[ Actionable_a(o,t), \]
\[ Viable_a(o,t\mid G,Q). \]

These predicates are not interchangeable:

\[ \boxed{ conceivable \neq visible \neq available \neq actionable \neq viable. } \]

No automatic implication ladder is assumed. A real option can be unavailable to the current actor, an available option can be invisible, and a visible option can be illusory.

Option status and evidence status are separate. A retrospective or under-evidenced analysis may therefore record:

\[ \boxed{ Unknown(Actionable_a(o,t)). } \]

with:

\[ \boxed{ Unknown\neq Unavailable, \qquad Unknown\neq Available. } \]

For Actionable, relevant evidence can include authority, access, means, information, time, dependencies, practical control, cost, and procedural permission.

Branch status may be represented through the labels:

\[ Realized(o), \quad Rejected(o), \quad Blocked(o), \quad Deferred(o), \quad Lost(o). \]

Rejected presupposes that the option was sufficiently real and present in the relevant choice field. Therefore:

\[ \boxed{ not\ chosen \neq rejected. } \]

Likewise:

\[ \boxed{ deferred \neq preserved. } \]

Option Invisibility is the paradigmatic legibility relation:

\[ Available_a(o,t) \land \neg Visible_a(o,t). \]

Option Invisibility records a divergence between option status and current legibility. It does not by itself assign an option-field error.

Option Blindness records unsupported exclusion from the relevant current or realistically transformable option field:

\[ \boxed{ OptionBlindness_a(o,t\mid F,Q) := UnsupportedExclusion_a(o,t\mid F,Q). } \]

Option Illusion records unsupported inclusion in the relevant effective option field:

\[ \boxed{ OptionIllusion_a(o,t\mid F,Q) := UnsupportedInclusion_a(o,t\mid F,Q). } \]

Accordingly:

[
\boxed{
OptionFieldMisrepresentation

OptionBlindness
\lor
OptionIllusion.
}
]

Unsupported means that the represented option-status assignment goes beyond what the declared frame, role, time, capacity, dependency, and evidence conditions currently warrant. The notation does not presuppose access to a complete or final option field. Blindness and Illusion are opposite directions of status error, not claims that their object ranges are otherwise identical: Blindness may concern unsupported exclusion of a current effective possibility or a realistically transformable one, while Illusion concerns unsupported inclusion in the relevant effective option field.

The evidence-status boundary remains active in both directions. Converting Unknown into an unsupported negative status can generate Option Blindness; converting it into an unsupported positive effective-option status can generate Option Illusion.

A special-case probe under an altered frame may produce:

\[ o\in\mathcal O(F^*) \]

without licensing:

\[ o\in\mathcal O(F_0). \]

Hence:

\[ \boxed{ o\in\mathcal O(F^*) \not\Rightarrow o\in\mathcal O(F_0). } \]

At the same time:

\[ \boxed{ invalid\ as\ option \not\Rightarrow invalid\ as\ probe. } \]

A.6 Option Relations

Where relation structure matters, the option field may be represented as:

\[ \boxed{ \mathcal O_t=(N_t,R_{O,t}), } \]

where \(N_t\) is the set of currently relevant option nodes and \(R_{O,t}\) the set of relevant relations among them.

Useful relation predicates include:

\[ enables(o_i,o_j), \]
\[ blocks(o_i,o_j), \]
\[ requires(o_i,o_j), \]
\[ precedes(o_i,o_j), \]
\[ excludes(o_i,o_j), \]
\[ raisesCost(o_i,o_j), \]
\[ closes(o_i,o_j). \]

These relations are open rather than exhaustive. A particular application may require other typed relations.

Second-order agency can alter node status:

\[ N_t\rightarrow N_{t+1}, \]

relation structure:

\[ R_{O,t}\rightarrow R_{O,t+1}, \]

or both.

A relation change can be praxeologically material even where the option count remains constant:

\[ N_t=N_{t+1} \]

while:

\[ R_{O,t}\neq R_{O,t+1}. \]

This notation supports analysis; it does not claim complete graph representation:

\[ \boxed{ \text{analytical graphability} \neq \text{complete graph capture}. } \]

A.7 Cost Topology

The relevant consequence and cost field at time (t) may be denoted:

\[ \boxed{ \mathcal K_t. } \]

A specific cost relation may be recorded schematically as:

[
K(bearer,t,type,source,status).
]

The standard cost types used in the paper are:

[
Direct,
]

[
NonAction,
]

[
OpportunityLoss,
]

[
Window,
]

[
Binding,
]

[
Reversal,
]

[
Externalized,
]

[
RelationChange,
]

[
Downstream.
]

The status field may distinguish, where useful:

  • Observed / directly established;
  • Externally supported;
  • Traceably inferred;
  • Hypothetical;
  • Unknown / residual (\Lambda) (non-event/remainder).

These statuses are epistemic bookkeeping, not a universal confidence scale. A claim-critical cost should retain its bearer, temporal relation, mechanism/source, and evidence status.

Two boundaries are mandatory:

\[ \boxed{ unseen\ consequence \neq zero\ consequence, } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ unseen\ consequence \neq permission\ to\ invent\ consequence. } \]

Cost types are non-fungible by default. VECTOR supplies no universal total-cost function:

\[ \boxed{ \mathcal K_t \neq TotalCostScore. } \]

Any domain-specific aggregation requires additional warrant.

A.8 Ought Notation

VECTOR distinguishes several proposition types that ordinary language may compress into the word ought. The notation is retained only under the following firewall:

OUGHT_* denotes typed must-like relations in ordinary-language space; it is not a moral operator family.

A choice command is represented:

[
\boxed{
OUGHT_{choice}(X)

\text{Choose }X.
}
]

An accountability relation is represented:

[
\boxed{
OUGHT_{account}(R)

\text{relation }R\text{ must remain accounted for in subsequent praxis}.
}
]

A constraint-relative practical necessity is represented:

[
\boxed{
OUGHT_{constraint}\(G,R_G\)

\text{if }G\text{ remains operative, a response in }R_G\text{ is required}.
}
]

A categorical or substantive moral requirement may be denoted:

\[ OUGHT_{moral}(X). \]

The types are not interchangeable:

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{choice} \neq OUGHT_{account} \neq OUGHT_{constraint} \neq OUGHT_{moral}. } \]

In particular:

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{constraint}(G,R_G) \not\Rightarrow OUGHT_{moral}(R_G). } \]

Likewise, accountability does not itself select an action:

\[ OUGHT_{account}(R) \not\Rightarrow OUGHT_{choice}(X). \]

The full typological discussion appears in Appendix D. Appendix A fixes only the notation used throughout the paper.

A.9 Stop and Non-Capture

VECTOR distinguishes insufficient current warrant from a case in which the VECTOR operation itself should not be applied.

Suspension may be denoted:

\[ \boxed{ Suspend_V(C). } \]

This means that the current claim cannot responsibly be carried at the requested strength under the present warrant or unresolved residue.

Non-capture may be denoted:

\[ \boxed{ NonCapture_V(C)=\varnothing_{V,C}. } \]

This means that producing the requested VECTOR comparison would itself violate the relevant claim type, reference continuity, boundary, or non-capture requirement.

Therefore:

\[ \boxed{ Suspend_V(C) \neq NonCapture_V(C). } \]

Suspension may be revisited if relevant conditions change. Non-capture states that the current requested operation should not be forced.

A stop condition records why the current iteration ends. A stop may follow from bounded test-burden closure, suspension, non-capture, insufficient external warrant, or another explicitly typed boundary.

No stop symbol confers validity by itself.

\[ \boxed{ \text{formal stop} \neq \text{validated claim}. } \]

Appendix B — Minimal VECTOR Record

The VECTOR Record is an audit device. It makes the conditions, pressures, revisions, calibration dependencies, and boundaries of a VECTOR claim inspectable without pretending that a completed form validates the claim.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Record completeness} \neq \text{claim correctness}. } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ RecordRichness \not\Rightarrow WarrantStrength. } \]

The record is intentionally claim-relative. Fields should be used when the claim type makes them material; auditability must not become bureaucratic inflation.

B.1 Human-Readable Record

Reference and Claim Conditions

Object: What is being evaluated?

Object type: Claim, reconstruction, trajectory, relation, option field, praxis scene, or another explicitly typed reference.

Frame: What is included and excluded?

Question / scope: What directional or orientational question is being asked?

Claim ceiling: What stronger conclusion is not licensed?

Perspective (\pi): From which declared position is the evaluation made?

Temporal position (t): At what evaluation state?

Where retrospective analysis matters, distinguish:

Scene time \(t_s\): When did the actor-facing praxis conditions obtain?

Analysis time \(t_a\): When is the later analysis being performed?

\[ \boxed{ Legibility_{analyst}(t_a) \not\Rightarrow Legibility_{actor}(t_s). } \]

Baseline Claim

\(S_0\): What proposition is actually being tested?

The baseline must remain stable enough to lose. Later revision should not overwrite it.

DIR

Relation: What bounded direction, trade-off, incomparability, or absence of DIR is currently claimed?

Comparison basis: Which structural difference carries the comparison?

Warrant source: What actually supports the claim—supplied evidence, source-carried structure, domain premise, comparison criterion, reference continuity, rival/counter-scene pressure, or an external empirical/legal/technical/historical/causal premise?

External premises: Which material premises are not generated by VECTOR itself?

Unresolved dependencies: Which downstream conclusions depend on a premise still under-warranted?

Critical regressions: Which losses would invalidate or materially reduce the claim?

Calibration

Record only thresholds material to the claim.

Threshold: Which distinction depends on a threshold?

Basis: What supports that threshold?

Domain source: Is calibration imported from a domain-specific source?

Competing calibration: Is another defensible threshold in play?

Unresolved: Does the analysis remain calibration-divergent?

Calibration disagreement may remain a legitimate result. The record should expose its basis rather than force false convergence.

ROT Profile (RP)

For each relevant rotation record:

  • family;
  • vulnerability being tested;
  • purpose;
  • changed conditions;
  • held-constant conditions;
  • reference continuity;
  • material distinctness from prior pressure;
  • credible route to claim loss;
  • result;
  • same-claim robustness relevance.

Also record:

Known materially relevant ROT not yet applied.

Stop basis: Why are remaining rotations redundant, irrelevant, externally blocked, or scope-changing rather than claim-critical?

The stored record is an RP (ROT Profile), not ROT itself.

Derived DIST

DIST is optional supporting shorthand rather than a core field.

If used, record:

Effective separation: What low-cost correction remains before further consolidation?

Source relations: Which (Χ) (distance), (Θ) (temporality), (Ψ) (self-binding), (Ω) (asymmetry), (Α) (attractor), publicness, option, capacity, or reversibility relations justify the shorthand?

Role variation: Does the separation differ across roles?

A DIST label without its source relations adds no warrant.

Reversibility

Available reversibility: What correction, exit, redirection, or partial-recovery budget remains?

Rotation cost \(C_{ROT}\): What practical friction attaches to reorientation?

Reversal cost \(C_{REV}\): What cost attaches to changing enacted or bound praxis?

Restoration: Is restoration possible, or only correction without reset?

Option Field

For each material option, keep option status, branch status where relevant, and evidence status distinct.

Option-status predicates include:

  • conceivable;
  • visible;
  • structurally available;
  • effectively actionable;
  • structurally viable.

Branch status, where material, includes:

  • realized;
  • rejected;
  • blocked;
  • deferred;
  • lost.

Evidence may establish a status, support only an inference, or leave it Unknown.

For actionability, inspect where relevant:

  • authority;
  • access;
  • means/resources;
  • information;
  • time/window;
  • dependencies on other actors;
  • practical control;
  • relevant cost;
  • procedural permission.

Use viability relationally, e.g. (Viable_a\(o,t\mid G,Q\)), rather than as a free-standing quality label.

Also record, where material:

  • Option Invisibility as a legibility condition;
  • Option Blindness or Option Illusion as Option-Field Misrepresentation;
  • the specific unsupported exclusion or inclusion claimed;
  • Special-Case Probes separately from effective option status.

Transformability / Second-Order Agency

A material transformation claim should identify:

  1. actor or actor configuration;
  2. target change;
  3. mechanism;
  4. effective authority/capacity;
  5. dependencies on other roles;
  6. temporal window;
  7. evidence;
  8. resulting node or relation change (\(\Delta N,\Delta R_O\)).

A merely conceivable transformation remains a probe.

Consequence Topology

For each claim-critical consequence or cost record:

  • type;
  • bearer;
  • temporal relation;
  • mechanism/source;
  • evidence status.

Recommended evidence statuses are:

  • Observed / directly established;
  • Externally supported;
  • Traceably inferred;
  • Hypothetical;
  • Unknown / residual.

Unknown residue remains (\Lambda) (non-event/remainder); it is not a positive hidden-cost assertion.

Constraint Record

Use this section only for constraint-level claims.

Record:

  • (G);
  • maintained by;
  • modifiable by;
  • claimed authority over (G);
  • burden bearers;
  • exit relations;
  • alternative carriers;
  • evidenced transformation routes;
  • consequences of abandoning/revising (G);
  • unknowns;
  • external legitimacy question.
\[ \boxed{ SystemRequirement \not\Rightarrow PersonDuty. } \]

Orientation-Uptake Record

Use this only where orientation-mediated attribution increase is claimed.

Record whether the orientation was:

  • presented;
  • actor-accessible;
  • understood well enough for contest or response;
  • praxis-available inside the relevant window;
  • enacted, rejected, contested, or otherwise carried.
\[ \boxed{ BeingTold \neq Uptake. } \]

Do not use this field to erase baseline structural attributability already supported independently under PMS-LOGIC.

Pressure and Result

Rivals: What serious rival can materially change or defeat the claim in its own vocabulary?

Counter-scenes / counter-positions: Which alternative configurations expose possible non-discrimination?

Provenance dependencies: Which reasons depend on the same source or premise?

Non-compensation: Which losses cannot be offset by unrelated strengths?

Critical open tests: What known claim-critical burden remains untested?

Use one typed result where applicable:

  • Robust Orientation;
  • Revised Robust Orientation;
  • Bounded Orientation;
  • Revision Required;
  • Trade-Off / Multidirectional;
  • Incomparable;
  • Suspend;
  • Non-Capture.

Residue and Stop Condition

\[ \Lambda_{remaining}. \]

What remains unknown, calibration-divergent, externally dependent, or non-captured?

Why may the current test iteration responsibly end?

Or:

Why must the claim be suspended or refused?

B.2 YAML Representation

The following is a compact reference, not a validation schema.

  constraint:
    G:
    maintained_by:
    modifiable_by:
    claimed_authority:
    burden_bearers:
    exit_relations:
    alternative_carriers:
    transformation_routes:
    abandon_or_revision_consequences:
    unknowns:
    external_legitimacy_question:

  orientation_uptake:
    presented:
    actor_accessible:
    understood:
    praxis_available:
    actor_response:

  pressure:
    rivals:
    counter_scenes:
    provenance_dependencies:
    non_compensation:
    critical_open_tests:

  result:
    status:
    surviving_claim:
    revisions:
    scope:
    residue:
    stop_reason:

The schema intentionally contains no default fields for numeric DIR (Direction) score, rotation-count score, DIST (Distance) percentage, Responsibility score, Adultness score, or universal confidence percentage. A domain may separately earn a metric; the record does not manufacture one.

B.3 Claim and Boundary Fields

Requiredness follows claim strength and type.

A result labeled Robust Orientation is not sufficiently audited without, at minimum, a declared baseline \(S_0\), materially relevant ROT (Rotation) pressure, serious rival pressure, declared scope, relevant residue, warrant-source visibility, and a stop reason. If a material threshold is disputed, its calibration basis must also remain visible.

An orientation-mediated attribution claim additionally requires the uptake/capacity/consequence bridge developed in Part V. A constraint claim additionally requires the constraint record. A retrospective actor-level claim requires the scene-time / analysis-time distinction where material.

By contrast, a Non-Capture result may stop earlier. If reference continuity, claim type, or the requested comparison already fails, VECTOR need not fabricate a full cost or option map merely to complete the record.

The central boundaries are:

\[ \boxed{ RecordIncomplete \neq ClaimFalse. } \]
\[ \boxed{ SchemaValid(record) \not\Rightarrow VECTORValid(claim). } \]
\[ \boxed{ RecordRichness \not\Rightarrow WarrantStrength. } \]

The record measures inspectability and dependency visibility, not truth.

Appendix C — Rotation Families

Appendix C is a reference catalogue for ROT (Rotation), not a second theory of rotation. Chapter 5 owns the theory. This appendix supplies default family descriptions, admissibility conditions, pseudo-rotation warnings, and a compact distinction between exploratory and robustness-bearing use.

Rotation families are:

\[ \boxed{ open\ but\ typed. } \]

The list below is therefore not exhaustive. A new rotation family is admissible only if its change, constants, reference relation, purpose, and loss capacity can be declared.

C.1 Rotation Reference Table

ROT family Changed Ideally constant Primary question Typical function
Role (\pi) (O,F,Q,t) Does DIR depend on role position? robustness
Actor / Affected (\pi) scene / (Q) Who bears effect, exposure, or cost? robustness / discovery
Inside / Outside access or relational position reference Does internal coherence hide external consequence or dependency? mixed
Intention / Effect focal relation scene / time Does the effect relation diverge from declared or supportable intention? mixed
Temporal temporal cut (O,Q) where possible Does DIR depend on periodization? probe / conditional robustness
(\Omega)-position asymmetry / exposure position scene Is the orientation sensitive to where capacity, cost, or exposure lies? robustness / discovery
Counterfactual declared condition explicit reference relation Which condition carries the result? probe
Rival-model model / frame reference / question Does another model discriminate the relevant structure better? robustness / rival pressure
Granularity resolution reference / question Is DIR dependent on level of resolution? conditional

The Typical function column is guidance, not deterministic classification.

A temporal rotation may test the same claim where temporal position is itself the declared pressure and enough reference continuity remains. In another case, changing the temporal cut may create a new bounded claim and therefore function only as a probe.

The Rotation Contract decides.

C.2 Admissibility Conditions

A serious ROT requires at least:

\[ \boxed{ DeclaredChange + DeclaredConstants + ReferenceContinuity + QuestionRelevance + LossCapacity. } \]

Purpose should also be recorded operationally.

The positive selection rule is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Rotation selection follows claim vulnerability, not desired outcome.} } \]

A robustness-bearing ROT (Rotation) should therefore have: claim relevance, sufficient reference continuity, material distinctness from already applied pressure, a credible route to claim loss, and an explicit reason why this rotation matters to this claim. Distinctness is functional rather than numerical: a differently worded perspective that tests the same dependency is not automatically new pressure.

Declared Change

The record must state what actually changes.

Examples:

\[ \pi_A\rightarrow\pi_B, \]
\[ t_0\rightarrow t_1, \]
\[ F_0\rightarrow F^*, \]

or:

\[ model_A\rightarrow model_B. \]

An operation whose changed condition cannot be named may still be analytically interesting, but it is not yet a well-typed ROT.

Declared Constants

The record must state which conditions are intended to remain sufficiently stable for the current claim.

For a role rotation this may be:

[
O,F,Q,t.
]

For a rival-model rotation the model itself changes while the reference and question should remain sufficiently comparable.

Declared constants need not be perfectly identical. They must be stable enough that claim continuity or claim change can be judged rather than assumed.

Reference Continuity

ROT must preserve enough relation to the relevant (O) for the result to bear on the intended claim.

If:

\[ O_0\rightarrow O_1 \]

materially changes what is being evaluated, the result should normally be typed as a new bounded claim rather than silently counted as robustness evidence about \(O_0\).

Question Relevance

A rotation is useful only if the change can materially bear on (Q).

Difference alone is insufficient.

A perspective can be novel, dramatic, or socially salient while remaining irrelevant to the actual directional claim.

Loss Capacity

A robustness-bearing ROT must be able, in principle, to produce:

  • weakening;
  • reversal;
  • scope reduction;
  • trade-off;
  • incomparability;
  • collapse;
  • non-capture.

Thus:

\[ \boxed{ \text{negative result must remain possible}. } \]

Loss capacity does not mean that the rotation must damage the claim. It means the test is not designed so that survival is guaranteed.

Purpose

The record should state whether the ROT is intended primarily to:

  • test same-claim robustness;
  • discover a relation;
  • expose dependency;
  • generate a counter-scene;
  • pressure a frame;
  • compare a rival account.

Purpose does not determine the result. It clarifies how the result may legitimately be used.

Granularity Boundary

If a granularity change materially retypes the object rather than pressuring the same claim, it becomes an exploratory probe or a STRATA-relevant analytical transformation. It cannot count as same-claim robustness merely because the word granularity appears in the ROT (Rotation) family table.

Stop Criterion

ROT (Rotation) testing may stop when known claim-critical vulnerabilities have been materially pressured and remaining rotations are redundant or irrelevant to the declared claim; when further pressure depends on unavailable external evidence; when a stronger test would require a new claim scope; or when the current result is already Suspend, Non-Capture, or Revision Required.

\[ \boxed{ \text{bounded stop} \neq \text{exhaustion of all possible perspectives}. } \]

C.3 Invalid / Pseudo-Rotation

The following patterns should not be counted as clean robustness-bearing ROT.

Pseudo-rotation Failure
Reference substitution A materially different (O) is introduced and described as merely another perspective on the original reference.
Frame laundering A claim-critical condition is removed in \(F^*\), and the resulting orientation is imported back into \(F_0\) as if the original frame had supported it.
Harmless rotation Only perspectives are selected under which the claim has little realistic possibility of loss.
Straw-rival rotation The rival is specified so weakly or translated so completely into the current framework that it cannot genuinely win.
Special-case laundering An option or relation valid only under altered conditions is treated as though it existed under the original conditions.
Retrospective import Information available only later is inserted into an earlier temporal state and treated as though it had been available then.
Perspective-count inflation The number of performed rotations is treated as a robustness metric.
Rotation without contract What changed, what remained stable, or what the operation was intended to test is not declared.

Special-case laundering can be written:

\[ F^* \rightarrow claim(F_0), \]

where the arrow represents an invalid back-import rather than a legitimate inference.

Retrospective import is especially important where option status, effective capacity, or responsibility is at issue:

\[ knowledge_{t_1} \not\Rightarrow knowledge_{t_0}. \]

Perspective-count inflation is invalid because:

\[ \boxed{ n(ROT)\uparrow \not\Rightarrow Robustness\uparrow. } \]

Ten highly correlated harmless rotations can carry less pressure than one materially distinct affected-role or rival-model rotation.

A pseudo-rotation may still generate useful orientation. The classification concerns what the operation can count as evidence for the original claim.

C.4 Probe versus Robustness Matrix

ROT type Orientation gain Same-claim robustness
Role, strong continuity yes potentially yes
Actor / affected-role yes potentially yes
(\Omega)-position yes potentially yes
Rival model yes yes, if the comparison remains bounded and reference/question continuity is sufficient
Inside / outside yes conditional
Intention / effect yes conditional
Counterfactual special case yes usually no
Large frame change yes generally no
Granularity shift yes conditional
Temporal cut change yes conditional

The matrix states typical functions, not immutable categories.

A rotation can produce orientation gain by revealing a previously invisible dependency, consequence, constraint, or relation even where the original claim cannot count the result as same-claim robustness evidence.

Hence:

\[ \boxed{ \text{orientation gain} \neq \text{same-claim robustness gain}. } \]

The converse also matters. Same-claim robustness is not obtained by accumulating rotations without regard to their material distinctness, relevance, or loss capacity:

\[ \boxed{ rotation\ count \neq rotational\ robustness. } \]

Appendix C therefore ends where Chapter 5 began: perspective becomes epistemically useful only when its change remains typed strongly enough to tell whether a claim survived, changed, generated something new, or ceased to carry.

Appendix D — Ought Typology

Appendix D is a reference typology, not a second derivation of the argument developed in Chapter 15. Its purpose is to make the claim type carried by an ought-like statement immediately inspectable.

\[ \boxed{ \text{reference typology} \neq \text{normative derivation}. } \]

The distinction matters because ordinary language uses ought, must, required, responsible, and necessary across several structurally different relations. VECTOR does not treat these expressions as interchangeable.

The four central types are:

\[ OUGHT_{choice}, \qquad OUGHT_{account}, \qquad OUGHT_{constraint}, \qquad OUGHT_{moral}. \]

Only the middle two are positive VECTOR claim types developed by the paper. OUGHT_choice is included to mark what does not automatically follow from a directional result. OUGHT_moral is included to mark the boundary beyond which VECTOR alone does not supply sufficient warrant.

After the Part V publicness and misuse stress test, the notation is retained with a canonical firewall rather than replaced by neutral aliases:

OUGHT_* denotes typed must-like relations in ordinary-language space; it is not a moral operator family.

The notation earns retention only while the types remain discriminable and the formula cannot bypass warrant, uptake, capacity, constraint-source, or legitimacy boundaries.

D.1 OUGHT_choice

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{choice}(X)=\text{Choose }X. } \]

OUGHT_choice is an outcome-directed prescriptive claim. It selects an action, option, response, or policy as the one to be chosen.

VECTOR does not derive this form directly from DIR.

Even where:

\[ X\prec_VY, \]

it does not follow that:

\[ OUGHT_{choice}(Y). \]

The same remains true after substantial testing:

\[ RobustOrientation(Y) \not\Rightarrow OUGHT_{choice}(Y). \]

A robust directional claim may establish that (Y) better carries a declared comparison than (X), that a consequence relation is more adequately preserved, or that one reconstruction survives relevant rotation while another collapses. None of these facts alone supplies every premise required for an outcome-directed prescription.

This is especially important where:

  • several response classes remain viable;
  • a relevant constraint may itself be revisable;
  • the costs of a response are non-fungible;
  • authority to decide is distributed or absent;
  • legitimacy requires external normative, legal, political, institutional, or domain-specific premises.

OUGHT_choice is therefore useful primarily as a boundary type inside VECTOR.

It names the inference that must not be smuggled in merely because a directional or practical relation has become strong.

D.2 OUGHT_account

[
\boxed{
OUGHT_{account}(R)

\text{R must remain accounted for in subsequent praxis.}
}
]

This is a VECTOR orientation-mediated relation, not a replacement for the baseline responsibility logic carried by PMS-LOGIC. PMS-LOGIC can support structural attributability where effective capacity and consequence relation exist even without actor awareness. VECTOR's additional claim is narrower: orientation can change that attribution only when the orientation actually enters the actor's praxis situation under the relevant conditions.

Thus:

\[ \boxed{ BaselineAttributability_{LOGIC} \neq \Delta Attributability_{orientation}. } \]

For the orientation-mediated increase, the dependency is at least:

[
RelevantOrientation
+
ExternalPremiseSupport
+
ActorUptake
+
EffectiveCapacity
+
ConsequenceRelation
+
Role/TimeConditions.
]

The formula is a dependency boundary, not a deductive algorithm. A material calibration threshold may remain open.

ActorUptake requires more than presentation. The relevant chain is:

\[ Orientation_{presented} \neq Orientation_{actor\text{-}accessible} \neq Orientation_{understood} \neq Orientation_{praxis\text{-}available} \neq Orientation_{enacted}. \]

Praxis-available does not mean agreement. It means that the relation has become sufficiently available for contest, verification, rejection, refusal, or action inside the material window.

\[ \boxed{ BeingTold(R) \not\Rightarrow ActorUptake(R). } \]

Where the orientation-mediated conditions carry, the technical primary conclusion is increased structural attributability. The older phrase loss of structural innocence may be used only as secondary shorthand.

\[ \boxed{ loss\ of\ structural\ innocence \neq guilt \neq blame \neq liability \neq moral\ fault. } \]

And:

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{account}(R) \not\Rightarrow OUGHT_{choice}(X). } \]

The actor may dispute the warrant, expose a stronger rival, show that capacity was absent, or choose another response while still carrying the relevant relation explicitly.

D.3 OUGHT_constraint

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{constraint}(G,R_G). } \]

Read:

While (G) remains operative, subsequent praxis must fall within a response class \(R_G\) that still satisfies the relevant constraint.

Schematically:

\[ R_G \subseteq \mathcal O^{effective}_t. \]

If several responses remain viable, VECTOR may reconstruct a restricted response class without selecting among them. If only one response remains effective, that response is practically necessary for maintaining (G) under the current conditions.

But:

\[ \boxed{ required\ for\ G \neq morally\ required. } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ Source(G) \neq Legitimacy(G). } \]

A strong constraint claim is incomplete if it hides the assignment structure. Where material, the record must expose:

  1. who maintains (G);
  2. who can revise (G);
  3. who claims authority over (G);
  4. who bears the costs of maintaining (G);
  5. who possesses exit;
  6. which alternative carriers or mechanisms exist;
  7. which Second-Order Agency routes are actually evidenced;
  8. what happens if (G) is abandoned or revised;
  9. what remains unknown;
  10. which legitimacy question remains external to VECTOR.

This blocks the laundering of system-level necessity into a person-level assignment:

\[ \boxed{ SystemCarriageRequired \neq PersonXMustCarry. } \]

OUGHT_constraint remains conditional. Its content changes if (G) changes:

\[ Revision(G) \Rightarrow \text{new constraint claim}. \]

That possibility does not make revision costless. Abandoning or modifying a commitment, continuity requirement, institutional function, or (Ψ) (self-binding) is itself praxis and may alter option status, role relations, cost topology, downstream dependencies, carriage distribution, and reversal cost.

The conditional structure therefore avoids both constraint naturalization and constraint trivialization. VECTOR can reconstruct that a constraint is operative; it cannot derive the constraint's moral, legal, political, or institutional legitimacy from that fact.

D.4 Tragic Constraint

Tragic Constraint is not a fourth ought type.

It is a configuration of OUGHT_constraint in which every currently effective response compatible with the maintained constraint carries relevant residual loss and no currently evidenced effective transformation is available within the declared window, capacity field, and dependency structure.

Schematically:

\[ \forall o\in R_G: ResidualLoss(o), \]

with:

\[ \neg EvidencedEffectiveTransformation_{t_s}(F,G,C_{eff}) \]

used only as a compact notation for the declared evidential status, not as an ontological claim that no transformation could ever exist.

Then:

\[ \boxed{ TragicConstraint(G,R_G). } \]

The bounded negative is admissible only after relevant Option Invisibility and unsupported exclusion have received pressure appropriate to the claim, including real option-space transformability where material. A candidate route defeats the tragic classification only if its relevant option status is itself supported; merely constructing an escape route does not bypass Option Illusion. Conversely, this burden does not require the analyst to fabricate another alternative where no effective route is evidenced.

The claim remains falsifiable by a newly evidenced real option, a newly evidenced transformation route, a materially different capacity distribution, a reopened window, or a rival account showing that the constraint field was misdescribed. Imagination alone does not falsify it; nor does missing imagination justify it.

\[ \boxed{ \text{currently no evidenced non-lossful route} \neq \text{ontological impossibility}. } \]

Tragic Constraint still does not establish that (G) is legitimate, that burden is fairly distributed, that any particular carrier is morally obligated, or that the least-bad available response is morally good.

D.5 Moral-Conversion Boundary

The typology can be summarized compactly.

Claim type Structural relation? Selects outcome? Moral authority from VECTOR?
DIR yes no no
OUGHT_account yes no no
OUGHT_constraint yes conditionally restricts no
OUGHT_choice prescriptive yes not from VECTOR alone
OUGHT_moral normative yes / possibly not from VECTOR alone

Four boundaries carry the table:

\[ \boxed{ capacity \neq obligation } \]
\[ \boxed{ operative\ constraint \neq legitimate\ constraint } \]
\[ \boxed{ practical\ necessity \neq moral\ goodness } \]
\[ \boxed{ FixpointRequirement \neq BearerAuthority. } \]

These are not after-the-fact ethical caveats. They determine what kind of claim VECTOR is making.

A structural necessity can be real while its source remains illegitimate.

A constraint can be legitimate while one proposed distribution of its costs remains unfair.

A functional fixpoint can be required while several possible carriers or procedures remain open.

An actor can possess capacity without thereby acquiring authority over others.

The boundary therefore protects both sides of the theory:

\[ \boxed{ \text{No moral automation} \neq \text{no practical consequence}. } \]

D.6 Constraint Source

Constraint source changes interpretation, but it does not determine legitimacy.

Four recurring source types are useful.

Constraint source Notation Minimal description
Self-bound \(G_{self}\) operative through the actor's maintained commitment or self-binding
Role-bound \(G_{role}\) operative through the functional requirements of a role
Infrastructure-bound \(G_{infra}\) operative because continuity or carriage is required for a system to continue functioning
Externally imposed \(G_{external}\) operative through law, power, coercion, institution, dependency, or another actor

Mixed forms are normal.

A self-bound goal may also be institutionally embedded.

A role-bound requirement may exist because infrastructure depends on it.

An externally imposed constraint may later be internalized through self-binding.

The typology therefore does not imply mutually exclusive causal classes.

Its main rule is:

\[ \boxed{ Source(G) \neq Legitimacy(G). } \]

A self-binding can be badly grounded.

An external constraint can be legitimate.

An infrastructure requirement can be real while its load is distributed unjustly.

A role requirement can be formally assigned while the role itself lacks real capacity.

Carriage requires one further distinction:

\[ \boxed{ SystemCarriageRequired \neq PersonXMustCarry. } \]

A system may require that some continuity function be carried. That does not identify a unique morally authorized bearer. Before a strong carriage or constraint claim is carried forward, the record should therefore expose maintainer, modifier, claimed authority, burden bearer, exit, alternative carrier, transformation route, and the external legitimacy question.

This is the appropriate stopping point for Appendix D. The typology tells the reader what kind of ought-like claim is present. It does not decide the moral question on the reader's behalf.

Appendix E — Minimal Cases and Countercases

Appendix E is a discriminative test suite, not an illustration gallery.

Each case places one or two VECTOR distinctions under pressure and asks whether the architecture produces a different output when the structure changes. The cases are intentionally minimal. They do not attempt to model the full empirical complexity of real institutions, conflicts, relationships, or public decisions.

Two formats are used.

Full Minimal Case develops enough structure to show why the output changes.

Micro-Countercase isolates a single tempting misclassification and the correction VECTOR must make.

The test is successful only if structurally different cases can produce structurally different outputs.

\[ \boxed{ \text{example count} \neq \text{theoretical warrant}. } \]

The cases test discrimination. They do not prove the theory by accumulation.

A marked adversarial subset carries a stronger burden. In these cases, VECTOR must expose a credible route to loss rather than merely illustrate one of its preferred distinctions. The explicit adversarial set in this revision is:

  • E.1 — DIR survives a serious rival with a credible route to win;
  • E.4 — the result remains Trade-Off rather than being forced into a total order;
  • E.11 — a plausible Second-Order Agency claim collapses under mechanism and authority audit;
  • E.21 — a serious domain-specific rival wins a specialist technical burden and VECTOR yields locally;
  • E.22 — DIST fails its standalone necessity test and is reduced from the core;
  • E.23 — a non-PMS rival addresses the same bounded orientation burden and forces DIR/ROT/standalone scope reduction.

A test set that contains only VECTOR victories would not test the architecture claimed in Chapter 19.

E.1 Rotation Confirms DIR under Serious Rival Pressure

Type: Full Minimal Case — Adversarial A

Baseline claim

A small organization must choose between two incident-reporting procedures after a recurring safety problem.

Procedure (X) records only incidents that caused immediate operational failure. Procedure (Y) records failures and near-misses, preserves which roles were exposed, and keeps the report open to later correction when additional information arrives.

The reference is the reporting procedure, the frame is the current safety-review process, and the declared question is narrow:

Which procedure better preserves the consequence and exposure structure needed for later corrective learning without creating a claim-critical reporting burden?

Under the initial operations perspective:

\[ X\prec_{V,C}Y. \]

Warrant and vulnerability

The baseline warrant is not the fact that (Y) resembles preferred PMS language. It depends on observed reporting completeness, whether role-specific exposure remains recoverable, whether the additional reporting load suppresses participation, and whether the retained information is actually used in later correction.

The claim would lose if the extra near-miss layer materially reduced reporting quality, produced enough burden to hide rather than reveal hazards, or if a simpler rival preserved the same corrective information more reliably.

ROT pressure

An affected-role ROT (Rotation) examines the same procedures from the position of staff who bear the safety exposure. The reference, question, and time remain stable. The rotation reveals near-miss exposure that (X) does not preserve because no outage occurred. The comparison does not reverse.

A temporal ROT (Rotation) then asks whether the difference survives across several review cycles. The relevant result remains: (Y)'s additional information continues to support correction rather than accumulating as unused record volume.

Serious rival

A rival procedure (R) is then allowed to remain in its own operational vocabulary. It uses a compact hazard-control record based on exposure counts, event severity, and corrective-action closure rather than preserving every near-miss narrative. (R) is simpler, cheaper to administer, and would defeat (Y) for the declared question if its aggregation preserved the same role-specific exposure and correction pathways with less reporting friction.

The rival therefore has a credible route to win.

In the bounded case, however, the aggregation in (R) removes which role repeatedly bears the near-miss exposure, and that loss prevents the review from distinguishing a recurring transferred-risk pattern. The loss is claim-critical for this question. (R)'s administrative advantage remains real, but it does not compensate for the missing exposure relation.

Output

\[ \boxed{ RobustOrientation(Y\succ X\mid C), } \]

with (R) recorded as a serious but locally losing rival rather than assimilated into VECTOR vocabulary.

What does not follow

The case does not show that (Y) is the universally superior reporting system, that richer records are always better, or that the rival model is generally inferior. A different question about administrative load or a different evidence pattern could reverse the result.

\[ \boxed{ survival\ under\ relevant\ ROT\ and\ rival\ pressure \neq final\ truth. } \]

E.2 Rotation Collapses DIR

Type: Full Minimal Case

Scene

A project lead compares two staffing arrangements.

Arrangement (X) distributes urgent coordination tasks among several team members.

Arrangement (Y) assigns one experienced coordinator to absorb nearly all urgent interruptions.

From the lead's perspective, (Y) appears clearly superior. Delivery becomes smoother, meeting load falls, and failures are resolved quickly.

The initial claim is:

\[ X\prec_{V,C}Y. \]

Rotation

Affected-role ROT moves to the coordinator's position while preserving the project, time interval, and declared question: which arrangement supports sustainable coordination without hiding critical load?

The rotation reveals that the apparent efficiency of (Y) depends on continuous unrecorded interruption, after-hours work, and loss of the coordinator's own project capacity.

The cost was not absent. It had been externalized into one role.

The original DIR depended on treating system-level smoothness as though it represented scene-wide adequacy.

Result

The claim-critical regression is not compensable by the observed delivery gain because the declared question explicitly includes load visibility and sustainable coordination.

Therefore:

\[ \boxed{ DIR\rightarrow Collapse. } \]

A revised claim may later show that a modified coordinator role with explicit capacity protection outperforms both (X) and the original (Y). That would be a new claim.

It would not mean the original (Y) survived.

Discrimination

The case establishes two things.

First:

\[ \boxed{ ROT\text{ must be able to hurt.} } \]

Second:

\[ \boxed{ Collapse \neq VECTOR\ failure. } \]

The collapse is the discriminative result.

E.3 Incomparability

Type: Micro-Countercase

Two emergency procedures answer different non-substitutable questions.

Procedure (X) minimizes immediate service interruption.

Procedure (Y) maximizes preservation of audit evidence needed to determine a later cause.

The current situation supplies no warranted priority relation between those two aims, and neither procedure dominates the other on both.

The tempting output is:

[
X=Y.
]

VECTOR instead returns:

\[ \boxed{ X\parallel_{V,C}Y. } \]

The result does not say the procedures are equally good. It says the declared comparison does not support a dominance relation.

\[ \boxed{ incomparability \neq equality. } \]

E.4 Trade-Off

Type: Full Minimal Case

Scene

A public organization is deciding how to publish a preliminary technical finding.

Version (X) publishes immediately with broad public reach but limited contextual explanation.

Version (Y) delays publication briefly to add uncertainty ranges, affected-role context, and a correction channel.

Under:

\[ Q_1=\text{speed of public warning}, \]

the relation is:

\[ Y\prec_{Q_1}X. \]

Under:

\[ Q_2=\text{reversible and context-preserving communication}, \]

the relation is:

\[ X\prec_{Q_2}Y. \]

Both questions are relevant. The warning loses value if delayed too long, but the public communication can also create binding, reputational fixation, and exposure before the claim is sufficiently contextualized.

There is no justified conversion of the two relations into one weighted total.

Output

\[ \boxed{ TradeOff_V(X,Y\mid C). } \]

Why not incomparability?

The directions are known.

VECTOR can say exactly where (X) carries one relation better and (Y) carries another better.

What is unavailable is a warranted non-compensatory rule that collapses them into one dominance claim.

Trade-Off therefore contains more structure than:

\[ X\parallel Y. \]

Discrimination

\[ \boxed{ TradeOff \neq Incomparable. } \]

The case also shows why a universal scale would reduce rather than increase the precision of the result.

E.5 Premature Enactment

Type: Micro-Countercase

An organization has a well-supported preliminary DIR (Direction) favoring a new procedure. A low-cost affected-role ROT (Rotation) and one serious rival comparison remain available, but the organization publicly binds itself to the change immediately.

The later ROT does not necessarily show that DIR was false.

The failure lies in prematurely closing review and correction space through public binding before a cheap, materially relevant test was performed. Derived DIST would summarize the narrower relation; it is not the causal mechanism.

The output is not:

bad direction.

It is:

premature enactment relative to available correction space.

For this fixture, the warranted case-level relation is:

\[ \boxed{ DIR \not\Rightarrow E_{immediate}. } \]

The fixture does not itself establish Robust Orientation. The materially relevant affected-role ROT and serious-rival comparison remain open at the pre-binding point, so the stronger robustness label has not yet been earned. That open burden does not prevent the timing diagnosis: public binding occurred before cheap material correction pressure was used.

Chapter 12 preserves the stronger general boundary:

\[ \boxed{ DIR_{robust} \not\Rightarrow E_{immediate}. } \]

E05 therefore separates a case-level diagnosis of premature enactment from the stronger general claim about enactment even after Robust Orientation has been earned.

E.6 Missed Window under Excessive Delay / Preserved Analytical Distance

Type: Micro-Countercase

A team keeps a reversible pilot under review because it wants one more round of analysis.

The pilot can be extended only before a contractual deadline. The additional review adds little new pressure and the deadline passes.

A formerly available branch changes branch status:

\[ Deferred \rightarrow Lost. \]

Deferred and Lost track the trajectory of the extension branch. They do not replace the separate option-status predicates used to type conceivability, visibility, structural availability, effective actionability, or viability.

The failure is not lack of reflection. It is treating continued delay and preserved analytical distance as though they preserved the practical option.

\[ \boxed{ delay \neq preservation. } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ \text{wider derived DIST} \not\Rightarrow adequacy\uparrow. } \]

The post-deadline record therefore marks the extension branch as Lost without inventing a new positive option-status predicate for the closed branch.

E.7 Option Invisibility

Type: Micro-Countercase

Two departments' current orientation represents:

\[ A=\text{keep current process}, \]

and:

\[ B=\text{replace it entirely}. \]

The fixture does not treat this A/B representation as an exhaustive claim that no further effective option exists. The tempting misclassification is instead that a path made visible by ROT must have been created by ROT.

An inside/outside rotation reveals that a shared support unit already possesses authority to separate one blocking dependency. This makes a staged transition (C) genuinely available and actionable in the same organizational scene.

The rotation did not create (C).

It made an existing actionable path visible.

\[ \boxed{ Available(C) \land \neg Visible(C) } \]

before ROT.

The correct classification is Option Invisibility, not Option Creation.

Crucially:

\[ \boxed{ \neg Visible(C) \not\Rightarrow \neg Available(C). } \]

No Option Blindness is diagnosed merely from (C)'s prior nonvisibility. Blindness would require an unsupported exclusion of the path from the relevant current or transformably reachable option field.

E.8 Option Illusion

Type: Full Minimal Case

Scene

A manager argues that an overloaded employee can simply delegate a recurring task to another unit.

The alternative is highly visible and superficially plausible.

The effective option test asks whether the employee actually possesses:

  • authority to transfer the task;
  • access to the receiving unit;
  • budget for the transfer;
  • an open temporal window;
  • cooperation from the unit on which the transfer depends.

None of these conditions holds.

The manager's claim therefore does more than describe a difficult visible path. It unsupportedly includes that path in the employee's relevant effective option field.

Output

[
Visible(o)
]

but:

\[ \neg Actionable(o). \]

The positive option-field error is:

[
\boxed{
OptionIllusion

UnsupportedInclusion.
}
]

Rotation

A role rotation to a senior director shows that the transfer is actionable for that director.

This does not retroactively make it actionable for the employee.

\[ \boxed{ Actionable_{director}(o) \not\Rightarrow Actionable_{employee}(o). } \]

Discrimination

The case blocks two common inflations:

\[ conceivable \rightarrow available \]

and:

\[ available\ to\ A \rightarrow available\ to\ B. \]

VECTOR can still ask whether second-order agency could change the conditions. But until the relevant authority, resource, cooperation, or timing condition is actually changed, the delegation does not belong to the employee's effective option field.

Option-Field Misrepresentation does not by itself establish responsibility, blame, obligation, or authority.

E.9 Special-Case Probe Produces Orientation

Type: Micro-Countercase

A conflict currently has no viable shared procedure.

The analyst constructs:

\[ F^*: \text{assume both parties have equal decision authority}. \]

Under \(F^*\), a shared procedure becomes viable.

The conclusion is not:

the parties currently possess that option.

Instead, the probe identifies the operative asymmetry that blocks it.

\[ \boxed{ o(F^*) \not\Rightarrow o(F_0). } \]

But:

\[ \boxed{ invalid\ as\ option \not\Rightarrow invalid\ as\ probe. } \]

The counterfactual increases orientation without increasing the current effective option field.

The probe does not become an Option Illusion merely by being constructed. Illusion would arise only if the \(F^*\) route were imported into \(F_0\) as an effective option without sufficient support.

E.10 Real Option-Space Transformation

Type: Full Minimal Case

Scene

A technical migration initially contains two options:

\[ A=\text{keep legacy system}, \]
\[ B=\text{move to new system}. \]

The relation is:

\[ A\ excludes\ B \]

because the systems cannot run simultaneously under the current data interface.

A team with real technical authority builds a temporary compatibility layer.

No new long-term destination is created. The significant change is relational.

The field changes from:

\[ A\ excludes\ B \]

to:

\[ A\ precedes\ B. \]

The organization can now run (A) during staged preparation and later migrate to (B).

Output

\[ \boxed{ R_{O,t} \rightarrow R_{O,t+1}. } \]

This is genuine second-order agency.

The team did not merely choose among a fixed set. It altered the relation among existing options.

Boundary

The same scenario described only in a hypothetical design document would not yet count as real option-space transformation. The positive transformation claim is earned by the supplied mechanism, effective authority/capacity, and implementation; it is not promoted from conceivability alone.

\[ \boxed{ analytic\ transformation \neq praxis\ transformation. } \]

E.11 Second-Order Agency Claim Collapses

Type: Full Minimal Case — Adversarial D

Baseline claim

A continuity-critical service has two unattractive current staffing responses during a short absence window. A project lead proposes a third path: temporarily move qualified staff from a neighboring unit and preserve both functions.

The proposal is not absurd. The neighboring unit has people with the relevant skills, and a transfer mechanism exists in the organization. The initial analysis therefore treats the field-transformation claim as a serious candidate rather than dismissing it as imagination.

The stronger baseline claim goes further: it promotes the proposed reassignment into the current effective option field and treats it as a third current staffing option.

Transformation claim

The proposed mechanism is:

\[ R_{O,t} \rightarrow R_{O,t+1} \]

through temporary cross-unit reassignment.

For the claim to count as effective Second-Order Agency, the audit must establish an actor or actor configuration with the authority and practical capacity to initiate the reassignment inside the relevant window, together with the dependencies required for it to become real.

Mechanism and authority audit

The audit finds:

  • the project lead can request but cannot authorize reassignment;
  • the role that can authorize it is unavailable until after the window closes;
  • the neighboring unit cannot release staff without that authorization;
  • no standing emergency mechanism bypasses the dependency;
  • the analysis has no evidence of another actor configuration capable of producing the transfer in time.

The proposed third path therefore remains useful as a probe: it identifies an institutional dependency that could be redesigned for future cases. It is not a current effective transformation.

The stronger baseline claim nevertheless includes it in the current effective option field despite the missing constitutive conditions. That promotion is:

\[ \boxed{ OptionIllusion=UnsupportedInclusion. } \]

The probe is not the illusion. The unsupported current-effective-option assignment is.

Output

\[ \boxed{ SecondOrderAgencyClaim \rightarrow Collapse. } \]

The current option field is not enlarged by the analyst's ability to describe the reassignment.

\[ \boxed{ conceivable\ field\ transformation \neq \text{effective second-order agency}. } \]

After the declared mechanism, authority/capacity, dependency, and timing audit, the bounded negative is warranted:

No currently evidenced effective transformation is available within the declared window, capacity field, and relevant dependency structure.

This is not Option Blindness. The exclusion follows from the positively specified failure of the practical conditions within the declared window, not from mere nonvisibility, Unknown, or unsupported exclusion.

What does not follow

The collapse does not establish that cross-unit reassignment is impossible in every future case. A changed authorization structure, a reopened window, or evidence of a distributed actor configuration could produce a new transformation claim. The negative result is bounded to the current mechanism, capacity field, dependencies, and time.

A future or changed-condition route may remain a valid probe without being imported into the present effective option field.

E.12 Distributed Agency

Type: Micro-Countercase

No individual department can authorize, fund, and implement a needed change.

An individual-only actor model therefore reasons:

\[ Capacity(a_i)<Requirement \quad\forall i \]

and then concludes:

[
NoEffectiveTransformation.
]

But three departments acting together can produce the change:

\[ Capacity(a_1+a_2+a_3)\ge Requirement. \]

The individual-only reconstruction has therefore excluded a transformation that the fixture establishes as available at the coordinated actor configuration. The error is an unsupported exclusion through distributed-agency erasure:

\[ \boxed{ DistributedAgencyErasure \rightarrow OptionBlindness. } \]

This Blindness result does not require prior Option Invisibility. The error lies in treating individual incapacity as evidence that the effective / transformably reachable field contains no such transformation.

The corrected analysis preserves:

\[ \boxed{ distributed\ capacity \neq equal\ individual\ capacity } \]

and separately:

\[ \boxed{ collective\ capacity \neq equal\ individual\ responsibility. } \]

A unit without the relevant authority does not acquire the same attribution merely because the collective could have acted. Option Blindness in the individual-only reconstruction likewise does not by itself establish actor fault, responsibility, or obligation.

E.13 Supported Indirect Cost versus Invented Cost

Type: Full Minimal Case

Scene A — Traceably inferred downstream cost

A maintenance deferral leaves a documented failure mode active. The mechanism connecting the deferred repair to a later outage risk is known from the supplied technical record, the bearer is the service that depends on the component, and the relevant temporal relation is explicit.

The cost claim is therefore recorded as Traceably inferred rather than directly observed:

\[ K(Service,t_{future},downstream,mechanism,TraceablyInferred). \]

VECTOR may carry the relation while preserving uncertainty about magnitude and timing.

Scene B — Invented hidden cost

A different proposal is challenged only with:

there may be some downstream cost we have not thought of.

No bearer, mechanism, temporal relation, or external support is supplied.

The correct status is:

\[ \boxed{Unknown/Residual,} \]

not a positive cost assertion.

Discrimination

\[ \boxed{ unseen\neq zero } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ unseen\neq invented. } \]

Unknown residue can motivate further inquiry. It cannot function as a universal defeat condition for an otherwise supported DIR or option claim. Unknown/Residual here is a consequence-evidence status: it neither establishes a positive consequence nor licenses an option-status inference.

E.14 OUGHT_account — Orientation-Mediated Attribution Increase

Type: Full Minimal Case

Baseline

A role already possesses effective capacity over a process and a known consequence relation. Any structural attributability carried by that configuration under PMS-LOGIC is the baseline; VECTOR does not require awareness in order to recreate or deny that source relation.

New orientation

A later review produces a bounded claim (R) that identifies an additional, previously unintegrated consequence of the process. The claim is externally supported where required, becomes actor-accessible, is understood well enough to be contested or acted on, and remains praxis-available inside the relevant \(t_s\) (scene-time) window.

The orientation-mediated dependency is therefore:

[
RelevantOrientation
+
ExternalPremiseSupport
+
ActorUptake
+
EffectiveCapacity
+
ConsequenceRelation
+
Role/TimeConditions.
]

Output

Where those conditions carry:

\[ \boxed{ \Delta Attributability_{orientation}>0. } \]

VECTOR may type the must-like continuation relation as:

\[ OUGHT_{account}(R), \]

meaning that later praxis cannot simply erase (R) as though the newly available relation had never entered the actor's praxis field.

Boundary

\[ \boxed{ BeingTold(R) \not\Rightarrow ActorUptake(R). } \]

And:

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{account}(R) \not\Rightarrow OUGHT_{choice}(X). } \]

The actor can dispute the warrant, expose a stronger rival, show that capacity has changed, or choose another response while still carrying the relation explicitly.

E.15 OUGHT_constraint

Type: Full Minimal Case

Scene

A service provider maintains an explicit continuity condition:

\[ G=\text{critical service remains available during a regional outage}. \]

Before the outage, several response paths can satisfy (G).

After simultaneous equipment failure and loss of external supply, the effective response class narrows to:

\[ R_G={X,Y}. \]

(X) uses an emergency backup system.

(Y) temporarily reduces service to a protected minimum.

Routine operation (Z) no longer satisfies (G).

Output

VECTOR may reconstruct:

\[ \boxed{ OUGHT_{constraint}(G,{X,Y}). } \]

It does not yet select between (X) and (Y). The response class remains evidentially bounded: a newly evidenced additional effective response satisfying (G) would expand \(R_G\), while a merely visible but practically unsupported route may not be inserted into it.

If a later failure removes (Y), then:

\[ R_G={X}. \]

At that point:

[
X
]

is practically necessary for maintaining (G), conditional on the continued operation of (G) and on the evidence-typed response field. A newly evidenced additional effective response satisfying (G) would defeat the singleton necessity claim; mere visibility alone does not warrant adding a response to \(R_G\).

Revision variant

The institution can instead abandon or revise (G), for example by suspending the service entirely.

That is a real branch.

But it produces a new praxis situation with a different cost topology: service loss, downstream exposure, legal or institutional consequences, and possible reversal costs.

Therefore:

\[ \boxed{ Revision(G) \neq costless\ escape. } \]

Revision(G) is not another response satisfying the unchanged (G). It changes the constraint architecture and therefore yields a new constraint claim.

Boundary

\[ \boxed{ required\ for\ G \neq morally\ required. } \]

VECTOR reconstructs the constraint relation. It does not establish the legitimacy of (G) or the moral status of the remaining burden. The necessity claim is bounded by corrigible bidirectional option-status discrimination: unsupported exclusion can make the response field too narrow, while unsupported inclusion can make it too broad.

E.16 Tragic Constraint

Type: Full Minimal Case

Scene

A care institution faces a sudden shortage that cannot be corrected within the relevant time window.

Its maintained continuity condition requires that a limited critical resource be assigned.

The effective response field contains three options.

(A) preserves one high-dependency service but suspends another.

(B) distributes the resource more evenly but leaves both services below their ordinary safe margin.

(C) closes one service entirely and concentrates remaining capacity on the other.

Every effective option carries serious, non-substitutable residual loss.

A hypothetical option (D) would avoid these losses if additional staff and equipment were available, but they cannot be obtained within the relevant window.

Structure

\[ \forall o\in R_G: ResidualLoss(o) \]

and no currently evidenced effective transformation is available within the declared window, capacity field, and dependency structure.

Output

\[ \boxed{ TragicConstraint(G,R_G). } \]

The output does not tell the institution which loss is morally justified.

It does something prior: it refuses both to prematurely contract the option field through unsupported exclusion and to inflate it by pretending that a non-lossful current response exists.

Discrimination

The case distinguishes tragedy from both premature closure and compulsory possibility.

If a real fourth path had been invisible, VECTOR should expose it. But prior nonvisibility alone is not Option Blindness. Blindness would arise where nonvisibility, Unknown status, blockage, or current non-actionability is used to exclude a real effective or realistically transformable route without sufficient support.

If a real transformation could be produced in time, VECTOR should include it. Hypothetical (D), however, remains a legitimate probe rather than a current effective option because its required means are positively unavailable within the declared window. Excluding (D) on that bounded evidence is not Option Blindness; promoting it despite those conditions would be Option Illusion.

Thus:

\[ \boxed{ TragicConstraint \neq premature\ option\text{-}field\ closure } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ TragicConstraint \neq failure\ to\ invent\ enough\ options. } \]

Successful Blindness pressure does not imply that another effective option must exist. Where no such route is currently evidenced after the declared pressure:

\[ \boxed{ \text{no currently evidenced good option} \neq \text{failure of imagination}. } \]

E.17 Fixpoint without Authority
