Type: Micro-Countercase

A coordination process cannot reach joint integration, but an operational output must still be produced before a deadline.

Some fixpoint function is required:

[
FixpointRequirement.
]

Two procedures can carry it:

  • a rotating designated coordinator;
  • a pre-agreed escalation protocol.

The existence of the functional requirement does not establish:

person (A) should rule.

\[ \boxed{ FixpointRequirement \neq BearerAuthority. } \]

The fixpoint is a function before it is a bearer.

E.18 Revision Is Not Survival

Type: Full Minimal Case

Baseline

A VECTOR analysis begins with:

\[ S_0: \text{Procedure }Y\text{ dominates }X\text{ across the relevant operating context}. \]

A rival model exposes a counter-scene in which (Y)'s advantage depends on high resource availability. Under low-resource conditions, (Y) creates a critical regression.

The broad claim collapses.

Revision

The analyst formulates:

\[ S_1: \text{Under high-resource conditions }F_H,\ Y\text{ dominates }X \text{ for }Q. \]

\(S_1\) then survives relevant role ROT, temporal pressure, and the serious rival.

The correct result is:

\[ \boxed{ RevisedRobustOrientation(S_1). } \]

The incorrect result is:

\(S_0\) survived testing.

It did not.

Why the distinction matters

If every successful repair were counted as survival, a sophisticated framework could never lose. It could continuously narrow its claims while reporting uninterrupted confirmation.

VECTOR therefore preserves lineage:

\[ S_0 \rightarrow S_1. \]
\[ \boxed{ Revision \neq Survival. } \]

The case is positive precisely because the theory improved through losing the original claim.

E.19 Echo Convergence

Type: Micro-Countercase

Five local analyses all support a similar directional conclusion.

At first sight:

\[ d_1,d_2,d_3,d_4,d_5 \rightarrow DIR_R. \]

Dependency audit shows:

\[ d_1,\ldots,d_5 \leftarrow A, \]

where (A) is the same untested assumption imported into every analysis.

The five outputs therefore do not provide five independent warrants for (A).

The convergence claim must be reduced until (A) is separately pressured.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Five echoes} \neq \text{five warrants}. } \]

E.20 Non-Capture

Type: Full Minimal Case

Scene

An evaluator is asked:

Which of two employees has the higher VECTOR?

The available material consists of several observed workplace enactments under different roles, constraints, histories, and authority conditions.

A superficial analysis could convert these observations into a person-level score and rank the employees.

That would violate the type of the object.

VECTOR can compare bounded enactments, trajectories, claims, option fields, or relational configurations under declared questions. It does not provide a scale of intrinsic person quality or dignity.

The requested comparison therefore cannot be repaired merely by adding more data.

Output

\[ \boxed{ \varnothing_{V,C}. } \]

Non-Capture is the correct result.

Contrast

If the question is retyped as:

Which of these two incident-response procedures preserved affected-role visibility under the declared event?

a bounded comparison may become legitimate.

That is a new claim, not completion of the original person-ranking request.

Discrimination

Non-Capture differs from Suspend.

Suspend would say:

more warrant may later make this question answerable.

Non-Capture says:

the requested VECTOR operation should not be produced in this form.

\[ \boxed{ Suspend \neq NonCapture. } \]

This is a positive output because it discriminates between insufficient evidence and an invalid claim type.

E.21 Serious Rival Wins

Type: Full Minimal Case — Adversarial C

Baseline claim

A service team must choose among staffing schedules for a measured arrival pattern. A VECTOR analysis can reconstruct role exposure, option availability, trade-offs, and the consequences of different staffing arrangements.

The declared question, however, is deliberately narrower:

Which schedule minimizes expected queue delay under the measured arrival and service distributions while meeting the fixed staffing constraint?

Rival

A calibrated domain-specific queueing model is supplied with the measured arrival rates, service-time distributions, and staffing limits. It produces out-of-sample predictions for queue delay that discriminate the candidate schedules directly. The model does not use DIR (Direction), ROT (Rotation), option topology, or PMS operators; it remains in its own technical vocabulary.

VECTOR can still identify that the staffing constraint is operative and can expose role-specific costs not contained in the technical objective. But for the declared queue-delay question, those broader distinctions do not improve the prediction and add unnecessary machinery.

The rival therefore performs the relevant discriminative work more directly and with stronger domain calibration.

Output

\[ \boxed{ RivalSuperiority \rightarrow VECTOR\ ScopeReduction. } \]

VECTOR yields the bounded technical question to the rival model. This is a genuine domain-deference result, but it does not yet pressure DIR (Direction)–ROT (Rotation) on VECTOR's own bounded orientation burden.

What does not follow

The result does not show that the rival answers questions about legitimacy, burden distribution, option-field legibility or misrepresentation, or practical attribution. Nor does VECTOR assimilate the rival as “really a VECTOR model.” The models answer different burdens; for this burden, the rival wins.

\[ \boxed{ \text{serious rival victory is a legitimate VECTOR result}. } \]

E.22 DIST Necessity Test — Architecture Reduction

Type: Full Minimal Case — Adversarial E

Two scenes share the same bounded DIR, the same nominal twenty-four-hour interval before activation, and substantial (Χ) (distance): the actors can still reflect on and understand a serious affected-role objection.

In Scene A, no material (Ψ) (self-binding) or public reliance has formed, the (Θ) (temporality) review window is practically open, and a valid stop remains available. The affected-role ROT (Rotation) can still change whether enactment occurs.

In Scene B, the same reflective (Χ) (distance) and clock interval remain, but prior (Ψ) (self-binding), public commitments, third-party reliance, and lower available reversibility make the same ROT (Rotation) primarily relevant to reversal or mitigation rather than low-cost pre-enactment correction.

DIST can summarize:

\[ DIST_A>DIST_B. \]

But removing DIST does not remove the discrimination. The source relations already support the different practical result:

\[ ROT_{pre\text{-}enactment\ actionable}(A), \qquad ROT_{reversal/correction\ relevant}(B). \]

Output

\[ \boxed{ DIST \rightarrow \text{derived supporting construct}. } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR_{core}:=\langle DIR,ROT\rangle. } \]

Discrimination

This case is adversarial because the expected success condition is reduction. The underlying orientation–enactment distinction survives; VECTOR's ownership claim over it becomes smaller.

E.23 Same-Burden Core Rival — Calibrated MCDA Reduces DIR–ROT Scope

Type: Full Minimal Case — Adversarial F

Bounded question

A public service must choose between two intake procedures for a six-month pilot. Both procedures are technically feasible. The relevant evidence is already supplied: measured processing efficiency, documented error reduction, accessibility testing, reversal performance, and affected-role feedback.

The declared frame includes the operating team and the affected service users. The question is explicitly orientational rather than merely predictive:

Which procedure is better supported for the pilot under the declared operational and affected-role criteria, while preserving the externally elicited priority range attached to those criteria?

For this case only, the four criteria are declared commensurable and normalized to a common 0–100 performance scale, where higher values are better:

Procedure Processing efficiency Error reduction Accessibility Reversibility
(A) 95 65 55 50
(B) 80 85 90 90

No criterion value is generated by VECTOR. The table is part of the supplied external decision record.

The relative criterion priorities are likewise external. The organization has elicited an admissible one-parameter priority range:

[
w(\lambda)

\bigl(
0.55-0.25\lambda,
0.15+0.05\lambda,
0.15+0.10\lambda,
0.15+0.10\lambda
\bigr),
\qquad
\lambda\in[0,1].
]

The range expresses a bounded shift from stronger operations emphasis at (\lambda=0) toward stronger error, accessibility, and reversibility emphasis at (\lambda=1). VECTOR does not validate the elicitation merely because the range has been supplied; the case assumes its external warrant so that the rival can be tested on the same bounded orientation burden.

VECTOR analysis

The initial operations perspective favors Procedure (A) because it yields the largest processing-efficiency gain. Affected-role and reversibility ROTs (Rotations) expose substantial counterpressure: Procedure (B) performs better on accessibility, error reduction, and reversibility.

VECTOR therefore detects a real vulnerability in the initial DIR (Direction). But DIR–ROT alone does not generate the external calibration needed to determine how much the four declared criteria count relative to one another. Without importing the supplied calibration, the strongest responsible VECTOR result is Trade-Off or suspension of a broader carried DIR.

This is not a defect in the Calibration Boundary. It is exactly what that boundary predicts. The harder question is whether VECTOR still adds primary standalone discrimination once the calibration has already been supplied.

Rival

The rival is a calibrated weighted-sum multi-criteria decision analysis (MCDA) with one-parameter sensitivity analysis. It remains in its own vocabulary. It uses alternatives, normalized criterion performance, weights, aggregate scores, and sensitivity. It does not translate stakeholder positions into ROT (Rotation), does not use PMS operators, and does not treat DIR (Direction) as a required intermediate relation.

The rival evaluates:

[
S\(o\mid \lambda\)

\sum_{i=1}^{4} w_i(\lambda)c_i(o).
]

At the operations-heavy edge of the externally admitted range:

\[ S(A\mid 0)=77.75, \qquad S(B\mid 0)=83.75. \]

At the affected-role/reversibility-heavy edge:

\[ S(A\mid 1)=67.75, \qquad S(B\mid 1)=86.00. \]

Because the score difference is linear in (\lambda), and Procedure (B) is ahead at both endpoints, (B) remains preferred throughout the full admitted interval:

\[ \boxed{ S(B\mid\lambda)>S(A\mid\lambda) \quad \forall\lambda\in[0,1]. } \]

The same sensitivity calculation also makes the reversal condition explicit. Extending the declared trade-off path outside the admitted calibration range, the scores switch at approximately:

\[ \lambda^*\approx -0.49, \]

which would correspond to assigning processing efficiency substantially more priority than the externally admitted range permits. The rival therefore shows both why (B) carries under the supplied calibration and what kind of priority shift would reverse the result.

Shared comparison criterion

The relevant comparison is not agreement with PMS. It is:

Which analysis discriminates the same bounded orientation question with the supplied calibration, preserves the declared priority variation, and makes the condition of reversal visible with less unsupported machinery?

Under that criterion, the MCDA rival wins this case.

Actual loss

The initial operations-centered direction toward (A) does not survive the externally calibrated same-burden comparison:

\[ \boxed{ B\prec_{V,C_{\text{ops}}}A \rightarrow Collapse. } \]

The carried result for the declared calibrated question is instead:

\[ \boxed{ A\prec_{V,Q}B. } \]

VECTOR may record this externally calibrated relation as a bounded DIR (Direction), but only by declaring the rival calibration as part of its warrant source. Reusing the DIR notation does not transfer the rival's calibration back into VECTOR as endogenous warrant.

More importantly, the rival shows that in this calibrated, commensurable multi-criteria class, general ROT (Rotation) does not earn primary standalone status merely because stakeholder priorities differ. Where those differences are already represented by externally supported parameter ranges and sensitivity analysis, ROT contributes additional discrimination only if it exposes a relation not represented in the rival model—for example an omitted role, a false commensurability assumption, option-field misrepresentation through unsupported exclusion or unsupported inclusion, or a claim-critical consequence outside the supplied criterion set.

The loss is therefore not merely case-level:

\[ \boxed{ RivalSuperiority_Q \rightarrow DIR\ ScopeNarrowing + ROT\ ScopeNarrowing. } \]

And at paper level:

\[ \boxed{ StandaloneVECTOR \rightarrow \text{scope reduction for calibrated commensurable multi-criteria orientation}. } \]

VECTOR can still audit whether the criteria omit a claim-critical role, whether the weight elicitation lacks external warrant, whether a non-compensatory relation was wrongly made commensurable, whether the represented option field exhibits unsupported exclusion or unsupported inclusion, or whether a later practical-attribution question exceeds the decision model. Those are different burdens. They do not restore primary DIR–ROT discrimination for the bounded question the rival has already carried better.

What would have defeated the rival

The rival would not earn this result if its criteria omitted a claim-critical affected role, if its normalized scores lacked external support, if the admissible weight path were invented by the analyst, if the sensitivity interval were chosen only to protect one outcome, or if a material relation could not responsibly be represented on the commensurable scale.

In those cases, ROT (Rotation) or another external critique could expose a loss that the MCDA model had hidden. Rival superiority therefore depends on the supplied calibration and the declared commensurability; it is not inherited from the rival's mathematical form.

What does not follow

The case does not show that MCDA replaces VECTOR across non-commensurable, poorly calibrated, category-sensitive, or non-capture cases. It does not show that every perspective can be represented as a weight. It does not convert a decision score into moral authority, and it does not establish the legitimacy of the externally supplied priority range.

It shows something narrower and more damaging to the standalone claim:

Where a bounded orientation problem is genuinely commensurable and externally calibrated, a non-PMS sensitivity architecture can perform the same directional and perspectival discrimination more directly, forcing VECTOR to yield primary status in that class.

That is a same-burden rival loss, not specialist-domain deference.

Appendix F — Source Lineage and Claim Map

Appendix F is the epistemic bookkeeping layer of PMS–VECTOR.

It is not a bibliography and not a second version of Chapter 17.

Chapter 17 gives the argumentative lineage: how prior PMS structures create the pressure field from which VECTOR emerges.

Appendix F asks a narrower audit question:

Which claims are corpus-carried, which are VECTOR reconstructions, which are new claims of VECTOR, and which are merely proposed formalizations?

The purpose is to prevent two opposite errors:

\[ \text{VECTOR novelty presented as canonical PMS} \]

and:

\[ \text{canonical PMS content presented as VECTOR novelty}. \]

The governing rule is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Lineage supports provenance, not authority inheritance.} } \]

A source may explain where a claim comes from without increasing its epistemic strength merely by being earlier or canonical.

F.1 Claim Provenance Categories

Four provenance labels are used.

Status Meaning
C — Corpus-carried Explicitly carried by the existing PMS corpus in materially relevant form
R — VECTOR reconstruction Reconstructed by VECTOR from one or more corpus-carried structures without claiming that the combined relation was already canonical
N — VECTOR-new A distinct positive claim introduced by PMS–VECTOR
P — Proposed / provisional formalization A notation, record field, schema, or formal device used for audit or reuse rather than claimed as an independently established theoretical result

The labels describe origin and claim status, not epistemic rank.

\[ \boxed{ C \not> R \not> N \not> P } \]

as a universal quality ordering.

A corpus-carried claim can be weak, local, or later reduced.

A VECTOR-new claim can be strongly argued.

A proposed notation can be useful without being theoretically deep.

The categories should be applied at sufficient granularity. Where one sentence contains both reconstruction and novelty, the claims should be split rather than labeled R/N.

\[ \boxed{ \text{Provenance describes origin, not epistemic strength.} } \]

A complete provenance table can still describe an inadequate theory perfectly. Therefore:

\[ \boxed{ ProvenanceCompleteness \neq TheoreticalAdequacy. } \]

Provenance is an audit of origin and dependency, not a substitute for discrimination, calibration, rivalry, or external warrant.

F.2 PMS Base

Only Base structures materially used by VECTOR are listed here.

Source-carried claim or structure Status VECTOR use
Δ–Ψ as the proposed PMS operator grammar C Source grammar; not modified by VECTOR
PMS is proposed as a generative basis rather than a finished self-sealing system C Establishes openness to later discrimination and critique
Completeness, optimal ordering, discriminative adequacy, and comparative superiority remain open questions C Central problem pressure for VECTOR
Translation / derivational productivity does not establish exhaustive capture C Supports non-capture and anti-assimilation boundaries
Genuine non-capture remains methodologically possible C Precedent for VECTOR non-capture
PMS contains operator-level directionality, including directed activation and asymmetry C Shows that VECTOR is not introducing direction into an otherwise directionless grammar
DIR as a meta-reconstructive directional relation N VECTOR claim; explicitly not a new Base operator
DIR ≠ \nabla, ROT ≠ \Phi, DIST ≠ Χ, t ≠ \Theta N VECTOR type boundaries
The claim that distributed local direction may support bounded meta-orientation N VECTOR's own theoretical problem and positive possibility

The key provenance boundary is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{open problem in Base} \neq \text{warrant for VECTOR as its solution}. } \]

Base makes the question legitimate. VECTOR remains responsible for its answer.

F.3 MIP

MIP is an adjacent model rather than a hidden VECTOR precursor. Its most relevant contributions concern enactment, power as practical room for action, trajectory-sensitive evaluation, and dignity boundaries.

MIP contribution Status VECTOR relation
Analyse enactments rather than infer the value of persons C Supports the person-ranking boundary
Power / practical room for action matters to fair appraisal C Local precedent for effective option and capacity differences
Real alternatives, authority, information, and burden distribution affect action appraisal C Supports role-bound option fields and capacity calibration
\(D_0\) dignity is untouchable / non-negotiable rather than a score of maturity C Supports the Chapter 16 dignity firewall and the safe use of its Adult Orientation shorthand
Role- and trajectory-sensitive enactment analysis C Precedent for temporally and relationally bounded comparison
“Adult Orientation” as a VECTOR-new supporting shorthand for corrigible consequence-carrying praxis N Label is new, but Chapter 19 reduces it from independent construct to supporting shorthand; not a MIP maturity score or person type
The Chapter 16 stance / Adult Orientation shorthand does not alter \(D_0\) R VECTOR application of the MIP dignity boundary

The audit rule for the supporting shorthand is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Adult Orientation shorthand} \neq AdultPerson. } \]

VECTOR uses MIP to constrain misuse, not to inherit an anthropology of the whole person or to restore independent theoretical status to the label.

F.4 Domain Papers

The domain papers provide local pressure, examples of direction-bearing distinctions, timing, non-integrability, coordination, capacity, and exit realism.

They do not collectively contain VECTOR in latent form.

F.4.1 ANTICIPATION

ANTICIPATION contribution Status VECTOR relation
Attribution requires effective capacity inside a relevant frame C Capacity gate for later attribution claims
Abstract possibility does not create universalized responsibility C Blocks retrospective and possibilist responsibility inflation
No retroactive guilt from later-recognized possibility where effective capacity was absent C Supports temporal discipline in option and Ought claims
Imagining a scenario does not itself generate responsibility C Supports conceivable ≠ capacity
Θ can accumulate costs and close windows C Timing, action-corridor, and missed-window pressure
Ψ should not be externalized from self-binding into automatic binding of others C Supports self-/other-binding boundary
General VECTOR timing architecture R Reconstructs these pressures through timing, option status, reversibility, and cost topology; derived DIST may summarize orientation–enactment separation
OUGHT_account / OUGHT_constraint N Not ANTICIPATION claims

The key audit boundary is:

\[ \boxed{ later\ possibility\ recognition \neq earlier\ effective\ capacity. } \]

F.4.2 CRITIQUE

CRITIQUE contribution Status VECTOR relation
Critique can be reconstructed as transition from reaction toward correction-capable praxis C Local directional precedent
Θ makes delay, accumulation, and irreversibility relevant C Timing and window pressure
Χ provides distance / interruption capacity C One source pressure for DIST without type identity
Revision windows can close C Reversibility and missed-window cases
Publicness can intensify exposure, fixation, and correction costs C Public/private timing and source relations behind role-relative derived DIST
Endless reframing or postponement can itself block correction C Counterweight to treating wider derived DIST as automatically better
DIST as effective orientation–enactment separation R Cross-domain VECTOR reconstruction
ROT as general perspective-test architecture N Not a CRITIQUE operation

The provenance relation is therefore one of pressure and reconstruction, not direct inheritance of VECTOR components.

F.4.3 CONFLICT

CONFLICT contribution Status VECTOR relation
Mature conflict can remain non-integrable C Precedent for trade-off, incomparability, and tragic structure
Infrastructure-coupled conflict can make carriage non-optional at system level C Source pressure for constraint-relative carriage
Roles can occupy asymmetric risk, temporal, and reversibility positions C Supports role-relative DIST and option fields
A fixpoint requirement can arise where integration fails but continuity/output remains required C Source pressure for functional necessity
Fixpoint can be carried by a person, role, procedure, or institutional mechanism C Blocks automatic bearer personalization
CONFLICT does not establish that a particular person morally ought to carry the load C Normative boundary retained by VECTOR
OUGHT_constraint as a general claim type N VECTOR addition
TragicConstraint(G,R_G) as VECTOR notation/configuration N VECTOR formalization of a broader constraint structure
FixpointRequirement ≠ BearerAuthority as an explicit VECTOR audit rule R Reconstruction preserving CONFLICT's functional/non-prescriptive boundary

Most importantly:

\[ \boxed{ system\text{-}level\ carriage \neq person\text{-}level\ duty. } \]

F.4.4 EDEN

EDEN contribution Status VECTOR relation
Truthful coordination of real (\Omega) differs structurally from false coordination C Local DIR precedent
Responsibility should follow real structuring effect rather than appearance C Consequence and attribution pressure
Learned legibility can stabilize both competence and blind spots C Supports non-privilege of the unrotated view
Counter-scenes can expose whether a structural claim discriminates relevant differences C Pressure for counter-scene testing
Description does not itself authorize binding application C Authority firewall
General DIR architecture N VECTOR generalization
ROT as perspective-testing/discovery architecture N VECTOR generalization rather than inherited EDEN operation

EDEN is therefore especially important to VECTOR's claim that:

\[ \boxed{ perspectival\ access \neq perspectival\ equivalence. } \]

F.4.5 LOGIC

LOGIC requires the strictest provenance handling because Chapter 15 extends a pressure that LOGIC itself deliberately stops before turning into prescription.

LOGIC contribution Status VECTOR relation
Potential means effective, frame-bound capacity rather than abstract possibility C Capacity gate
Responsibility tracks structurally attributable consequences under relevant asymmetry and temporality C Attribution pressure
Non-action can cease to be structurally neutral where effective capacity and attributable consequence are present C Source pressure for structural attributability / non-neutrality; not itself VECTOR Ought
LOGIC operates on responsibility, not prescriptive ought statements C Hard provenance boundary
Effective potential introduces no obligation by itself C Hard normative boundary
Orientation can alter the actor's legibility of relevant capacity and consequence N VECTOR-specific relation
OUGHT_account N VECTOR-specific claim type
OUGHT_constraint N VECTOR-specific claim type
The OUGHT_choice / OUGHT_account / OUGHT_constraint typology N VECTOR-specific architecture

Therefore:

\[ \boxed{ LOGIC\text{ responsibility} \neq VECTOR\text{ Ought typology}. } \]

The latter is downstream and independently burdened.

F.4.6 SEX

VECTOR makes only a limited use of SEX.

SEX contribution Status VECTOR relation
Exit can alter the future trajectory without erasing the prior history of a relation C Local precedent for Exit ≠ Reset
Binding and exposure can leave downstream effects after exit C Reversibility and cost-topology pressure
Exit changes trajectory; it does not reset history as a generalized VECTOR rule R Cross-domain reconstruction
No larger VECTOR architecture is attributed to SEX — Explicit scope boundary

The audit purpose of this section is partly negative: VECTOR should not overstate dependence on a domain paper where the actual lineage is narrow.

F.5 DISCIPLINE

DISCIPLINE is a use-boundary and application discipline. It is not a validator for VECTOR.

DISCIPLINE contribution Status VECTOR relation
PMS readability does not itself authorize application C Precedent for orientation ≠ authority
Claim ceilings and bounded use must remain explicit C VECTOR boundedness discipline
Suspend / refuse / stop are legitimate outcomes C Precedent for VECTOR suspension and non-forcing
Later work does not inherit findings or claim ceilings automatically C Non-inheritance discipline
Falsehood can remain false while organizing real practice C Direct source pressure for Chapter 11
A false claim can orient action, delay, burden, exposure, and follow-up cost C Practical Power of Falsehood
orientation effect ≠ orientation truth as a general VECTOR principle R VECTOR generalization into option/cost topology
VECTOR directional claims N Not validated by DISCIPLINE

The key relation is:

\[ \boxed{ DISCIPLINE: \text{May this use proceed?} } \]

versus:

\[ \boxed{ VECTOR: \text{What directional relation, if any, is warranted?} } \]

The two can constrain one another in application without collapsing into one layer.

F.6 UNDER LOAD

UNDER LOAD supplies pressure rather than warrant.

UNDER LOAD contribution Status VECTOR relation
PMS success can itself become a pressure condition C Source of VECTOR success-trap discipline
Translation is not capture C Anti-assimilation pressure
Coherence is not sufficiency C Anti-self-confirmation pressure
Self-binding is not final self-arbitration C Self-test boundary
Corpus structure is not external warrant C External-warrant boundary
Application / transformation discipline is not verdict authority C Authority firewall
More structure is not more warrant C Anti-formalization inflation
VECTOR must remain reducible by its own distinctions R Reconstruction of UNDER LOAD pressure into VECTOR self-reduction
Failure families and reduction ladder N VECTOR-specific pressure architecture

The relation is:

\[ \boxed{ UNDER\ LOAD \rightarrow VECTOR\ pressure } \]

not:

\[ \boxed{ UNDER\ LOAD \rightarrow VECTOR\ warrant. } \]

VECTOR does not supersede UNDER LOAD. It remains one of the structures that UNDER LOAD can pressure.

F.7 STRATA

STRATA supplies the closest methodological discipline for controlled movement among representations, but its operations and authority remain distinct from VECTOR.

STRATA contribution Status VECTOR relation
Exactly three core operations: COMPOSE, DECOMPOSE, PROJECT_AS C Hard operation boundary
Origin-type / type integrity C Borrowed methodological discipline
Reference continuity C Borrowed methodological discipline
Contextual boundedness and explicit transformation loss C Borrowed methodological discipline
No universal scale theory C Precedent for local comparison without global rank
Stop and non-capture C Precedent for bounded transformation
Paths / transitions can open or close action corridors C Local source pressure for option-field temporality
ROT N Not PROJECT_AS
Option-Space Transformation N Praxis transformation, not a fourth STRATA operation
Non-compensatory VECTOR comparison R Methodological reconstruction of a discipline already carried by STRATA
Rotation Contract N VECTOR-specific operational device

Two boundaries are non-negotiable:

\[ \boxed{ ROT\neq PROJECT_AS } \]

and:

\[ \boxed{ OptionSpaceTransformation \neq \text{fourth STRATA operation}. } \]

The general rule is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{borrowed discipline} \neq \text{inherited warrant}. } \]

The rule governing this section is intentionally simple:

\[ \boxed{ \text{If a claim is new, mark it }N. } \]

Novelty does not weaken VECTOR. Unclear provenance would.

F.9 Claim Dependency Map

Provenance asks:

Where does the claim come from?

Dependency asks:

What else must weaken, change, or disappear if a parent claim fails?

The governing rule is:

\[ \boxed{ \text{Dependency transmits vulnerability, not automatic warrant.} } \]

A child claim may depend on a parent. The existence of several children does not thereby provide several independent warrants for the parent.

Robust Orientation

A result of Robust Orientation depends at minimum on:

\[ DIR + RelevantROT + RivalPressure + ProvenanceAudit + NonCompensation + \Lambda\ preservation. \]

A compact dependency representation is:

TXT
RobustOrientation
├── typed DIR
├── materially relevant ROT
│   ├── declared change
│   ├── declared constants
│   ├── reference continuity
│   └── loss capacity
├── serious rival pressure
├── provenance / echo audit
├── non-compensation
├── no unresolved claim-critical regression
└── preserved residue

If materially relevant ROT is missing, the result need not become false. It may reduce to:

[
BoundedOrientation.
]

If reference continuity fails, the result may become:

[
NonCapture
]

or a new bounded claim.

If the rival defeats the baseline, the claim may require revision or collapse.

Dependency therefore determines how to reduce, not merely whether to reject.

OUGHT_account

The dependency map must distinguish baseline PMS-LOGIC attributability from VECTOR's orientation-mediated uplift.

\[ \boxed{ BaselineAttributability_{LOGIC} \neq \Delta Attributability_{orientation}. } \]

For the latter, OUGHT_account depends on:

[
RelevantOrientation
+
ExternalPremiseSupport
+
ActorUptake
+
EffectiveCapacity
+
ConsequenceRelation
+
Role/TimeConditions.
]

Operationally:

TXT
OUGHT_account(R) / orientation-mediated uplift
├── R is relevantly oriented under bounded DIR/ROT pressure
├── material external premises are supported
├── orientation is actor-accessible and understood enough for contest/response
├── actor/role has relevant effective capacity
├── consequence relation connects capacity to subsequent praxis
└── role and scene-time conditions keep the relation materially live

If uptake is missing, VECTOR cannot claim that its orientation increased attributability merely because the analyst stated the claim. This does not erase a baseline LOGIC relation independently supported by capacity and consequence.

If effective capacity falls away, the orientation-mediated account claim must be reduced or retyped. If only visibility remains, Visible(R) does not preserve the uplift. If an external premise or consequence relation is defeated by a rival explanation, the practical attribution claim must weaken accordingly.

OUGHT_constraint

\[ OUGHT_{constraint}(G,R_G) \]

depends on:

[
OperativeG
+
ExternalPremiseSupport
+
EffectiveOptionField
+
ConstraintRelations.
]

Operationally:

TXT
OUGHT_constraint(G, R_G)
├── G is actually operative
├── material factual premises about G are supported
├── effective option field is correctly typed
├── response class R_G is non-empty
├── each member of R_G satisfies the relevant G-condition
├── maintainer / modifier / claimed authority are visible
├── burden bearers / exit / alternative carriers are visible
├── evidenced transformation routes are considered
└── legitimacy remains external unless separately warranted

If an invisible but real option is discovered, \(R_G\) changes. If (G) is revised, the old constraint claim does not simply persist. If system-level carriage is real but the bearer assignment is unsupported, the system requirement may remain while the person-level claim collapses.

\[ \boxed{ SystemCarriageRequired \neq PersonXMustCarry. } \]

Tragic Constraint

Tragic Constraint depends on a valid OUGHT_constraint configuration plus:

\[ \forall o\in R_G: ResidualLoss(o) \]

and the bounded evidential claim that no currently evidenced effective transformation is available within the declared scene-time window, capacity field, and dependency structure.

TXT
TragicConstraint
├── valid operative G relation
├── effective option field correctly typed
├── residual loss across R_G
├── mechanism audit for second-order transformations
├── capacity / authority / dependency / window audit
└── no currently evidenced non-lossful route in the declared field

If a previously invisible no-loss option is real, tragedy falls. If Second-Order Agency can create or open such an option in time, tragedy falls. If a rival shows that the field was misdescribed, tragedy falls. A merely imagined rescue route does not by itself change current option status.

\[ \boxed{ \text{tragic constraint is falsifiable by evidenced possibility, not by imagination alone}. } \]

Corrigible Consequence-Carrying Praxis / “Adult Orientation” Shorthand

The Chapter 16 stance does not depend on choosing the substantively correct outcome. Its content is already carried by lower-level relations:

TXT
CorrigibleConsequenceCarryingPraxis
├── bounded relevant orientation
├── preserved corrigibility
├── willingness to carry relevant consequence
├── awareness of capacity limits
├── preserved Λ / residue
├── handling of orientation–enactment source conditions without reifying derived DIST
├── recognition of distributed agency where relevant
└── no conversion of orientation into authority over others

A specific choice can later prove wrong while the stance remains corrigible. Conversely, a substantively successful choice does not establish the described stance if the result depended on unrotatable certainty, hidden cost externalization, or authority inflation.

Chapter 19 shows that the dependency map remains intact when the Adult Orientation label is deleted. The label therefore carries no independent dependency node and remains supporting shorthand only.

VECTOR Standalone Claim

The strongest dependency map concerns VECTOR itself.

The standalone theory claim depends on additional discrimination beyond what existing PMS structures already supply.

TXT
StandaloneVECTOR
├── DIR adds discriminative purchase
├── ROT adds more than ordinary reframing
├── prior DIST core claim has been genuinely reduced rather than protected
├── Adult Orientation has been reduced to supporting shorthand after deletion testing
├── option-status architecture discriminates real from illusory possibility
├── second-order agency adds field-transformation discrimination under mechanism evidence
├── cost topology adds non-fungible consequence discrimination without speculative inflation
├── convergence / closure machinery produces real claim loss and bounded stop
├── specialist rivals can win specialist burdens without assimilation
├── same-burden non-PMS rivals can reduce DIR / ROT / standalone scope (E.23)
├── Ought typology prevents rather than causes normative conflation
└── remaining DIR–ROT core can still be reduced if these contributions fail

If every major component becomes losslessly reducible to pre-existing PMS terms:

\[ \boxed{ StandaloneVECTOR \rightarrow Reduction. } \]

If only one component fails, component reduction may be sufficient.

If the theory works only in some domains, domain non-capture or scope reduction may be appropriate.

If a rival architecture provides the same or better discrimination with less complexity, rival superiority may justify stronger reduction. E.23 already enacts that branch for calibrated commensurable multi-criteria orientation, narrowing the domain in which DIR (Direction)–ROT (Rotation) can claim primary standalone discrimination.

The dependency map therefore closes the appendices where Chapter 19 placed the burden:

\[ \boxed{ \text{A theory of orientation must be capable of orienting away from itself.} } \]

Appendix F does not add a final argument for VECTOR. It makes visible what would have to change if VECTOR's arguments fail.

That is the intended function of provenance and dependency: not to make the theory harder to remove, but to make its removal, reduction, or revision traceable.
